INSTRUCTION: ADD
SUMMARY: ADD reads the integer values from source registers rs1 and rs2, adds them as XLEN‑bit (64‑bit in RV64I) integers, and writes the low XLEN bits of the sum to destination register rd. A 32‑bit addition must be performed with the separate ADDW instruction; ADD itself always operates on the full XLEN width. ADD never raises any arithmetic exception—overflow is silently wrapped and mcause remains unchanged. No special privilege or access‑control checks are required to execute ADD.

INSTRUCTION: ADD.UW
SUMMARY: ADD.UW is an RV64‑only integer instruction that zero‑extends the low 32‑bit word of the .uw‑suffixed source register, adds it to the full‑XLEN value of the other source register using modulo‑2^XLEN arithmetic, and writes the XLEN‑wide sum to the destination register. It modifies only the destination register, leaving all other architectural state unchanged. On RV32 the encoding is reserved and the instruction has no defined behavior. The instruction is not privileged and can be executed in any privilege mode.

INSTRUCTION: ADDI
SUMMARY: ADDI reads a source register (rs1) and a 12‑bit signed immediate, sign‑extends the immediate to XLEN bits, adds them, and writes the XLEN‑bit architectural result to the destination register (rd). The operation never raises an arithmetic exception; any overflow is discarded and only the low XLEN bits are stored. If the immediate is zero the instruction acts as a move (copies rs1 to rd), and when rd equals rs1 the register is overwritten with the computed sum (e.g., an immediate of –1 subtracts one). The instruction is allowed in all privilege modes and has no side effects beyond updating rd.

INSTRUCTION: ADDIW
SUMMARY: ADDIW is an RV64I arithmetic instruction that reads a 64‑bit source register rs1, adds a sign‑extended 12‑bit immediate, truncates the 64‑bit sum to the low 32 bits, and writes the sign‑extension of that 32‑bit result to the destination register rd. If the immediate field is zero, the operation reduces to sign‑extending the lower 32 bits of rs1 (the SEXT.W pseudo‑instruction). Execution on a core that does not implement RV64I (e.g., RV32I) triggers an illegal‑instruction exception with mcause = 2. No other privilege checks are imposed.

INSTRUCTION: ADDW
SUMMARY: ADDW is a RV64I‑only integer instruction; it is unavailable on RV32I implementations. It reads the low‑32‑bit portions of the two source registers, adds them as signed 32‑bit values, discards any overflow, and then sign‑extends the 32‑bit sum to 64 bits before writing the result to the destination register. No architectural exceptions are defined for this operation.

INSTRUCTION: AND
SUMMARY: The AND instruction reads the integer registers identified by rs1 and rs2, computes their XLEN‑bit bitwise logical AND, and writes the result to the integer register rd. No architectural registers other than rd are modified, and the operation is allowed in all privilege modes. The instruction never raises an exception or trap for any defined encoding. The fields rs1, rs2, and rd denote the two source registers and the destination register, respectively.

INSTRUCTION: ANDI
SUMMARY: ANDI reads the integer register rs1, sign‑extends the 12‑bit immediate, and computes their bitwise logical AND. The resulting XLEN‑wide value is written to the integer register rd. The instruction is an I‑type integer operation that is permitted in all privilege modes and does not generate any defined exceptions. No other architectural state is accessed or altered.

INSTRUCTION: ANDN
SUMMARY: The ANDN instruction reads integer registers rs1 and rs2 and writes to destination register rd the result of rs1 bitwise‑AND with the bitwise‑NOT of rs2. The computation is performed on full XLEN‑wide values (32 bits on RV32, 64 bits on RV64) and the XLEN‑wide result is stored in rd. rs1 and rs2 are read‑only for this operation; only rd is written and no architectural status flags or other side effects are produced. ANDN is an unprivileged integer‑logical instruction available in all privilege modes.

INSTRUCTION: AUIPC
SUMMARY: AUIPC forms a 32‑bit offset by placing the 20‑bit U‑type immediate in the upper bits and zeroing the lower 12, sign‑extends this offset to XLEN, adds it to the address of the AUIPC instruction, and writes the result into the destination register rd. When the immediate field is zero, the PC of the AUIPC instruction itself is written to rd. The value placed in rd can be used by a following load, store, or JALR whose own 12‑bit immediate is added, providing full‑range PC‑relative addressing. No special privilege or access‑control checks are imposed; the operation is valid in all privilege modes.

INSTRUCTION: BCLR
SUMMARY: BCLR reads the source value from rs1 and a bit index from rs2, interpreting the low log2(XLEN) bits of rs2 as an unsigned position 0 to XLEN‑1. It clears the selected bit in the rs1 value, leaves all other bits unchanged, and writes the result to rd. The instruction has no special privilege or access‑control constraints beyond the normal register‑file permissions. No architectural exceptions are defined for this operation.

INSTRUCTION: BCLRI
SUMMARY: BCLRI is a base instruction valid on both RV32 and RV64 implementations. It reads the source register rs1 and an unsigned shamt immediate, using the least‑significant log2(XLEN) bits of the immediate as the bit index to clear. The destination register rd receives the value of rs1 with that indexed bit forced to zero while all other bits stay unchanged. The instruction imposes no special privilege or exception requirements beyond normal architectural rules.

INSTRUCTION: BEXT
SUMMARY: BEXT reads rs1 and rs2, masks rs2 with XLEN‑1 to form an index, shifts rs1 right by that index, masks the result with 1, and writes the zero‑extended bit (0 or 1) to rd. The instruction does not modify any architectural state other than rd. An RV64 implementation must support BEXT, it is executable in any privilege mode, and no exceptions are defined for its operation.

INSTRUCTION: BEXTI
SUMMARY: BEXTI reads register rs1, extracts the single bit at the index supplied by the immediate (shamt) field after masking the immediate with XLEN‑1, and writes that bit zero‑extended into destination register rd. The effective index is the lower log2(XLEN) bits of the immediate, and the result is the least‑significant bit of rs1 logically right‑shifted by that index. The instruction is permitted in all privilege modes and does not trigger any architectural exceptions. No other registers or side effects are involved.

INSTRUCTION: BINV
SUMMARY: BNIV reads two integer registers, rs1 and rs2, and writes a single integer register, rd. The value written to rd is rs1 with the bit at the index given by the low‑order bits of rs2 inverted (rd = rs1 ⊕ (1 << (rs2 mod XLEN))). The instruction produces no architectural side effects other than the rd write and does not access memory or modify any other registers. BINV never raises an exception for any rs2 value and is allowed in all privilege modes.

INSTRUCTION: BINVI
SUMMARY: The BINVI instruction uses a source integer register rs1, a destination integer register rd, and a shift‑amount immediate shamt. It masks shamt to the low log2(XLEN) bits to obtain an index, flips the corresponding bit of the value read from rs1 by XOR‑ing with 1<<index, and writes the result to rd. No other integer registers or any architectural state are altered, and the operation is permitted at any privilege level.

INSTRUCTION: BLT
SUMMARY: BLT compares the values in its two source registers as signed two's‑complement integers, using the sign bit to establish ordering. If the first source register's signed value is less than the second's, control transfers to the instruction's target label; otherwise execution continues with the next sequential instruction. No special privilege checks or exception behavior are defined for this operation.

INSTRUCTION: BLTU
SUMMARY: BLTU is an unsigned‑less‑than branch that compares the unsigned values in its two source registers (rs1 and rs2). If rs1 < rs2 the program counter is set to the address computed from the branch immediate; otherwise execution continues with the following instruction. The instruction is permitted in all privilege modes and imposes no special access restrictions. It can be used after an unsigned addition, using the addition result as rs1 and one addend as rs2, to branch only when an unsigned overflow has occurred.

INSTRUCTION: BNE
SUMMARY: BNE compares the values in its two source registers and takes the branch when they differ; if they are equal execution falls through to the next sequential instruction. When the branch is taken, the sign‑extended immediate offset is added to the address of the BNE instruction to form the branch‑target address. The instruction has no special privilege or access‑control requirements and does not raise exceptions under normal operation.

INSTRUCTION: BREV8
SUMMARY: BREV8 reads an integer register rs and writes to integer register rd an XLEN‑wide value whose each byte is the bit‑reversed version of the corresponding byte of rs. The source word is treated as a sequence of 8‑bit bytes; the eight bits in each byte are reversed (bit 0 ↔ bit 7, …) and the reversed bytes are concatenated in their original order, producing a fully defined XLEN‑bit result. The instruction only reads rs and writes rd, leaving all other architectural state—including privilege and control registers—unchanged. If any byte is not processed, the instruction fails to retire successfully.

INSTRUCTION: BSET
SUMMARY: BSET reads rs1 and rs2, masks rs2 to the low log2(XLEN) bits to obtain a bit index, and creates a mask by left‑shifting a 1 by that index using XLEN‑bit logical shift semantics. It writes rd with the bitwise OR of rs1 and the mask, setting exactly that bit in the rs1 value while leaving all other bits unchanged; rs1 and rs2 remain unchanged. The instruction affects no other architectural state, has no privilege‑level restrictions, and does not raise exceptions under normal operation.

INSTRUCTION: BSETI
SUMMARY: BSETI reads the source register rs1 and an immediate bit index supplied in the low log2(XLEN) bits of the shamt field. It produces a value equal to rs1 with the indicated bit forced to one while all other bits remain unchanged, and writes this result to the destination register rd. The instruction has no privilege‑level restrictions and does not generate any defined exceptions. No other architectural state is affected.

INSTRUCTION: C.ADD
SUMMARY: C.ADD reads integer values from registers rd and rs2, adds them, and writes the sum back to rd, having the same architectural effect as a 32‑bit ADD rd, rd, rs2. Encodings with rs2 = x0 are not C.ADD (they denote C.JALR or C.EBREAK), and any encoding where rd = x0 is treated as a hint that performs no register update, only advancing the PC. The instruction requires a non‑zero rs2; otherwise it is not defined as C.ADD. If a trap is taken on the target instruction or an interrupt occurs between a hint‑encoded NTL hint and its target, execution resumes at the target without re‑executing the hint, preserving program semantics.

INSTRUCTION: C.ADDI
SUMMARY: C.ADDI uses a destination register (rd) and a sign‑extended 6‑bit immediate. When rd ≠ x0 and the immediate is non‑zero, it adds the immediate to rd's current value and writes the result back, behaving like an addi rd,rd,imm. In all other encodings (immediate = 0 or rd = x0) the instruction is a hint or C.NOP: the PC advances, performance counters may update, and rd is written with its original value, leaving architectural state otherwise unchanged. No privilege checks are imposed on any form of the instruction.

INSTRUCTION: C.ADDI16SP
SUMMARY: C.ADDI16SP updates the stack pointer register x2 by adding a non‑zero sign‑extended 6‑bit immediate that is multiplied by 16. The result must stay within the signed range -512 to 496. Architecturally this is equivalent to the 32‑bit instruction `addi x2, x2, nzimm[9:4]`. No additional privilege or access checks beyond normal execution are required.

INSTRUCTION: C.ADDI4SPN
SUMMARY: C.ADDI4SPN reads the stack pointer (x2), adds a zero‑extended immediate value shifted left two bits (i.e., multiplied by 4), and writes the sum to the compressed destination register rd′. The immediate field must be non‑zero; an encoding with uimm = 0 is defined as an illegal instruction. The operation is unrestricted by privilege level and follows the normal read‑write semantics of integer registers. No other registers are accessed.

INSTRUCTION: C.ADDIW
SUMMARY: C.ADDIW is defined only for RV64C implementations and adds a 6‑bit immediate, sign‑extended to the operand width, to a non‑zero destination register rd that also supplies the source operand. The addition is performed modulo 2³² and the resulting 32‑bit value is sign‑extended to 64 bits before being written back to rd. When the sign‑extended immediate is zero, the instruction behaves like a sign‑extend‑word (sext.w) of rd. Encodings with rd = x0 are reserved and must not be used.

INSTRUCTION: C.ADDW
SUMMARY: C.ADDW adds the contents of rd′ (which also serves as rs1′) and rs2′, takes the low‑32‑bit sum, sign‑extends it to 64 bits, and writes the result to rd′. The instruction expands architecturally to addw rd′, rd′, rs2′. It is defined only for the RV64 base ISA; in RV32 the encoding is reserved and triggers an illegal‑instruction trap. No additional privilege checks or exceptions are specified beyond this ISA‑mode restriction.

INSTRUCTION: C.AND
SUMMARY: The C.AND instruction reads the architectural values of the integer registers identified by rd′ and rs2′, computes their bitwise AND, and writes the result back to rd′, replacing its previous value. It treats rd′ as both the destination and the first source operand, giving the same architectural effect as an AND rd′, rd′, rs2′ instruction. The operation applies to all integer registers without privilege restrictions and does not raise any architectural exceptions.

INSTRUCTION: C.ANDI
SUMMARY: C.ANDI reads the compressed register rs1′, using it as both source and destination (rd′), and computes a bitwise AND with a sign‑extended 6‑bit immediate. The immediate is sign‑extended to the processor XLEN before the operation, and the result is written back to the same register. The instruction is identified by the 01 opcode together with the 100 funct3 field and is architecturally equivalent to the uncompressed ANDI rd′,rd′,imm. It follows the same privilege and execution rules as the standard ANDI instruction, with no additional restrictions.

INSTRUCTION: C.BEQZ
SUMMARY: C.BEQZ tests the compressed register rs1′ for equality with zero and, if equal, jumps to a target address computed by sign‑extending its immediate offset and adding it to the address of the C.BEQZ instruction; otherwise execution continues with the next sequential instruction. The immediate field provides a signed offset that may reach any address within +/-256 bytes of the instruction. The instruction has the same architectural effect as the 32‑bit beq rs1′, x0, offset form. No special privilege checks or exception conditions are defined for this instruction.

INSTRUCTION: C.BNEZ
SUMMARY: C.BNEZ tests the compressed register rs1′ and takes the branch when rs1′ ≠ 0. The instruction contains a sign‑extended offset field that is added to the current PC to form the branch target address, which must lie within ±256 bytes of the PC. No special privilege level checks or exception conditions are defined for this instruction.

INSTRUCTION: C.EBREAK
SUMMARY: C.EBREAK is a compressed instruction whose rs2 field must be x0 and is decoded as a breakpoint exception. Executing it raises a synchronous breakpoint exception (cause 3) that writes the faulting instruction's virtual address (or zero) to mtval/stval and stores that address in mepc (or the current‑privilege epc), without incrementing minstret or retiring the instruction. The exception does not cause entry into Debug Mode and performs no architectural operation beyond the exception, unless overridden by an external debug environment. When the exception traps to M‑mode or S‑mode and sctrctl.BPFRZ = 1, the breakpoint is not recorded.

INSTRUCTION: C.FLD
SUMMARY: C.FLD reads a double‑precision floating‑point value from memory into the destination floating‑point register rd′. The effective address is formed by zero‑extending the immediate offset, scaling it by 8, and adding it to the base register rs1′. Architecturally it is equivalent to the uncompressed instruction fld rd′, offset(rs1′) with the same fields. The instruction is only defined on implementations that support both the Zca extension and the D (double‑precision) extension.

INSTRUCTION: C.FLDSP
SUMMARY: C.FLDSP loads a double‑precision floating‑point value from memory into the floating‑point destination register rd, using an offset (scaled by 8) added to the stack pointer x2 as the effective address and expanding to the 32‑bit "fld rd, offset(x2)" instruction. It writes the FP register specified by the rd field and masks the low three address bits to zero, so the actual read occurs from an aligned address. Pointer‑masking is applied like any explicit load but is suppressed when MXR is active, follows the effective mode's settings under MPRV or SPVP, and is bypassed for accesses generated by page‑table walks, IOMMUs, devices, or DMA. The instruction inherits FLD's permission checks, exception conditions, and permits misaligned accesses subject to the same limitations when pointer masking does not apply.

INSTRUCTION: C.FLW
SUMMARY: C.FLW loads a 32‑bit single‑precision floating‑point value from memory into the floating‑point register rd′, using rs1′ as a base address plus an offset that is zero‑extended, scaled by four, and added to rs1′. It has the same architectural effect as an FLW with the same rd′, rs1′, and offset, and is recognized by the opcode pattern 011…00. Implementations that support the Zca extension must not define C.FLW as a valid instruction.

INSTRUCTION: C.FLWSP
SUMMARY: C.FLWSP loads a 32‑bit single‑precision floating‑point value from memory into the destination floating‑point register rd, using an effective address formed by adding the stack pointer x2 to a zero‑extended immediate shifted left two bits. It has the same architectural effect as the base ISA instruction flw rd, offset(x2) and is defined only for RV32 implementations. The instruction imposes no privilege‑level restrictions and performs the same permission checks and exception handling as FLW, including pointer‑masking and the treatment of misaligned accesses. No side‑effects other than the floating‑point load are specified.

INSTRUCTION: C.FSD
SUMMARY: C.FSD is a 16‑bit compressed instruction that stores a double‑precision floating‑point value from source floating‑point register rs2′ to memory. The effective address is computed by zero‑extending the offset, multiplying it by 8, and adding the result to the base address in integer register rs1′. Its operation is semantically identical to the standard `fsd rs2′, offset(rs1′)` and is defined only on implementations that provide the C extension together with both the D and F extensions.

INSTRUCTION: C.FSDSP
SUMMARY: C.FSDSP stores a 64‑bit double‑precision value from floating‑point source register rs2 to memory using the stack pointer x2 plus a zero‑extended immediate offset shifted left three bits as the effective address. The instruction is only defined on implementations that provide the F and D extensions (i.e., RV32DC or RV64DC); otherwise an illegal‑instruction exception (mcause = 2) is raised. All accesses are subject to pointer‑masking and may be misaligned, with the architectural effect identical to executing an aligned fsd rs2, offset(x2) after applying the mask to each constituent access.

INSTRUCTION: C.FSW
SUMMARY: C.FSW stores the 32‑bit single‑precision floating‑point value from source floating‑point register rs2′ into memory. The effective address is computed by adding a zero‑extended immediate offset, multiplied by 4, to the base integer register rs1′. It has the same architectural effect as the 32‑bit instruction fsw rs2′, offset(rs1′) and is subject to the normal memory‑access and privilege checks of store operations.

INSTRUCTION: C.FSWSP
SUMMARY: C.FSWSP is a 16‑bit RV32 compressed instruction (requiring the Zcf extension) that stores a 32‑bit single‑precision floating‑point value from source register rs2 to the address obtained by adding a zero‑extended, 4‑byte‑scaled immediate to the stack pointer (x2). The immediate is formed from the uimm[5:2] and uimm[7:6] fields, zero‑extended, multiplied by 4, and the resulting address is subject to the current privilege mode's pointer‑masking transformation. It is architecturally equivalent to the 32‑bit FSW rs2, offset(x2) and permits misaligned stores, which are carried out as a sequence of aligned accesses with pointer‑masking applied to each. If the Zcf extension is not implemented or any exception other than the normal store occurs, an illegal‑instruction exception is raised.

INSTRUCTION: C.J
SUMMARY: C.J performs an unconditional jump by sign‑extending its immediate offset, adding it to the current PC, and targeting an address within ±2 KiB; the offset must be 2‑byte aligned or an illegal‑instruction exception (mcause = 2) is raised. The instruction expands to a jal x0 with the same offset, so no architectural register is written. If the encoding‑11 transfer‑type filter bit is implemented and set, the CTR CSR must retain its prior value across the jump. No other privilege checks are defined.

INSTRUCTION: C.JAL
SUMMARY: C.JAL encodes a 2‑byte‑aligned signed immediate that is sign‑extended and left‑shifted one bit to produce a jump target within ±2 KiB of the current PC. It updates the program counter to PC + (sign‑extended immediate << 1) and writes the address of the following instruction (PC + 2) into the link register x1. If either the link register does not receive PC + 2 or the PC is not set to the computed target, the architectural state is considered incorrect.

INSTRUCTION: C.JALR
SUMMARY: C.JALR performs an unconditional indirect jump to the address contained in source register rs1 and writes the address of the following instruction (pc + 2) into the link register x1. The instruction is architecturally valid only when rs1 ≠ x0; encodings with rs1 = x0 are defined as the C.EBREAK instruction. If the Zicfilp extension is enabled and rs1 is not x1, x5, or x7, the hart updates the exception link pointer (ELP) to LP_EXPECTED. A software‑check exception caused by Zicfilp on a C.JALR has higher priority than an illegal‑instruction exception but lower priority than an instruction‑access‑fault exception.

INSTRUCTION: C.JR
SUMMARY: C.JR performs an unconditional indirect jump by loading the program counter with the address contained in its rs1 operand. It expands to the base instruction jalr x0, 0(rs1), so no link register is written and the instruction is only valid when rs1 ≠ x0 (encodings with rs1 = x0 are reserved). When rs1 = x5 the jump returns to the address in x5, while any other non‑zero rs1 (except x1) acts as a plain indirect jump without linkage. No other registers are accessed and the operation has no special privilege restrictions.

INSTRUCTION: C.LBU
SUMMARY: C.LBU loads a single byte from memory at the address obtained by adding a zero‑extended 2‑bit immediate (derived from instruction bits 5‑6) to the value in source register rs1′ (one of x8‑x15). The byte is zero‑extended to the XLEN of the executing hart and written to destination register rd′ (also x8‑x15). The instruction obeys the normal load‑access rules of the current privilege mode and generates the standard load‑access fault on an illegal address.

INSTRUCTION: C.LD
SUMMARY: C.LD loads a 64‑bit doubleword from memory into a destination register rd′, with both rd′ and the base register rs1′ restricted to the registers x8–x15. The effective address is computed by adding rs1′ to an unsigned immediate offset that is zero‑extended and then scaled by 8. The instruction is defined only for RV64 implementations, requires the Zclsd extension (which also mandates Zilsd and Zca), and is otherwise treated as an illegal instruction on those implementations.

INSTRUCTION: C.LDSP
SUMMARY: C.LDSP is a RV64C instruction that loads a 64‑bit word from memory into a destination register rd (rd ≠ x0), producing the same architectural effect as the base ISA instruction ld rd, offset(x2). The effective address is computed by adding the current stack pointer (x2) to the zero‑extended immediate offset multiplied by eight, and then applying the required pointer‑masking transformation (including any privilege‑mode masking dictated by MPRV or SPVP). The instruction must support misaligned accesses, with the result defined as the composition of the pointer‑masked aligned accesses that would result from splitting the misaligned operation. Encodings with rd = x0 are reserved and have no defined architectural effect.

INSTRUCTION: C.LH
SUMMARY: C.LH is a compressed load that reads a signed 16‑bit halfword from memory at the address formed by adding an unsigned immediate offset to the source register rs1' (both rd' and rs1' are selected from x8‑x15). The halfword is sign‑extended to the processor's XLEN and written to the destination register rd'. The instruction performs the same privilege and permission checks as a regular load and raises only the standard load exceptions. No other architectural checks or side‑effects are introduced.

INSTRUCTION: C.LHU
SUMMARY: C.LHU uses a compressed source register rs1' (x8‑x15) and a compressed destination register rd' (x8‑x15). It forms an unsigned immediate with the instruction's bit 5 as imm[1] (all other bits zero), adds this to rs1' to obtain the effective address, and loads a 16‑bit halfword from that address. The halfword is zero‑extended to XLEN and written to rd'. Execution never raises an exception, regardless of privilege level.

INSTRUCTION: C.LI
SUMMARY: C.LI places a sign‑extended 6‑bit immediate into the architectural destination register rd, acting identically to a 32‑bit addi rd, x0, imm. When rd is x0 the instruction is defined as a hint and must not change any architectural state. The instruction is required to have its rd field set to x0, so it never updates a general‑purpose register. Apart from advancing the program counter (and any performance counters), C.LI does not modify any other architectural state.

INSTRUCTION: C.LUI
SUMMARY: C.LUI writes an upper‑immediate to a destination register rd, but the encoding is only defined when rd is neither x0 nor x2 and the immediate is non‑zero. If rd = x0 with a non‑zero immediate the instruction is treated as a hint that produces no architectural state change, and if rd = x2 with a non‑zero immediate the encoding is reinterpreted as the C.ADDI16SP instruction. An encoding whose immediate field is zero raises an illegal‑instruction exception (the encoding is reserved for future extensions). In all defined cases the program counter is advanced to the following instruction.

INSTRUCTION: C.LW
SUMMARY: C.LW is a 16‑bit compressed load that reads a 32‑bit word from memory into the destination register rd′. The effective address is formed by adding the zero‑extended offset (scaled by 4) to the base register rs1′, giving the same architectural effect as the 32‑bit instruction lw rd′, offset(rs1′). It resides in quadrant 0 (opcode bits 00) and uses the rs1′ field as the base and the rd′ field as the destination; it is defined for both RV32 and RV64 base ISAs with no extra operand constraints. Execution follows the normal privilege model for loads, with no special exception behavior beyond that of a standard lw.

INSTRUCTION: C.LWSP
SUMMARY: C.LWSP loads a 32‑bit word from memory into the destination register rd, and rd must be non‑zero (otherwise an illegal‑instruction exception is raised). The effective address is formed by adding the stack pointer x2 to a zero‑extended unsigned immediate encoded in the instruction and scaled by 4. Architecturally it has the same effect as executing lw rd, offset(x2), and the address is subject to the standard pointer‑masking rules that apply to all explicit memory accesses. If the masked address causes a permission or other fault that would not arise for an equivalent lw to the same masked address, the processor takes an unexpected exception (e.g., an illegal‑instruction exception).

INSTRUCTION: C.MOP.1
SUMMARY: C.MOP.1 performs an XLEN‑wide store to the address derived from the shadow‑stack pointer (ssp) without writing any architectural register. The effective address is implicitly ssp‑(XLEN/8) and ssp is decremented only after the store completes successfully. Execution is gated by the xSSE flag: when xSSE = 1 the store proceeds, otherwise no memory update occurs and ssp remains unchanged. Any attempt by the instruction to read an architectural register triggers an illegal‑instruction exception.

INSTRUCTION: C.MOP.11
SUMMARY: The C.MOP.11 instruction performs its operation without writing to any architectural register, so it may only read registers or internal state. No architectural registers are altered, giving the instruction no side effects on the visible register file. It is not subject to special privilege constraints and may be executed in any privilege mode. No exception or fault behavior is defined by the rules.

INSTRUCTION: C.MOP.13
SUMMARY: C.MOP.13 executes without altering any architectural register file entry, leaving the processor's register state unchanged for the duration of the instruction.

INSTRUCTION: C.MOP.15
SUMMARY: C.MOP.15 is a compressed instruction that performs its defined operation without writing to any architectural register; all register accesses are read‑only. Consequently, it does not alter the programmer‑visible register state regardless of privilege level.

INSTRUCTION: C.MOP.3
SUMMARY: C.MOP.3 is a read‑only operation that may access architectural registers but never updates them. Consequently, it produces no side‑effects on the integer register file. No special privilege or access‑control checks are defined for this instruction. No exception or fault behavior is specified beyond the standard architectural rules.

INSTRUCTION: C.MOP.5
SUMMARY: C.MOP.5 is a shadow‑stack check that loads an XLEN‑bit value from the address held in the shadow‑stack pointer (ssp) and, without writing any integer register, compares it to the contents of x5. The instruction executes only when the architectural flag xSSE is 1; otherwise it has no architectural effect. If the loaded value differs from x5, a software‑check exception with cause 18 and xtval set to "shadow stack fault (code 3)" is raised (lower‑priority than any load/store/AMO access‑fault), and only when the load succeeds and the values match is ssp incremented by XLEN/8 bytes.

INSTRUCTION: C.MOP.7
SUMMARY: C.MOP.7 performs its defined operation without writing any architectural register, though it may read source registers as needed. It is subject only to the normal ISA privilege model and imposes no additional access restrictions. Consequently, no architectural state is altered, avoiding any write‑back side effects. Any exceptions are handled according to the standard ISA exception mechanisms.

INSTRUCTION: C.MOP.9
SUMMARY: C.MOP.9 executes without modifying any integer register, making it effectively read‑only with respect to the architectural GPR file. No privilege or access‑control constraints are imposed by the rule, so the instruction is permitted in all privilege modes. No exception or special‑case behavior is defined beyond the guarantee that integer registers remain unchanged.

INSTRUCTION: C.MUL
SUMMARY: C.MUL treats the compressed register rsd′ (x8‑x15) as both the destination rd′ and the first source rs1′, with rs2′ (also x8‑x15) providing the second source operand. It multiplies the signed XLEN‑bit values in rsd′ and rs2′, forming a 2·XLEN‑bit product, and writes the low XLEN bits of that product back into rsd′. No other architectural state is altered and the instruction has no special exception behavior beyond the normal execution flow.

INSTRUCTION: C.MV
SUMMARY: C.MV is defined only for encodings where the destination register rd is x0 and the source register rs2 is any non‑zero register; any other combination is reserved, maps to C.JR when rs2 = 0, or is treated as a hint. Architecturally the instruction would copy the value of rs2 into rd (equivalent to an ADD with x0 as the first operand), but execution must not modify any integer‑register state (including x0) nor any memory state. The sole observable effect is advancing the program counter to the following instruction, and implementations may treat it as a no‑op aside from optional performance‑counter updates. No special privilege or access‑control checks apply; the instruction is available in all privilege levels.

INSTRUCTION: C.NOP
SUMMARY: C.NOP produces no architectural effect except advancing the program counter to the following instruction (and updating any performance counters). It does not read or write any registers, memory, or other state and generates no exceptions, permission checks, or side‑effects. The instruction is recognized as a valid compressed operation on RV64 implementations.

INSTRUCTION: C.NOT
SUMMARY: C.NOT computes the one's‑complement of the value in the specified register and writes the result back to that same register, which must be one of the eight standard registers x8‑x15. The instruction is permitted in any privilege mode and never raises an illegal‑instruction exception. It yields an architectural result identical to a 32‑bit `xori rd, rd, -1`, i.e., a bitwise NOT of the source operand written to the destination.

INSTRUCTION: C.NTL.ALL
SUMMARY: C.NTL.ALL is a hint that merely advances the program counter to the next instruction and may update performance counters, leaving all architectural registers, CSRs, and memory unchanged. It never raises an exception, and if a trap is taken on the target instruction the hint is ignored rather than applied to the trap handler. An interrupt occurring after the hint has completed but before the target is fetched causes a return to the target address without re‑executing the hint, with the architectural state exactly as if the hint had run normally. Implementations that ignore hints may treat C.NTL.ALL as a no‑op.

INSTRUCTION: C.NTL.P1
SUMMARY: C.NTL.P1 is a hint that has no architectural effect other than advancing the program counter (and optionally updating performance counters). It must not modify any registers or other visible state, and the semantics of its target instruction are preserved unchanged. If a trap is taken on the hint, the first trap‑handler instruction executes as if the hint were absent, producing its normal result; similarly, an interrupt occurring between the hint and its target causes execution to continue directly at the target without re‑executing the hint. Implementations may treat C.NTL.P1 as a no‑op, performing only the required PC increment.

INSTRUCTION: C.NTL.PALL
SUMMARY: C.NTL.PALL is a no‑operation hint that advances the program counter to its following instruction without modifying any architectural registers, memory, or CSR state. Its only side effect is the optional update of implementation‑defined performance counters. If an interrupt is taken after C.NTL.PALL but before the target instruction, control resumes directly at the target instruction, skipping a re‑execution of C.NTL.PALL. The instruction is permitted in any privilege mode because it does not alter privileged state.

INSTRUCTION: C.NTL.S1
SUMMARY: C.NTL.S1 advances the program counter to the next instruction and otherwise leaves all architectural registers, memory, and performance counters unchanged (aside from optional counting of its execution). It must not affect the architecturally visible results of the immediately following instruction, and even if an implementation treats it as a computational hint it may not mutate any architectural state. If a trap is taken on the hinted target, the trap handler's first instruction executes normally with the hint ignored, and if an interrupt occurs before the target executes, the handler returns (mret) to the original target address with the same architectural state as if the interrupt had not occurred.

INSTRUCTION: C.SB
SUMMARY: C.SB computes an effective address by adding a zero‑extended unsigned immediate to the value in the compressed base register rs1′ (x8‑x15). It stores the least‑significant byte of the compressed source register rs2′ (also x8‑x15) to the byte at that address, performing a byte‑store to memory. The instruction imposes no architectural prerequisites and may execute in any processor state without privilege checks. Its behavior otherwise matches the standard 32‑bit store‑byte semantics.

INSTRUCTION: C.SD
SUMMARY: C.SD stores the 64‑bit value from source register rs2′ (or the double‑word formed by rs2′/rs2′+1) to memory. The effective address is computed as the contents of base register rs1′ plus a zero‑extended immediate multiplied by eight, and both rs1′ and rs2′ must be registers x8‑x15. The instruction is defined only for implementations that implement the RV64 base ISA with the RV64C extension; it raises an illegal‑instruction exception if the RV64C extension is absent, if the Zcf extension is present, or if the required ISA support is missing. If rs2′ is x0, the value written to memory is zero.

INSTRUCTION: C.SDSP
SUMMARY: C.SDSP stores the 64‑bit value from source register rs2 to memory at the address formed by adding the stack pointer (x2) to a zero‑extended immediate multiplied by eight, where the immediate is assembled from the instruction's uimm[5:3] and uimm[8:6] fields. The operation is architecturally equivalent to executing the uncompressed instruction sd rs2, offset(x2) with the same rs2 and offset. If rs2 encodes x0, a zero value is written instead of the register contents. The effective address is subject to the privilege‑mode pointer‑masking mechanism, unless overridden by the MXR setting.

INSTRUCTION: C.SEXT.B
SUMMARY: C.SEXT.B is a 16‑bit compressed instruction that uses one register operand, which serves simultaneously as the source and the destination. It sign‑extends the least‑significant byte of that register to the full XLEN by copying bit 7 of the byte into all more‑significant bits of the destination register. The instruction is defined only when the Zbb extension is implemented in the processor. No additional privilege checks or exception behavior are specified beyond the Zbb requirement.

INSTRUCTION: C.SEXT.H
SUMMARY: C.SEXT.H uses a single register operand, rd′/rs1′, which serves as both source and destination and must be one of x8‑x15. The instruction sign‑extends the low 16 bits of the source value to the full XLEN by replicating bit 15 into all higher bits of the destination. It is defined only when the Zbb extension is present, and any implementation that includes the Zcb extension is required to implement C.SEXT.H. No other privilege or access checks are specified.

INSTRUCTION: C.SH
SUMMARY: C.SH stores the low‑order halfword (bits 15‑0) of the source register rs2′ to the effective address computed as rs1′ plus a zero‑extended 2‑bit immediate (uimm); only the targeted 16‑bit halfword in memory is updated. The instruction imposes no privilege or architectural prerequisites and can be executed in any mode. On a core lacking the Zcb extension it triggers an illegal‑instruction exception (mcause = 2).

INSTRUCTION: C.SLLI
SUMMARY: C.SLLI reads the value in the register identified by rd (which also acts as rs1), logically left‑shifts it by the shamt immediate, and writes the result back to the same register. The instruction is only defined when rd ≠ x0 and shamt ≠ 0; if either field is zero the encoding is treated as a hint that leaves all architectural state unchanged except for normal PC advancement and optional performance‑counter updates. No architectural state besides the destination register (or none for hints) and the program counter is modified, and no exception is raised. Execution is permitted at any privilege level provided the rd and shamt constraints are satisfied.

INSTRUCTION: C.SRAI
SUMMARY: C.SRAI uses the compressed register rd′ as both source and destination for an arithmetic right‑shift by the immediate shift amount. The immediate field (shamt/uimm) must be zero; any non‑zero value raises an illegal‑instruction exception, and a zero immediate causes the instruction to be a HINT that only advances the PC (and optional performance counters) without altering architectural state. When executed as a HINT the destination register must be x0 or identical to the source register to guarantee no state change. No permission checks or other exceptions are performed, and the operation otherwise follows the semantics of the base RVI shift‑right‑arithmetic‑immediate instruction.

INSTRUCTION: C.SRLI
SUMMARY: C.SRLI performs a logical right shift on the compressed register rd′ by the amount encoded in the shamt field, writes the shifted value back to rd′, and is architecturally equivalent to the 32‑bit instruction srli rd′, rd′, shamt. The instruction's immediate field must be zero; if the shamt value itself is zero the operation is defined as a hint with no architectural effect. Execution only advances the program counter (and may update performance counters) without altering any other architectural state. No special privilege or access checks are required beyond normal instruction execution.

INSTRUCTION: C.SUB
SUMMARY: C.SUB reads the integer value from source register rs2′ and the integer value from destination register rd′, subtracts the former from the latter, and writes the result back into rd′. The compressed encoding supplies the rd′ and rs2′ fields to identify the destination and second‑source registers. Execution produces exactly the same architectural result as the full‑width instruction sub rd′, rd′, rs2′ and is permitted in all unprivileged modes. No additional side effects or exceptions are defined beyond those of the equivalent sub instruction.

INSTRUCTION: C.SUBW
SUMMARY: C.SUBW reads the source register rs2′ and the destination register rd′, computes rd′ − rs2′, sign‑extends the low 32‑bit result, and writes it back to rd′. The instruction has the same architectural effect as an RV64 subw rd′, rd′, rs2′. It follows the normal register‑file access rules with no special privilege restrictions. In RV64 implementations the operation is defined behavior and must not raise an illegal‑instruction exception.

INSTRUCTION: C.SW
SUMMARY: The C.SW instruction stores a 32‑bit word from the source register rs2′ into memory. The target address is formed by zero‑extending the immediate, multiplying it by 4, and adding the result to the base register rs1′. The operation has the same architectural effect as the 32‑bit store instruction `sw rs2′, offset(rs1′)` with identical register contents and immediate value. Execution requires that the effective address be writable in the current privilege mode, otherwise a store‑access fault is raised.

INSTRUCTION: C.SWSP
SUMMARY: C.SWSP stores the 32‑bit value from source register rs2 to memory at an address formed by adding a zero‑extended unsigned immediate (derived from the uimm field) multiplied by 4 to the stack pointer x2. The effective address is transformed by the current pointer‑mask before the write is performed. Misaligned stores are allowed and are executed as the masked address for each aligned sub‑access that makes up the misaligned store. Since C.SWSP is not a fence or invalidate operation, pointer masking is required for its address.

INSTRUCTION: C.XOR
SUMMARY: C.XOR reads the architectural values from the destination register rd′ and source register rs2′, computes their bitwise exclusive‑OR, and writes the result back to rd′. It uses the same integer‑register fields as the standard xor rd′, rd′, rs2′ instruction, giving it identical architectural effect. The operation is unprivileged and may be executed in any mode that permits ordinary integer register writes. No special exceptions or fault conditions are defined beyond those of the regular xor instruction.

INSTRUCTION: C.ZEXT.B
SUMMARY: C.ZEXT.B takes a single architectural register that serves as both source and destination, reads its least‑significant byte, zero‑extends that byte to the full XLEN, and writes the result back to the same register. The instruction is valid only when encoded with exactly one register operand; any other encoding causes an illegal‑instruction exception. It imposes no privilege‑mode or architectural prerequisites and may be executed in any mode. Functionally it is equivalent to the 32‑bit sequence `andi rd, rd, 0xff`.

INSTRUCTION: C.ZEXT.H
SUMMARY: C.ZEXT.H is a 16‑bit compressed instruction that operates on a register in the x8–x15 range, using it as both source and destination. It reads the low 16 bits of the register, zero‑extends them to the processor's XLEN, and writes the result back to the same register. The instruction is only available when the Zbb extension is implemented, performs no privilege or permission checks, and does not raise any architectural exceptions. No other architectural state is modified.

INSTRUCTION: C.ZEXT.W
SUMMARY: C.ZEXT.W reads an architectural register (rd′/rs1′), zero‑extends its lower 32 bits, and writes the 64‑bit result back to the same register, clearing all upper bits. It is defined only on implementations that support the Zba extension; on processors without Zba the encoding is reserved and raises an illegal‑instruction exception. Architecturally the instruction has the same effect as executing add.uw rd′, rd′, zero on a 32‑bit implementation.

INSTRUCTION: CBO.CLEAN
SUMMARY: CBO.CLEAN is a cache‑block clean instruction whose legality depends on the CBCFE fields in the menvcfg and senvcfg CSRs. In machine mode it executes only when menvcfg.CBCFE = 1, and any non‑zero offset also triggers an illegal‑instruction exception. In supervisor or user modes the instruction is permitted only if menvcfg.CBCFE = 1 (and the instruction is enabled for S‑mode use), and for user mode senvcfg.CBCFE must additionally be 1; otherwise an illegal‑instruction exception is raised. All other illegal‑instruction and virtual‑instruction exception conditions are identical to those defined for CBO.FLUSH.

INSTRUCTION: CBO.FLUSH
SUMMARY: The rs1 register supplies the virtual address for CBO.FLUSH, need not be cache‑block aligned, and is reported as the faulting address if a fault occurs. Execution is allowed in machine mode unconditionally, but in any less‑privileged mode it requires the corresponding CBCFE control bit (menvcfg.CBCFE for S/H, senvcfg.CBCFE for U) to be set; otherwise an illegal‑instruction exception is raised, and if Zicbom is absent the same restriction applies. For an HS‑qualified CBO.FLUSH, a virtual‑instruction exception occurs when henvcfg.CBCFE = 0, while henvcfg.CBCFE = 1 enables the instruction. When neither illegal‑instruction nor virtual‑instruction conditions are met, the instruction completes normally.

INSTRUCTION: CBO.INVAL
SUMMARY: CBO.INVAL uses the value in rs1 as the target virtual address, which need not be cache‑block aligned. Execution is always allowed in machine mode, but in any less‑privileged mode it requires the CBIE field in the relevant environment‑configuration register (menvcfg for M‑mode, senvcfg for S/U‑mode) to be non‑zero; if CBIE=00 an illegal‑instruction exception is raised, and U‑mode is further permitted only when the instruction is also enabled for S‑mode and senvcfg.CBIE is 01b or 11b. If a fault occurs, the faulting address is defined as the value in rs1.

INSTRUCTION: CBO.ZERO
SUMMARY: CBO.ZERO uses rs1 as the effective address and zeros every byte of the cache block containing that address, with the result observable by subsequent loads regardless of alignment. Execution is allowed only when the instruction is HS‑qualified and the CBZE enable bit is set in the appropriate envcfg register for the current privilege mode (menvcfg for M, senvcfg for S/U); otherwise an illegal‑instruction exception is raised. Standard privilege and PMP checks (including MPRV, SUM, MXR, and per‑block PMP bits) are applied, and any denial generates a store/AMO‑type access‑fault or page‑fault exception, with mcause indicating a store fault and tval written with the rs1 address. If pointer masking would block the access, the instruction performs no store, and any fault reports the rs1 virtual address as the faulting address.

INSTRUCTION: CLMUL
SUMMARY: The CLMUL instruction reads the two source registers rs1 and rs2, interprets their XLEN‑bit values as binary polynomials over GF(2), and computes their carry‑less (polynomial) product. It writes the lower XLEN bits of the full 2·XLEN‑bit product into the destination register rd. The operation is defined for RV64 and must be implemented in any core that includes the Zbkb or Zbkx extensions (or otherwise provides CLMUL). If an architectural exception occurs or the instruction fails to retire successfully, the carry‑less multiplication may be incomplete and no guaranteed result is written to rd.

INSTRUCTION: CLMULH
SUMMARY: The CLMULH instruction uses three integer registers: rs1 and rs2 as sources and rd as the destination. It computes the carry‑less multiplication of the XLEN‑bit values in rs1 and rs2, forms the 2·XLEN‑bit product, and writes the upper XLEN bits of that product to rd. Only rd is updated; rs1, rs2, and all other architectural registers remain unchanged. The operation is non‑privileged and has no defined exception behavior.

INSTRUCTION: CLMULR
SUMMARY: CLMULR reads the architectural registers rs1 and rs2, treats each as bit‑reversed, computes their carry‑less product, bit‑reverses the 2·XLEN‑bit result, and extracts bits 2·XLEN‑2 down to XLEN‑1 to form an XLEN‑bit value. The extracted XLEN‑bit value is written to the destination register rd, overwriting its prior architectural contents. The instruction is an XLEN‑wide integer operation with no special privilege or access‑control requirements and may be executed in any privilege mode. No architectural exceptions are defined for CLMULR; it completes normally regardless of operand values.

INSTRUCTION: CLZ
SUMMARY: The CLZ instruction reads the XLEN‑bit value from source integer register rs, counts the number of contiguous zero bits starting at the most‑significant bit, and writes that count into destination integer register rd. If the source value is zero, the full XLEN is written to rd; otherwise the result equals XLEN-1-i where i is the index of the highest set bit (producing zero when the MSB is one). The operation has no special privilege or access restrictions beyond normal integer‑register read/write semantics.

INSTRUCTION: CLZW
SUMMARY: CLZW is a RV64‑only instruction that reads the low‑order 32 bits of source register rs (ignoring the upper 32 bits) and counts the consecutive zero bits starting from the most‑significant bit of that 32‑bit word. The resulting count—0 to 31 when any bit in rs is set, 0 when bit 31 is 1, and 32 when rs is zero—is written to destination register rd and extended to the processor's XLEN. Execution of CLZW modifies only rd; no other architectural state or privilege level is affected.

INSTRUCTION: CM.JALT
SUMMARY: CM.JALT uses the 8‑bit index field to select an entry from the 256‑entry jump table whose base address is given by the jvt CSR; for XLEN = 64 the table address is computed as jvt.base + (index << 3) and the XLEN‑wide entry is read with execute permission only. The fetched address (with its least‑significant bit cleared) becomes the new PC, and pc+2 is written to the link register x1 (ra) as the return address. The table must be aligned on a 64‑byte boundary, otherwise a misaligned‑instruction exception (mcause = 0) is raised; any fetch exception on the CM.JALT sets xEPC to the faulting PC, xCAUSE to the appropriate cause, and, if non‑zero, xTVAL to the faulting address. When all transfer‑type filter bits in the CTR are unimplemented or cleared, the instruction is recorded as a control transfer in enabled privileged mode.

INSTRUCTION: CM.JT
SUMMARY: CM.JT uses the index encoded in the instruction to select one of 256 XLEN‑wide entries in a jump‑vector table located in instruction memory, reading the entry with the processor's current data‑endianness. The fetched XLEN‑bit value has its least‑significant bit cleared and the resulting address is loaded into the program counter; for XLEN = 64 the table address must be computed as jvt.base + (index << 3). The instruction is legal in any privilege mode and never raises an illegal‑instruction exception. On any fetch exception of the CM.JT instruction, xEPC is set to the CM.JT address, xCAUSE receives the fault code, and xTVAL is written with the faulting fetch address if it is non‑zero.

INSTRUCTION: CM.MVA01S
SUMMARY: CM.MVA01S is defined only for 64‑bit implementations; executing it on a core with XLEN < 64 triggers an illegal‑instruction exception (mcause = 2). The instruction writes the value from the source s‑register r1s' into the architectural register a0 (x10) and the value from r2s' into a1 (x11). Both destination updates are required to occur atomically; if atomicity is not guaranteed, the post‑instruction state will contain the new value in one of the two registers while the other retains its prior content. No additional privilege checks are specified beyond the XLEN constraint.

INSTRUCTION: CM.MVSA01
SUMMARY: CM.MVSA01 transfers the architectural values of a0 and a1 into two distinct destination registers chosen from s0–s7, with the first s‑register receiving a0 and the second receiving a1 while leaving all other architectural state unchanged. The instruction is only legal when the two s‑register specifiers differ. Upon execution each destination's corresponding xreg (xreg1 for the first, xreg2 for the second) is written a 3‑bit code that encodes the selected s‑register index: the most‑significant bit indicates whether the index's upper two bits are non‑zero, the middle bit indicates whether they are zero, and the three‑least‑significant bits contain the index itself. No additional privilege or access checks are imposed beyond the distinct‑register requirement.

INSTRUCTION: CM.POP
SUMMARY: CM.POP transfers the contents of a contiguous block of saved registers (always ra followed by s0‑sN, N≤11) from the stack to the architectural registers, using the rlist field to encode the register list and the spimm immediate to size the stack adjustment. The instruction reads 32‑bit words from memory at addresses sp+offsets (starting at sp+12 for the smallest list and increasing by 4 for each additional register) and writes them in descending order (highest s register down to ra) before adding stack_adj = stack_adj_base + spimm·16 to sp atomically. Execution is defined only when the Zca extension is present and the Zcd combination (C + D) is absent; otherwise an illegal‑instruction exception is raised. The stack‑pointer update occurs only if all loads complete without exception or interrupt, and no architectural state other than the listed registers and sp is modified.

INSTRUCTION: CM.POPRET
SUMMARY: CM.POPRET restores the return‑address register ra and a contiguous subset of saved registers s0‑s12 (as selected by the rlist field) from stack locations computed from a stack‑adjustment derived from rlist and the unsigned spimm immediate, using XLEN‑sized loads in a fixed descending order. After all loads complete without exception, it atomically adds the computed adjustment to sp and performs a ret jump to the restored ra, forming a single atomic return phase. If a trap occurs before the load sequence finishes, registers not yet loaded retain their pre‑return values, while any registers already loaded are visible in the architectural state at trap entry. When executed in an enabled privileged mode with default CTR transfer‑type filters cleared, the instruction is recorded as a function‑return transfer in the relevant CSR.

INSTRUCTION: CM.POPRETZ
SUMMARY: CM.POPRETZ takes a register‑list operand containing between one and thirteen registers selected from ra and s0‑s12, but only the specific lists {ra}, {ra,s0}, {ra,s0‑s1}, … {ra,s0‑s11} are legal; any other list (including {ra,s0‑s10}) raises an illegal‑instruction exception. When executed, it loads each register in the list from the current stack frame using offsets derived from the immediate field, writes the constant 0 to a0 as the function return value, increments the stack pointer by a total adjustment computed from a base value plus 16‑byte increments, and finally jumps to the address now held in ra. The load phase, the a0 write, the stack‑pointer adjustment, and the return must all complete without exception; if a trap occurs, any loads that have already completed are re‑executed after the trap handler, and the instruction commits atomically only after all phases succeed. Once the stack‑pointer update is committed, the subsequent return must be performed, with no partial commitment of a0 or the return allowed.

INSTRUCTION: CM.PUSH
SUMMARY: CM.PUSH stores the registers given by a register‑list that must contain ra and a contiguous subset of s0‑s12 (1‑13 registers), using a 32‑bit word store on RV32 or a 64‑bit doubleword store on RV64, and writes each register in descending register number to consecutive decreasing addresses below the original stack pointer. After all stores complete without precise faults, the stack pointer is decremented once by a total adjustment equal to the smallest 16‑byte‑aligned base that fits the list plus the signed immediate field, and this update becomes visible only after the instruction finishes. The instruction raises an illegal‑instruction exception if the Zca extension is not implemented, the register‑list is not one of the permitted sets (including the {ra, s0‑s10} case), or the requested stack‑adjustment value is not allowed for the given list size. No other privilege checks are required; the processor must be certain the instruction will commit before performing the stack‑pointer update, guaranteeing atomicity with respect to interrupts and exceptions.

INSTRUCTION: CPOP
SUMMARY: CPOP reads the integer register rs, counts the number of 1‑bits in its XLEN‑bit value, and writes that population count to integer register rd. The result is a non‑negative integer in the range 0 to XLEN, rd is the only architectural register modified, and rs retains its original value. The instruction is defined for both RV32 (XLEN = 32) and RV64 (XLEN = 64) implementations, automatically using the appropriate XLEN width. No additional privilege checks or exception behavior are specified beyond the normal integer‑register write.

INSTRUCTION: CPOPW
SUMMARY: CPOPW reads the low‑order 32 bits of integer source register rs, ignoring the upper XLEN‑32 bits, and counts the number of 1‑bits in that word (result 0–32). The 32‑bit count is written to the low 32 bits of destination register rd and the remaining bits of rd are cleared (zero‑extended to the full XLEN). The instruction updates only rd, leaving all other architectural state unchanged. It executes without raising an exception for any valid source value and has no special privilege restrictions.

INSTRUCTION: CSRRC
SUMMARY: CSRRC reads the addressed CSR, zero‑extends it to XLEN, writes that original value to rd, and, unless rs1 is x0, clears each writable CSR bit for which the corresponding mask bit in rs1 is high, then writes the modified value back to the CSR. If rs1 is x0 the CSR is not written, providing a legal read‑only access that does not raise an illegal‑instruction exception for read‑only CSRs, while any non‑zero rs1 forces a write‑back even when the mask is zero. Access to the seed CSR is privileged: rs1 = x0 causes an illegal‑instruction exception, rs1 ≠ x0 is permitted but the mask bits are ignored; attempts to read or write the seed CSR or any CSR without the required privilege (e.g., mip in non‑M mode without explicit grant) also raise an illegal‑instruction exception. For the mip CSR, only the software‑writable SEIP bit participates in the read‑modify‑write, the external interrupt signal contributes to the value placed in rd but is never used in computing the value written back.

INSTRUCTION: CSRRCI
SUMMARY: CSRRCI treats the targeted CSR as a source register, always reading it and applying any read‑side effects. The zero‑extended 5‑bit immediate supplied in the rs1 field selects which CSR bits are cleared; when the immediate is non‑zero the CSR is also a destination and the write may trigger write‑side effects, while a zero immediate causes no write or write‑side effects. The immediate value itself is not written back, and the instruction provides a legal method to read a CSR. An illegal‑instruction exception is raised only if the immediate is zero and the target CSR is the seed CSR; otherwise read‑only CSRs can be accessed without fault.

INSTRUCTION: CSRRS
SUMMARY: CSRRS always reads the addressed CSR, zero‑extends the value and writes it to destination register rd, while rs1 supplies a mask that, when non‑zero, sets the corresponding writable bits of the CSR. If rs1 is x0 the instruction is read‑only (no CSR write or per‑field side effects) but still triggers any read side effects; it is legal for read‑only CSRs, whereas writing a read‑only CSR or reading a seed CSR with rs1 = x0 raises an illegal‑instruction exception. Only software‑writable fields (e.g., the SEIP bit) participate in the read‑modify‑write, with SEIP being OR‑ed with the rs1 bit on write and with the external interrupt signal on read, while other CSR bits are unaffected by the mask.

INSTRUCTION: CSRRSI
SUMMARY: CSRRSI reads the addressed CSR, zero‑extends the value and writes it to integer register rd, using the 5‑bit uimm field as a mask that sets to 1 each writable CSR bit where the mask has a 1. If uimm is zero the instruction performs only the read, generates no CSR write side‑effects, and never faults for a read‑only CSR (except that reading the seed CSR with uimm = 0 is illegal). When uimm is non‑zero the instruction must write to the CSR, affecting only masked writable fields, and raises an illegal‑instruction exception if the CSR is read‑only, if rd is x0, or if the target is the seed CSR with a zero mask. The immediate value itself is not written to any architectural state.

INSTRUCTION: CSRRW
SUMMARY: CSRRW atomically swaps a CSR's value with the integer register rs1, writing the old CSR value (zero‑extended) to rd unless rd is x0, in which case the read is omitted but the write still occurs (writing zero if rs1 is x0). The instruction always performs a write; for the seed CSR the write is ignored, the CSR's current value is copied to rd, and any access to the seed CSR outside M‑mode without explicit permission raises an illegal‑instruction exception. When the mip CSR is targeted, rd receives the SEIP field equal to the logical‑OR of the software‑writable bit B and the external‑interrupt signal E, while a write updates only B from bit 9 of rs1, independent of E. Access to regular read/write CSRs such as sscratch is allowed, and if a write fails to apply defined side effects, subsequent reads return the original pre‑instruction value for those fields.

INSTRUCTION: CSRRWI
SUMMARY: CSRRWI writes a CSR with a value obtained by zero‑extending the 5‑bit immediate encoded in the rs1 field to XLEN bits. If the destination register rd is x0, the instruction performs only the write, omitting any CSR read and its side effects; otherwise it reads the current CSR value, writes the zero‑extended immediate, and places the original CSR content into rd. CSR accesses are subject to the normal privilege checks applicable to CSR instructions. The rs1 field supplies the immediate, while rd either discards or receives the prior CSR value.

INSTRUCTION: CTZ
SUMMARY: The CTZ instruction reads the integer value in source register rs, counts the number of consecutive zero bits starting at the least‑significant bit, and writes that count into destination register rd. If rs contains zero, the count equals the architectural XLEN; otherwise the count is the index of the least‑significant set bit (zero when bit 0 is one). The result is stored in the architectural register identified by rd with no privilege restrictions. No other side effects are defined.

INSTRUCTION: CTZW
SUMMARY: CTZW reads the low‑order 32‑bit word of rs, ignoring all higher bits, and counts the number of consecutive zero bits starting at bit 0 up to the first one‑bit (yielding a value from 0 to 32, with 32 when the word is all zeros). The resulting count is written to rd and extended to the processor's XLEN width. The instruction is defined only for implementations that support the RV64 base ISA and is not defined for RV32. No other side effects or privilege restrictions are specified.

INSTRUCTION: CZERO.EQZ
SUMMARY: CZERO.EQZ reads registers rs1 and rs2; if rs2 equals zero it writes the constant zero to rd, otherwise it copies the value of rs1 into rd. It also creates a predicate that enables the immediately‑following integer instruction only when rs2 is non‑zero, and this predicate does not affect any later instructions. The instruction makes no other architectural changes, performs no memory or CSR accesses, and advances the program counter normally. It is available in all privilege modes without special restrictions.

INSTRUCTION: CZERO.NEZ
SUMMARY: CZERO.NEZ reads source registers rs1 and rs2 and the condition register rc, producing a result in destination register rd. If the architectural value read from rs2 is non‑zero, rd is written with zero regardless of other operands. When rc equals zero, the instruction executes either an addition (rd = rs1 + rs2) or an XOR (rd = rs1 ^ rs2) as encoded; otherwise rd remains unchanged. No special privilege or exception handling beyond this zero‑write behavior is defined.

INSTRUCTION: DIV
SUMMARY: DIV reads the signed XLEN‑bit dividend from rs1 and divisor from rs2, computes the signed quotient rounded toward zero, and writes the quotient to rd. For a non‑zero divisor without overflow the result satisfies dividend = divisor × quotient + remainder, while a zero divisor forces the quotient to all‑ones (‑1). If the dividend is the most‑negative XLEN‑bit value and the divisor is –1, signed‑division overflow occurs and DIV returns the dividend as the quotient and zero as the remainder. DIV belongs to the integer multiply/divide (M) extension and may be executed in any privilege mode that implements this extension.

INSTRUCTION: DIVU
SUMMARY: DIVU reads rs1 as an unsigned dividend and rs2 as an unsigned divisor, and writes the XLEN‑bit unsigned quotient to rd, rounding toward zero. If the divisor is non‑zero, the quotient Q satisfies dividend = divisor × Q + remainder, where the remainder is the unsigned value defined by the matching REMU operation. When the divisor is zero, the instruction produces the maximal unsigned value (all XLEN bits set). Any overflow condition triggers an illegal‑instruction exception with mcause set to the illegal‑instruction code, otherwise normal privilege checks apply.

INSTRUCTION: DIVUW
SUMMARY: DIVUW reads the lower 32 bits of rs1 and rs2 as unsigned operands, computes the unsigned quotient rounded toward zero, and writes the 32‑bit quotient sign‑extended to 64 bits into rd. If the lower 32 bits of rs2 are zero, the instruction returns the value 0xFFFFFFFF sign‑extended to 64 bits instead of trapping. The operation never generates an overflow exception. DIVUW is an unprivileged integer instruction that is available in all privilege modes.

INSTRUCTION: DIVW
SUMMARY: DIVW divides the low‑32‑bit signed value in rs1 by the low‑32‑bit signed value in rs2, writing the 32‑bit quotient to rd and sign‑extending it to 64 bits. If the divisor's low 32 bits are zero, the instruction writes the sign‑extended value –1 to rd. When the dividend's low 32 bits equal –2³¹ and the divisor's low 32 bits equal –1, the operation produces the sign‑extended –2³¹ quotient (the architectural quotient equals the dividend, remainder zero). DIVW is a regular integer arithmetic instruction that can be executed in any privilege mode without special access checks.

INSTRUCTION: FADD.Q
SUMMARY: FADD.Q adds the quad‑precision floating‑point value in source register rs1 to the quad‑precision value in source register rs2. The sum is rounded according to the rounding mode specified by the rm field and the rounded result is written to the destination register rd, which is designated by the rd field.

INSTRUCTION: FADD.S
SUMMARY: The FADD.S instruction reads the single‑precision floating‑point values from source registers rs1 and rs2, adds them, and produces a single‑precision result. The result is written to the floating‑point destination register rd; no extra privilege restrictions or explicit exception handling are defined beyond the usual floating‑point exception behavior.

INSTRUCTION: FCLASS.D
SUMMARY: FCLASS.D reads a double‑precision floating‑point operand from a source FP register and writes a classification bitmap to an integer destination register. The bitmap uses the ten categories (‑inf, ‑normal, ‑subnormal, ‑zero, +zero, +subnormal, +normal, +inf, signalling NaN, quiet NaN) identical to those defined for FCLASS.S. The instruction is unprivileged, available in any mode with FP support, and does not raise architectural exceptions; special‑value operands simply set the appropriate classification bits.

INSTRUCTION: FCLASS.H
SUMMARY: FCLASS.H reads a half‑precision floating‑point value from an f‑register and writes an integer classification code to an integer register. The classification encoding matches the definition used by the single‑precision classify instruction, but the operand is interpreted as half‑precision. The instruction is available in all privilege modes and imposes no special access controls. No architectural exceptions are generated by the operation.

INSTRUCTION: FCLASS.S
SUMMARY: The FCLASS.S instruction reads a single‑precision floating‑point value from rs1 and writes a 10‑bit class mask into the integer destination register rd, with each bit denoting a distinct floating‑point class. Only bits 0‑9 of rd are defined, all other bits are cleared, and exactly one of those class bits is set after execution. The instruction does not modify any floating‑point exception flags. It is unprivileged and may be executed in any privilege mode without additional access checks.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.BF16.S
SUMMARY: FCVT.BF16.S converts the 32‑bit floating‑point value in source floating‑point register rs1 to a bfloat16 value and writes the result to destination floating‑point register rd; it is defined only when the single‑precision floating‑point extension F is implemented. The conversion is rounded according to the current rounding‑mode (RM) field. The operation may raise floating‑point exceptions for overflow, underflow, inexact (when mantissa bits are discarded), or invalid (e.g., NaN input).

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.D.H
SUMMARY: FCVT.D.H converts a half‑precision floating‑point value in source register rs1 to double‑precision and writes the result to destination register rd. The conversion uses the rounding mode given by the rm field, following the defined floating‑point‑to‑floating‑point rounding rules. The instruction is only defined when the Zdinx extension is present; if Zdinx is not implemented, execution triggers an illegal‑instruction exception. No special privilege or access restrictions beyond the normal floating‑point register permissions apply.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.D.L
SUMMARY: FCVT.D.L reads a 64‑bit signed integer from integer register rs1 and writes the resulting double‑precision floating‑point value to floating‑point register rd. The conversion uses the rounding mode specified by the rm field. Input values outside the representable range are treated identically to the FCVT.int.S instruction (producing the defined overflow or NaN result). The instruction follows the standard privilege and access checks for floating‑point operations; no extra restrictions apply.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.D.LU
SUMMARY: FCVT.D.LU converts the unsigned integer held in integer register rs1 to a double‑precision floating‑point value and writes the result to floating‑point register rd, using the rounding mode encoded in the rm field. The instruction is defined only for RV64 implementations; on any other base ISA it triggers an illegal‑instruction exception (mcause = 2). Conversion follows the same valid‑input range limits as the single‑precision integer‑to‑float conversions, raising the same floating‑point exceptions for out‑of‑range inputs. No other architectural state is accessed or modified.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.D.Q
SUMMARY: FCVT.D.Q converts a quad-precision floating-point value to a double-precision floating-point result. The destination register (rd) receives the converted double-precision value, while the rm field of the encoding selects the rounding mode applied during the conversion. The instruction follows the same rounding-mode semantics as other double-precision floating-point-to-floating-point conversions and has no special privilege or exception conditions beyond those defined for standard floating-point operations.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.D.W
SUMMARY: FCVT.D.W reads a signed 32‑bit integer from integer register rs1 and writes the corresponding double‑precision floating‑point value to floating‑point register rd. The rm field is present but does not affect the result; the conversion is defined to be exact regardless of rounding mode. Execution never raises an illegal‑instruction or privilege‑level exception, and the only possible exception is an invalid‑conversion when the integer is out of the representable range for double‑precision, in which case the destination register and fflags are set as they would be by a FCVT.int.D (or FCVT.int.S) instruction. The instruction follows the normal access control for integer and floating‑point registers without imposing additional privilege restrictions.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.D.WU
SUMMARY: FCVT.D.WU reads an unsigned integer from integer register rs1 (32 bits on RV32, 32‑ or 64‑bits on RV64) and writes the converted double‑precision floating‑point result to floating‑point register rd. The conversion ignores the rounding‑mode (rm) field and yields an exact result for all defined inputs. For values outside the defined domain, the architectural response matches that of the FCVT.int.S instruction. No sign‑extension of the source integer is performed.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.H.D
SUMMARY: FCVT.H.D reads a double‑precision floating‑point value from the source FP register (rs1) and writes the converted half‑precision result to the destination FP register (rd), using the rounding mode specified by the rm field. The instruction is defined only when both the D and Zfhmin extensions are implemented; if either is absent the encoding is reserved and executing it raises an illegal‑instruction exception. No additional privilege checks are required beyond the presence of the required extensions. The rounding mode is taken directly from the rm field according to the standard floating‑point rounding‑mode semantics.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.H.L
SUMMARY: FCVT.H.L converts a signed 64‑bit integer in integer source register rs1 to a half‑precision floating‑point value written to floating‑point destination register rd, using the rounding mode specified by the rm field. The instruction is only defined when XLEN = 64; otherwise execution raises an illegal‑instruction exception (mcause = 2). It exhibits the same rounding, overflow, underflow, inexact, and invalid‑operation exception behavior as other integer‑to‑floating‑point conversions and is encoded as an OP‑FP instruction with fmt = H, rs2 = L, the appropriate funct5, and rm/rd fields; any deviation from this encoding is reserved.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.H.LU
SUMMARY: FCVT.H.LU converts an unsigned 64‑bit integer in integer register rs1 to a half‑precision floating‑point value written to floating‑point register rd, using the rounding mode specified by the rm field. The instruction is valid only on RV64 implementations; on RV32 it triggers an illegal‑instruction exception. The rs2 field must be encoded as L[U] to select the unsigned‑integer source, and the fmt field must be set to H to indicate a half‑precision result. No other architectural state is accessed.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.H.Q
SUMMARY: FCVT.H.Q reads a quad‑precision floating‑point register (source) and writes the converted half‑precision result to a half‑precision floating‑point register (destination). The rm field of the instruction selects the rounding mode used for the conversion. The operation follows the same architectural rules and exception behavior (invalid, overflow, underflow, inexact) as double‑precision floating‑point‑to‑floating‑point conversions and is available in all privilege modes without extra access restrictions.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.H.S
SUMMARY: FCVT.H.S is a floating‑point conversion instruction that is only architecturally available when the Zfinx extension is present, and it must be implemented if the Zfhmin extension is realized. No other privilege checks or exception behavior are defined beyond these extension‑dependency requirements.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.H.W
SUMMARY: FCVT.H.W reads a signed 32‑bit integer from integer register rs1, converts it to a half‑precision floating‑point value, and writes the result to floating‑point register rd. The rounding mode applied during the conversion is determined by the instruction's rm field.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.H.WU
SUMMARY: FCVT.H.WU reads a 32‑bit unsigned integer from integer register rs1, converts it to a half‑precision floating‑point value using the rounding mode specified in the rm field, and writes the result to floating‑point register rd. The conversion treats the source value as unsigned, ignoring any sign extension, and applies only to 32‑bit inputs (RV64 uses FCVT.H.LU for 64‑bit values). Execution requires the F‑extension to be enabled and a valid rounding mode; otherwise an illegal‑instruction exception is raised. Apart from the standard floating‑point conversion exceptions (e.g., overflow, invalid operation), no additional exceptions are generated.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.L.D
SUMMARY: The instruction reads a double‑precision floating‑point value from floating‑point register rs1 and writes the resulting signed 64‑bit integer to integer register rd. The rounding mode applied to the conversion is taken from the instruction's rm field. It uses the same valid‑input range and raises the same exceptions for invalid inputs (e.g., NaN, overflow, inexact) as the single‑precision floating‑point‑to‑integer conversion instruction. No special privilege or access restrictions beyond the normal floating‑point unit permissions are required.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.L.H
SUMMARY: FCVT.L.H reads a half‑precision floating‑point value from a source floating‑point register and writes the resulting signed 64‑bit integer to an integer destination register. The conversion uses the rounding‑mode field (rm) encoded in the instruction to round the source value before conversion. The instruction is defined only for RV64 implementations; executing it on an RV32 core triggers an illegal‑instruction exception.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.L.Q
