SUMMARY: FCVT.L.Q converts a quad‑precision floating‑point value in a source floating‑point register to a signed 64‑bit integer written to a destination integer register, using the processor's current rounding mode. The conversion follows the same semantics as double‑precision‑to‑integer instructions, including overflow handling and NaN propagation as defined by the active rounding mode. The instruction is only defined for RV64 implementations; on RV32 the encoding is reserved. No special privilege level is required beyond normal execution.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.L.S
SUMMARY: FCVT.L.S converts the floating‑point value in source register rs1 to a signed 64‑bit integer placed in integer register rd, using the rounding‑mode field rm. It raises the Invalid exception when the rounded value is not representable, clips the result to the nearest representable limit (‑2^63 or 2^63‑1), and yields ‑2^63 for inputs below ‑2^63, negative infinity, or NaN, while inputs above 2^63‑1 or positive infinity produce 2^63‑1. If the rounded result differs from the original operand and no Invalid exception is set, the Inexact exception flag is raised.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.LU.D
SUMMARY: FCVT.LU.D reads a double‑precision floating‑point value from source FP register rs1, converts it to an unsigned integer, and writes the result to integer destination register rd. The instruction is only defined on RV64 implementations (XLEN = 64) and is illegal on RV32. It accepts exactly the same valid inputs as FCVT.int.S, and any input that is invalid for FCVT.int.S triggers the same architectural exception. The conversion is rounded according to the rounding mode specified in the rm field.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.LU.H
SUMMARY: FCVT.LU.H converts a half‑precision floating‑point value from an fp register to an unsigned 64‑bit integer written to an integer register. It is defined solely for RV64 implementations, so execution on RV32 triggers an illegal‑instruction exception. The rounding‑mode field (rm) in the instruction encoding specifies the rounding mode used for the conversion.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.LU.Q
SUMMARY: FCVT.LU.Q reads a quad‑precision floating‑point value from a source floating‑point register, converts it to an unsigned integer, and writes the result to a destination integer register. The provided rules do not specify any privilege‑level restrictions for this instruction. No exceptional or trap behavior beyond the conversion itself is defined in the given semantics.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.LU.S
SUMMARY: FCVT.LU.S converts the single‑precision floating‑point value in source register rs1 to an unsigned 64‑bit integer written to destination register rd, using the rounding mode specified in the rm field and is defined only for RV64 implementations. If the rounded value is outside the unsigned‑64 range, the result is clipped to 0 for negative inputs or to 2^64−1 for positive inputs (including NaN and infinities) and the invalid exception flag is set. When the rounded input equals the minimum or maximum representable integer, the result is respectively 0 or 2^64−1. If rounding changes the operand value and no invalid exception occurs, the inexact exception flag is set, and the instruction produces a full 64‑bit result without sign‑extension.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.Q.D
SUMMARY: FCVT.Q.D reads a double‑precision floating‑point value from source register rs1 and writes the converted quad‑precision result to destination register rd. The conversion adheres to IEEE‑754 rules for double‑to‑quad operations, preserving NaNs, infinities, signed zeros and raising the appropriate floating‑point exception flags. It is a normal floating‑point instruction and may be executed in any privilege mode that permits floating‑point operations, with no extra access restrictions.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.Q.H
SUMMARY: FCVT.Q.H converts a half‑precision floating‑point value to a quad‑precision result, placing the output in the floating‑point register identified by rd. The rounding mode used for the conversion is selected by the rm field. No special privilege or access‑control restrictions are specified beyond the normal floating‑point register write permissions. The provided rules do not define any additional exception behavior.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.Q.L
SUMMARY: The instruction reads a signed 64‑bit integer from integer register rs1, converts it to a quad‑precision floating‑point value, and writes the result to floating‑point register rd. It is defined only for RV64 implementations; on any implementation lacking the RV64 base ISA it raises an illegal‑instruction exception. The conversion is performed exactly and the rounding‑mode field (rm) is ignored, so the rounding mode does not affect the result. No other side effects or privilege checks are specified.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.Q.LU
SUMMARY: FCVT.Q.LU reads a 64‑bit unsigned integer from an integer register (x) and writes the converted quad‑precision floating‑point value to a floating‑point register (f). The instruction is defined only on RV64 implementations; on RV32 or on cores lacking RV64 support its encoding is reserved and it cannot be executed. The conversion is exact, so the current floating‑point rounding mode does not affect the result and no inexact or overflow exceptions are raised.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.Q.W
SUMMARY: The FCVT.Q.W instruction reads a 32‑bit signed integer from an integer register (rs1) and writes the converted quad‑precision floating‑point value to a floating‑point register (fd). The conversion follows the same architectural rounding modes, overflow handling, and special‑case treatment defined for integer‑to‑double‑precision conversions, producing infinities or NaNs as required. It is a regular floating‑point instruction with no additional privilege checks beyond the standard floating‑point access permissions. Any exception conditions (e.g., overflow, invalid operation) are signaled according to the floating‑point exception flags.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.Q.WU
SUMMARY: FCVT.Q.WU converts a 32‑bit unsigned integer from an integer register to a quad‑precision floating‑point value written to a floating‑point register and is required in any RV32 or RV64 implementation that includes the floating‑point extension. The instruction uses the rm field to select the rounding mode and may raise the standard floating‑point exception flags (invalid operation, overflow, underflow, inexact) like its double‑precision counterparts. It follows the same privilege and access controls as other floating‑point instructions and is not guaranteed to produce an exact result, allowing rounding according to the chosen mode.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.S.BF16
SUMMARY: FCVT.S.BF16 reads a BF16 value from a source floating‑point register, converts it exactly to an IEEE‑754 single‑precision (FP32) value, and writes the result to a destination floating‑point register. For normal numbers or infinities the conversion shifts the BF16 bits left by 16 and zeros the low 16 bits; NaN inputs raise the invalid‑operation exception and the FP32 result is NaN‑boxed by setting the upper FLEN‑32 bits to 1. The instruction has no privilege restrictions and only affects floating‑point registers.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.S.D
SUMMARY: FCVT.S.D reads a double‑precision floating‑point value from a source floating‑point register and writes the converted single‑precision result to a destination floating‑point register. The conversion is rounded according to the rounding‑mode field (RM) specified in the instruction.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.S.H
SUMMARY: FCVT.S.H reads a half‑precision floating‑point value from source register rs1 and writes the converted single‑precision result to destination register rd. The conversion uses the rounding mode specified in the rm field. The instruction is a regular floating‑point operation with no special privilege or access‑control requirements, so it may be executed in any privilege mode. Standard floating‑point exception flags are updated according to the conversion outcome.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.S.LU
SUMMARY: FCVT.S.LU reads an unsigned integer from integer register rs1 and writes the converted single‑precision floating‑point value to floating‑point register rd. The conversion is rounded according to the rounding mode given in the rm field, following the standard floating‑point conversion rounding rules. If the rounded result cannot be represented, it is clipped to the nearest representable single‑precision value and the invalid‑operation exception flag is set; otherwise, when the rounded result differs from the original integer and no invalid‑operation occurs, the inexact‑operation flag is set.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.S.Q
SUMMARY: FCVT.S.Q reads a quad‑precision floating‑point value from a source floating‑point register and writes the converted single‑precision result to a destination floating‑point register. The instruction belongs to the standard floating‑point ISA and can be executed in any privilege mode. No extra operand fields are needed beyond the source and destination registers. No special access‑control or exception behavior is defined beyond the conversion operation.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.S.WU
SUMMARY: FCVT.S.WU reads an unsigned integer from integer register rs1 and writes the resulting single‑precision floating‑point value to floating‑point register rd, using the rounding mode encoded in the rm field. The conversion is rounded according to rm and then clipped to the nearest representable single‑precision value; if the rounded result cannot be represented, the Invalid exception flag is set. If the rounded result differs from the original operand and Invalid is not set, the Inexact exception flag is raised. No special privilege or access‑control restrictions are imposed beyond the standard floating‑point enable checks.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.W.D
SUMMARY: FCVT.W.D reads a double‑precision floating‑point value from source floating‑point register rs1, converts it to a signed 32‑bit integer using the rounding mode encoded in the rm field, and writes the result to integer register rd. On RV64 implementations the 32‑bit result is sign‑extended to 64 bits before being written. The instruction raises the same floating‑point exception flags that would be raised by an equivalent FCVTMOD.W.D conversion. No special privilege or access‑control restrictions are imposed beyond those of ordinary integer‑register write operations.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.W.H
SUMMARY: FCVT.W.H reads a half‑precision floating‑point value from source floating‑point register rs1, converts it to a signed 32‑bit integer, and writes the result to integer register rd. The rounding mode applied during the conversion is taken from the instruction's rm field. No additional privilege checks or exception behavior are defined beyond the conversion and rounding semantics.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.W.Q
SUMMARY: FCVT.W.Q reads a quad‑precision floating‑point value from an f‑register and writes the converted signed 32‑bit integer to an x‑register. The instruction belongs to the floating‑point conversion class and imposes no additional privilege or access‑control requirements beyond normal execution rights. Its operation is defined solely by the source floating‑point register and the destination integer register fields. No explicit exception behavior is specified in the provided rule.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.W.S
SUMMARY: FCVT.W.S converts the single‑precision floating‑point value in source FP register rs1 to a signed 32‑bit integer placed in integer register rd, using the rounding mode given by the rm field; if XLEN > 32 the result is sign‑extended to the full register width. Values that round outside the signed‑32 range are clipped to –2³¹ for negative overflow (including –∞) or to 2³¹‑1 for positive overflow, NaN, or +∞, and the invalid exception flag is set, otherwise the exact integer is written. The inexact exception flag is raised when rounding changes the value and no invalid exception occurs. The instruction has no special privilege requirements and follows normal integer‑register write semantics.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.WU.D
SUMMARY: The instruction reads a double‑precision floating‑point value from source FP register rs1, rounds it according to the rm field, converts it to an unsigned 32‑bit integer, and writes the result to integer register rd. In RV64 implementations the 32‑bit result is sign‑extended to 64 bits before being stored. The valid input range matches that of FCVT.int.S, and any out‑of‑range operand causes the same exception or undefined‑result behavior as FCVT.int.S. The operation is not privileged and may be executed in any privilege mode.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.WU.H
SUMMARY: The instruction reads a half‑precision floating‑point value from source FP register rs1, converts it to an unsigned 32‑bit integer using the rounding mode specified by the rm field, and writes the result to integer register rd. It operates under the normal privilege model with no additional access restrictions, and any exception conditions follow the standard floating‑point conversion semantics.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.WU.S
SUMMARY: FCVT.WU.S reads a floating‑point value from source fp register rs1, converts it to an unsigned 32‑bit integer, and writes the result to integer register rd. The conversion is rounded according to the rm rounding‑mode field, and if the rounded value lies outside the range [0, 2³²‑1] it is clipped to the nearest bound and the invalid exception flag is set. When the rounded result differs from the original operand and no invalid exception occurs, the inexact exception flag is raised; negative inputs (including –∞) produce 0, while +∞ or NaN produce 2³²‑1.

INSTRUCTION: FCVTMOD.W.D
SUMMARY: FCVTMOD.W.D converts a double‑precision floating‑point value in rs1 to a signed 32‑bit integer, writes the sign‑extended result to integer register rd, and always rounds toward zero. The instruction requires rs2 = 8 and the rounding‑mode field set to RTZ; any other rm value makes the encoding reserved. Conversions of ±∞ or NaN produce an integer zero, and the operation raises the same floating‑point exception flags as the standard FCVT.W.D for the given operand. No special privilege or access checks beyond those of ordinary floating‑point instructions apply.

INSTRUCTION: FDIV.Q
SUMMARY: FDIV.Q divides the quad‑precision floating‑point value in source register rs1 by the quad‑precision value in source register rs2, applying the rounding mode encoded in the RM field, and writes the quad‑precision result to destination register rd. The instruction accesses the floating‑point register file and follows the normal floating‑point execution model. No explicit privilege checks or exception conditions are specified beyond the standard floating‑point behavior.

INSTRUCTION: FDIV.S
SUMMARY: FDIV.S writes the single‑precision division result to the floating‑point register identified by rd, using the value in rs1 as the dividend and the value in rs2 as the divisor. An optional rm field selects the rounding mode, which governs how the division result is rounded according to the F‑extension rounding‑mode semantics.

INSTRUCTION: FENCE
SUMMARY: The rs1 and rd fields in a FENCE are reserved for finer‑grain fence extensions; base implementations ignore them and software must write zero. The fence‑mode (fm) field together with the predecessor and successor sets define ordering, and any configuration using a reserved fm or set value is treated by a base implementation as a standard fence with fm cleared to 0000. Standard software must use only non‑reserved fm, predecessor, and successor encodings. No special privilege checks apply beyond the normal execution rights for the instruction.

INSTRUCTION: FENCE.I
SUMMARY: The FENCE.I instruction does not read or write any architectural registers; its funct12, rs1, and rd fields are reserved and are ignored by base implementations. Consequently, the instruction has no operand registers and its execution proceeds without additional access‑control checks or defined exceptions.

INSTRUCTION: FEQ.S
SUMMARY: FEQ.S reads two single‑precision floating‑point source registers (rs1, rs2) and writes a 32‑bit integer result to integer register rd. It writes 1 to rd when the operands are equal and neither is NaN; otherwise it writes 0. The instruction is executable in any privilege mode that permits floating‑point operations and does not modify any floating‑point destination registers. It sets the floating‑point invalid‑operation flag only if a signaling NaN appears in either source, and any NaN (signaling or quiet) forces the result to 0.

INSTRUCTION: FLD
SUMMARY: FLD reads a 64‑bit double‑precision floating‑point value from the effective memory address and writes the unmodified bit pattern—including the payload of non‑canonical NaNs—into the destination floating‑point register (size FLEN). The effective address undergoes pointer‑masking according to the current privilege‑mode settings (including MPRV, SPVP, and MXR) and is not exempt from CSR‑access exemptions. Misaligned accesses whose low three bits are non‑zero are serviced by combining the two aligned 8‑byte words without raising an exception, but if the misalignment exceeds the implementation's supported limits a load‑address‑misaligned exception (mcause = 5) is raised with mtval set to the faulting address; otherwise, when XLEN < 64 or the address is unaligned the load may be performed non‑atomically.

INSTRUCTION: FLE.S
SUMMARY: The FLE.S instruction compares the single‑precision floating‑point values in source registers rs1 and rs2 and writes an integer to destination register rd: 1 if rs1 ≤ rs2, otherwise 0. If either operand is a NaN, the result written to rd is forced to 0 and the invalid‑operation exception flag is set (quiet NaNs also trigger the flag). The instruction reads rs1 and rs2 as floating‑point registers and writes rd as an integer register, and it is subject only to the normal privilege checks for floating‑point operations.

INSTRUCTION: FLEQ.D
SUMMARY: FLEQ.D compares two double‑precision floating‑point registers (rs1 and rs2) and writes a 0/1 integer result to destination register rd, indicating whether rs1 ≤ rs2. The instruction is defined only when the D extension is implemented and follows the normal floating‑point privilege and access rules applicable to all FP instructions. Its comparison behavior is identical to that of FLE.D. If either source operand is a quiet NaN, the invalid‑operation exception flag is not set.

INSTRUCTION: FLEQ.H
SUMMARY: FLEQ.H reads two half‑precision floating‑point registers (rs1, rs2) and writes a 0/1 integer result to the integer destination register rd, indicating whether rs1 ≤ rs2. Its comparison behavior is defined identically to the FLE.H instruction, including treatment of signed zeros and rounding‑mode‑independent outcomes. When either source operand is a quiet NaN, the invalid‑operation exception flag is not set, allowing the instruction to complete without raising an exception. The instruction belongs to the standard floating‑point extension and may be executed in any privilege mode that permits floating‑point operations, subject to the usual f‑status and privilege checks.

INSTRUCTION: FLEQ.Q
SUMMARY: FLEQ.Q compares the two quad‑precision floating‑point source registers (rs1, rs2) and writes a 1‑or‑0 Boolean result to the integer destination register (rd), producing exactly the same outcome as the single‑precision FLE.S instruction for the same operand values. The instruction operates under the normal floating‑point instruction privilege and accesses the standard integer and floating‑point register files. When either operand is a quiet NaN, FLEQ.Q does not raise the invalid‑operation exception flag. No other architectural state is modified.

INSTRUCTION: FLEQ.S
SUMMARY: The FLEQ.S instruction compares two single‑precision floating‑point registers and writes a Boolean "less‑than‑or‑equal" result to the destination register. For any operand pair that is not a quiet NaN, its comparison outcome and the floating‑point exception flags are identical to those of the FLE.S instruction. When either source operand is a quiet NaN, the instruction performs the comparison without setting the invalid‑operation exception flag. The instruction observes the normal floating‑point privilege, rounding‑mode, and exception‑flag controls.

INSTRUCTION: FLH
SUMMARY: The FLH instruction loads a 16‑bit half‑precision floating‑point value from memory at the address formed by adding an immediate offset to a base integer register and writes it into a destination floating‑point register, converting the half‑precision encoding to the processor's FLEN format. The loaded bits are transferred unchanged, non‑canonical NaN payloads are preserved, and the result is NaN‑boxed by placing the halfword in the low bits and filling the upper bits with the NaN‑box pattern. The instruction requires normal load privileges for the effective address, and any transformed‑for‑trap version that contains a non‑zero immediate offset triggers an illegal‑instruction exception (mcause = 2).

INSTRUCTION: FLI.D
SUMMARY: The FLI.D instruction, defined only when the D extension is present, writes a double‑precision floating‑point constant into the destination floating‑point register rd. The constant for entry 1 (the smallest positive normal double) is defined to differ numerically from the single‑precision constant loaded by the analogous FLI.S entry. Execution of FLI.D does not modify any floating‑point exception flags. No other architectural state is affected.

INSTRUCTION: FLI.H
SUMMARY: The FLI.H instruction is only defined when the Zfh or Zvfh extension is implemented. It uses the rs1 field as an index (0‑31) into a table of half‑precision constants and writes the selected constant into the floating‑point destination register rd. Entry 1 may return the IEEE‑754 half‑precision minimum positive normal (0x0400) unless that value is also produced by the corresponding FLI.S entry, in which case both reads yield the same value; entry 1 is numerically larger than entries 2 and 3 because those are subnormal, and entry 29 (identical to entry 30) produces the half‑precision positive‑infinity pattern 0x7C00. Execution never raises any floating‑point exception flags.

INSTRUCTION: FLI.Q
SUMMARY: The FLI.Q instruction writes a quad‑precision floating‑point constant into the destination floating‑point register rd, analogous to the single‑precision FLI.S operation but using the quad format. The immediate field value 1 encodes the minimum positive normal quad‑precision number, which differs from the value 1 encoding in other floating‑point formats. No architectural privilege checks or special access controls are defined for this instruction. Execution of FLI.Q does not raise or set any floating‑point exception flags.

INSTRUCTION: FLI.S
SUMMARY: The FLI.S instruction writes a single‑precision constant into the destination floating‑point register rd, the constant being selected by the integer source register rs1 according to a fixed mapping (e.g., –1.0 for 0, the smallest positive normal for 1, powers of two from 2⁻⁸ up to 2¹⁶, fractional values with limited mantissa precision, +∞ for 30, and a canonical NaN for 31). For rs1 values 8‑22 the loaded value may have only the two most‑significant mantissa bits non‑zero, while all other specified constants have a zero mantissa. Execution of FLI.S never sets any floating‑point exception flags. The instruction has no side effects beyond writing rd and is not privileged.

INSTRUCTION: FLQ
SUMMARY: FLQ loads a 128‑bit floating‑point value, preserving all bits (including non‑canonical NaN payloads), into a floating‑point register whose width is FLEN. The instruction must be encoded with the mew bit cleared; any encoding that sets mew=1 for a 128‑bit load is reserved, and a transformed instruction whose funct3, rd, or opcode differ from the trapping FLQ causes an illegal‑instruction exception. When MXR is active in the effective privilege mode, address masking is not applied to the load address. If a misaligned FLQ requires aligned sub‑accesses that would raise a fault (page‑fault, access‑fault, or illegal‑instruction), the entire operation faults with the same mcause and the destination register is not updated.

INSTRUCTION: FLT.S
SUMMARY: FLT.S compares the single‑precision floating‑point values in source registers rs1 and rs2, treating the 32‑bit contents as IEEE‑754 binary32 numbers. It writes the integer result 1 to destination integer register rd if rs1 < rs2, and 0 otherwise, including when either operand is NaN. If a NaN is encountered, the floating‑point invalid‑operation exception flag is set. The instruction is executable in any privilege mode that permits standard floating‑point operations.

INSTRUCTION: FLTQ.D
SUMMARY: FLTQ.D compares two double‑precision floating‑point registers and writes a 0/1 boolean result to an integer destination register. It uses the standard source fields (fs1, fs2) and integer destination field (rd) of the R‑type format. The instruction is available in any privilege mode that permits floating‑point operations when the F extension is enabled. It does not set the invalid‑operation exception flag for quiet NaN inputs, but otherwise raises the flag under the same conditions as FLT.D.

INSTRUCTION: FLTQ.H
SUMMARY: FLTQ.H reads two source floating‑point registers, interprets their contents as half‑precision values, and writes a destination integer register with the result of the "less‑than" comparison. The comparison result is identical to that produced by FLT.H for the same operands. Exception flags are raised exactly as in FLT.H except that a quiet NaN in either source does not set the invalid‑operation flag. The instruction is subject to the same privilege and access controls as other floating‑point compare operations.

INSTRUCTION: FLTQ.Q
SUMMARY: FLTQ.Q compares two 128‑bit floating‑point registers and writes a 0/1 integer result to the destination integer register indicating whether the first operand is less than the second. It uses the standard FP source fields (rs1, rs2) and an integer destination field (rd) with no additional encoding bits. The instruction is executable in any privilege mode and requires no special access control. It never raises the invalid‑operation exception when either source operand is a quiet NaN; other FP exceptions follow the usual handling rules.

INSTRUCTION: FLTQ.S
SUMMARY: FLTQ.S reads two floating‑point registers (rs1, rs2) and writes a 0/1 integer result to rd based on the less‑than comparison defined for FLT.S. The instruction is executable in all privilege modes and follows the usual floating‑point access controls. It never sets the invalid‑operation exception flag when either source operand is a quiet NaN.

INSTRUCTION: FLW
SUMMARY: FLW computes its effective address by adding a sign‑extended 12‑bit byte offset to the base address in integer register rs1, applies the architectural pointer‑masking transformation, and performs a 32‑bit memory access. The loaded single‑precision value is written unchanged, preserving all bits (including non‑canonical NaN payloads), into the destination floating‑point register rd, which is FLEN‑wide. Misaligned accesses are allowed but are not required to be atomic; the result may be a mixture of old and new data and is defined as the composition of masked aligned sub‑accesses. For synchronous exceptions other than misaligned‑address, load‑access‑fault, or load‑page‑fault, the trap‑instruction register mtinst must not contain a transformed FLW encoding, and the instruction ignores the mew bit.

INSTRUCTION: FMAX.S
SUMMARY: FMAX.S reads two single‑precision floating‑point registers rs1 and rs2 and writes the larger numeric value to the destination floating‑point register rd. Positive zero is selected over negative zero; if both operands are NaNs the canonical NaN is produced, while a single NaN yields the non‑NaN operand. The instruction follows the IEEE 754‑2019 maximum operation semantics for single‑precision values. If any operand is a signaling NaN, the invalid‑operation exception flag is set, and the instruction executes only when the floating‑point unit is enabled (no special privilege required).

INSTRUCTION: FMAXM.D
SUMMARY: FMAXM.D reads two source floating‑point registers (rs1, rs2) containing double‑precision values and writes the result to a destination floating‑point register (rd). It returns the larger operand in the usual floating‑point maximum sense, but if either operand is a NaN the canonical NaN is produced. The instruction belongs to the double‑precision floating‑point extension and can be executed in any privilege mode that has the FP unit enabled, with no additional access restrictions. Encountering a NaN may raise the invalid‑operation exception flag according to the standard floating‑point exception handling rules.

INSTRUCTION: FMAXM.H
SUMMARY: The FMAXM.H instruction reads two half‑precision floating‑point values from source registers rs1 and rs2 and writes the result to destination register rd. If either source contains a NaN, rd receives the canonical NaN; otherwise rd is set to the greater of the two operands according to the IEEE‑754‑2019 maximum operation. The instruction is defined only when the F (or Zfh) extension is implemented and the floating‑point state (FS field in mstatus) permits floating‑point execution. No additional architectural exceptions are raised beyond the NaN handling described.

INSTRUCTION: FMAXM.Q
SUMMARY: The FMAXM.Q instruction reads two quad‑precision floating‑point registers and writes the result to a destination floating‑point register. If neither source operand is NaN, the result is the same as the IEEE‑754‑2019 maximum operation (identical to FMAX.Q); if either operand is NaN, the canonical NaN is written to the destination. Execution is governed by the standard floating‑point enable checks and is permitted in any privilege mode that allows floating‑point instructions.

INSTRUCTION: FMAXM.S
SUMMARY: FMAXM.S reads two single‑precision floating‑point source registers and writes the result to a destination floating‑point register. It returns the operand with the greater IEEE‑754‑2019 numeric value, correctly handling signed zeros and infinities. If either operand is a NaN, the instruction produces the canonical NaN as the result. The instruction is available in all privilege modes and does not impose special access controls.

INSTRUCTION: FMIN.Q
SUMMARY: The FMIN.Q instruction reads two quad‑precision floating‑point source registers and writes the quad‑precision minimum of those operands to a floating‑point destination register. No other architectural state is accessed. The operation is permitted in any privilege mode and does not generate special architectural exceptions beyond the normal floating‑point exception handling.

INSTRUCTION: FMIN.S
SUMMARY: FMIN.S reads two single‑precision floating‑point values from source registers rs1 and rs2 and writes the computed minimum to destination register rd. The result follows the IEEE 754‑2019 minimum operation: the numerically smaller operand is returned, with ‑0.0 preferred over +0.0 when values compare equal; if exactly one operand is NaN the non‑NaN value is returned, and if both are NaNs a NaN result is produced according to the IEEE rule (without forcing the canonical NaN). Any signaling NaN input raises the invalid‑operation exception flag, even when the final result is not a NaN. The instruction is unrestricted by privilege level beyond the usual permission to execute floating‑point operations.

INSTRUCTION: FMINM.D
SUMMARY: FMINM.D reads two double‑precision floating‑point source registers and writes the result to a floating‑point destination register. It computes the IEEE‑754‑2019 minimum of the two source values. If either source operand is a NaN, the result is the canonical NaN. The instruction follows the standard floating‑point privilege and access controls with no additional restrictions.

INSTRUCTION: FMINM.Q
SUMMARY: The FMINM.Q instruction reads two quad‑precision floating‑point registers (rs1, rs2) and writes the result to a destination floating‑point register (rd). It is defined only when the floating‑point extension is present and is executable in any privilege mode that permits FP instructions. The operation returns the IEEE‑754‑2019 minimum of the two operands; if either operand is a NaN the result is the canonical NaN, otherwise the smaller operand is selected using the signed‑zero ordering identical to FMIN.S. No architectural side effects occur beyond the normal floating‑point exception flags.

INSTRUCTION: FMINM.S
SUMMARY: The FMINM.S instruction reads two single‑precision floating‑point registers, computes the IEEE‑754‑2019 minimum of the operands, and writes the result to a destination floating‑point register. If either source operand is a NaN, the result is the canonical NaN as defined by the IEEE‑754 standard. The instruction shares the encoding of FMIN.S but is selected when bit 13 of the instruction word is set to 1, and it is executable in all privilege modes without additional access restrictions.

INSTRUCTION: FMSUB.S
SUMMARY: The instruction is a R4‑type floating‑point operation that reads the source registers rs1, rs2, and rs3 and writes the fused‑multiply‑subtract result to destination register rd, with the rounding mode selected by the rm field. If it is not encoded in the R4‑type format, an illegal‑instruction exception is raised. The hardware computes (rs1 × rs2) – rs3 as a single fused step and rounds the result according to rm. An invalid‑operation exception flag is set when one multiplicand is Infinity and the other is zero, regardless of rs3's value (including a quiet NaN).

INSTRUCTION: FMUL.Q
SUMMARY: FMUL.Q reads two quad‑precision floating‑point registers as source operands and writes the quad‑precision product to a destination floating‑point register. The rounding mode applied to the multiplication is taken from the instruction's rm field. The operation uses the same architectural semantics, rounding behavior, and floating‑point exception handling as the double‑precision FMUL instruction, but on quad‑precision values. No special privilege or access‑control restrictions beyond normal floating‑point register access are imposed.

INSTRUCTION: FMUL.S
SUMMARY: FMUL.S multiplies the single‑precision floating‑point values in source registers rs1 and rs2, rounds the exact product according to the rounding mode specified by the rm field, and writes the result to destination register rd. The rm field selects one of the standard IEEE‑754 rounding modes for the operation. The instruction is executable in any privilege mode that permits floating‑point instructions, with no additional access checks. No architectural exceptions are defined beyond the normal floating‑point exception flags that may be set by the rounding result.

INSTRUCTION: FMV.D.X
SUMMARY: FMV.D.X is defined only on implementations with XLEN >= 64; it is unavailable on smaller XLEN cores. The instruction copies the full 64‑bit value from integer source register rs1 into destination floating‑point register rd, interpreting the bits as an IEEE‑754 double‑precision value. All bits are transferred verbatim, preserving even non‑canonical NaN payloads. No special privilege level is required beyond normal user‑mode access.

INSTRUCTION: FMV.H.X
SUMMARY: FMV.H.X reads the lower 16 bits of an integer source register (rs1), interprets them as an IEEE‑754‑2008 half‑precision floating‑point value, and writes that value into the lower half of a floating‑point destination register (rd) using a NaN‑boxed representation. The exact bit pattern of the source half‑precision value, including any non‑canonical NaN payload, is preserved unchanged in the NaN‑boxed result. No special privilege or access‑control checks are required beyond the normal rights to read integer registers and write floating‑point registers. Bits of rd outside the lower 16 are set according to the NaN‑boxing convention.

INSTRUCTION: FMV.W.X
SUMMARY: FMV.W.X moves the low 32 bits of integer register rs1 into floating‑point register rd, treating the bit pattern as an IEEE‑754‑2008 single‑precision value and leaving the pattern unchanged, including any non‑canonical NaN payloads. The instruction encodes an rs2 field that must not be 1; any encoding with rs2 = 1 triggers an illegal‑instruction exception. No privilege‑level restrictions are imposed beyond the standard architectural checks for register access.

INSTRUCTION: FMV.X.D
SUMMARY: FMV.X.D is defined only on implementations where XLEN ≥ 64 and the Zdinx extension is present. It copies the unmodified 64‑bit double‑precision bit pattern from floating‑point source register rs1 into integer destination register rd, preserving all NaN payloads. The instruction raises no floating‑point exceptions and does not affect any floating‑point control or status registers.

INSTRUCTION: FMV.X.H
SUMMARY: FMV.X.H reads a half‑precision floating‑point value from source floating‑point register rs1 and writes it to integer register rd, placing the 16‑bit payload in rd's low half and sign‑extending the floating‑point sign bit into the upper XLEN‑16 bits. The operation preserves the exact bit pattern of the half‑precision payload, including any non‑canonical NaN encoding. Execution requires the Zfh extension; if the processor lacks Zfh, the instruction raises an illegal‑instruction exception. No other architectural state is altered and the instruction is permitted in all privilege modes.

INSTRUCTION: FMV.X.W
SUMMARY: The FMV.X.W instruction moves the raw 32‑bit IEEE‑754 single‑precision encoding from floating‑point register rs1 into the lower half of integer register rd, leaving the bit pattern unchanged, including any non‑canonical NaN payloads. On RV64 implementations the upper 32 bits of rd are filled with a sign‑extension of the source floating‑point value's sign bit; on RV32 the register width limits the result to the transferred 32 bits. The operation is permitted in all privilege modes and does not raise any architectural exceptions. No other side effects occur.

INSTRUCTION: FMVH.X.Q
SUMMARY: FMVH.X.Q moves the upper 64 bits of a quad‑precision floating‑point register into an integer register, enabling the high half of a 128‑bit value to be captured. It is intended to be used together with FMV.X.D, which transfers the lower 64 bits, so the full 128‑bit value can be assembled across two integer registers. The instruction is permitted in all privilege modes and follows the standard read/write permissions for the source FP register and destination integer register. No special exception conditions are defined for FMVH.X.Q.

INSTRUCTION: FMVP.D.X
SUMMARY: FMVP.D.X reads the low‑order 32 bits of integer register rs1 and the high‑order 32 bits of integer register rs2, concatenates them to form a 64‑bit double‑precision value, and writes that value to floating‑point register rd, overwriting its previous contents. The operation requires normal read access to the two integer registers and write access to the destination floating‑point register, and it is not a privileged instruction. No exception behavior is specified by the given rules.

INSTRUCTION: FMVP.Q.X
SUMMARY: FMVP.Q.X is a floating‑point move‑pair instruction defined only on RV64 implementations that include the Q extension. It reads the lower 64 bits of a double‑precision value from integer register rs1 and the upper 64 bits from integer register rs2, concatenates them, and writes the assembled double‑precision result to floating‑point register rd. Executing the instruction without the Q extension causes an illegal‑instruction exception.

INSTRUCTION: FNMADD.S
SUMMARY: FNMADD.S is a single‑precision floating‑point instruction that writes to destination register rd the value –(rs1 × rs2) – rs3, using the rounding mode selected by the rm field in the encoding. It reads three source floating‑point registers (rs1, rs2, rs3) and a 3‑bit rm field that determines the IEEE‑754 rounding mode. Execution is allowed in any privilege mode that enables the F extension (i.e., when the floating‑point unit is present and accessible). The instruction sets the invalid‑operation exception flag when one multiplicand is ±∞ and the other is zero, even if rs3 is a quiet NaN.

INSTRUCTION: FNMSUB.S
SUMMARY: FNMSUB.S reads floating‑point registers rs1, rs2 and rs3, computes –(rs1 × rs2) + rs3, and writes the rounded result to floating‑point register rd. The rounding mode is taken from the instruction's rm field. The operation raises the invalid‑operation exception when one multiplicand is infinity and the other zero, even if rs3 is a quiet NaN. No special privilege or access restrictions beyond normal floating‑point register use apply.

INSTRUCTION: FROUND.D
SUMMARY: FROUND.D is defined only when the D extension is implemented. It reads a double‑precision value from floating‑point register rs1, rounds it to an integer according to the rm rounding‑mode field, and writes the integral result, encoded as a double‑precision floating‑point number, into floating‑point register rd. If rs1 holds +0, −0, +∞, or −∞ the value is copied to rd unchanged. A signaling NaN in rs1 raises the invalid‑operation exception flag and no other exception flags are set.

INSTRUCTION: FROUND.H
SUMMARY: The FROUND.H instruction reads a half‑precision floating‑point value from source register rs1, rounds it to an integer using the rounding mode indicated by the rm field, and writes the resulting integer as a half‑precision float into destination register rd. If rs1 contains +0, –0, or an infinity, that value is copied to rd unchanged. If rs1 holds a signaling NaN, the invalid‑operation exception flag is set and no other floating‑point exception flags are raised.

INSTRUCTION: FROUND.Q
SUMMARY: FROUND.Q reads a quad‑precision floating‑point value from source FP register rs1, rounds it to an integer using the rounding mode given by the rm field, and writes the integer result as a quad‑precision float to destination FP register rd. If the source operand is ±0 or ±∞, that value is copied to rd unchanged. When the source is a signaling NaN, only the invalid‑operation exception flag is set and no other exception flags are raised. The instruction implements the IEEE‑754‑2019 roundToIntegral (non‑exact) operation and is available without special privilege restrictions.

INSTRUCTION: FROUND.S
SUMMARY: FROUND.S reads a single‑precision floating‑point value from source register rs1, rounds it to an integer using the rounding mode encoded in the rm field, and writes the result to destination register rd as a single‑precision floating‑point number. Zero and infinite inputs are transferred to rd unchanged. If rs1 holds a signaling NaN, the instruction raises the invalid‑operation exception flag and suppresses all other exception flags. The instruction is available in all privilege modes provided the floating‑point unit is enabled.

INSTRUCTION: FROUNDNX.D
SUMMARY: The FROUNDNX.D instruction reads a double‑precision value from floating‑point source register rs1, rounds it to an integral value using the rounding mode encoded in the rm field, and writes the result to floating‑point destination register rd as a double‑precision value. If rs1 holds +0, –0, or an infinity, that exact value is copied to rd unchanged. A signaling NaN in rs1 raises the invalid‑operation exception, and any non‑NaN operand whose rounded result differs from the original raises the inexact exception; the rounding follows the IEEE‑754‑2019 roundToIntegralExact operation. The instruction follows the standard privilege and access controls applied to all floating‑point operations, with no additional restrictions.

INSTRUCTION: FROUNDNX.Q
SUMMARY: FROUNDNX.Q reads a quad‑precision floating‑point value from an FP source register, rounds it to an integral value using the IEEE‑754‑2019 roundToIntegralExact operation, and writes the result to the destination FP register. If the operand is zero or infinite the value is transferred unchanged; otherwise the inexact exception flag is set when the rounded result differs from the original non‑NaN operand. A signaling NaN input raises the invalid‑operation exception and suppresses all other exception flags. The instruction has no special privilege restrictions and updates the standard floating‑point status‑flags fields.

INSTRUCTION: FROUNDNX.S
SUMMARY: FROUNDNX.S reads a single‑precision floating‑point value from rs1, rounds it to an integral value using the rounding mode given in the rm field, and writes the resulting single‑precision floating‑point integer to rd. Zero and infinite inputs are copied unchanged, while a signaling NaN causes only the invalid‑operation exception flag to be set. For all other inputs the inexact exception is raised if the rounded result differs from the original value and not raised otherwise, conforming to the IEEE 754‑2019 roundToIntegralExact operation.

INSTRUCTION: FSD
SUMMARY: FSD stores a double‑precision floating‑point value from source register rs2 to the memory address computed as rs1 plus a zero‑extended offset multiplied by 8. It is defined only in implementations that provide the RV32DC or RV64DC extensions and requires the Zfinx extension, otherwise an illegal‑instruction exception is raised. The access is subject to PMP checks, must have write permission, and follows pointer‑masking rules; an unaligned address with masking disabled raises a store‑address‑misaligned exception, and a misaligned or XLEN‑<64 store is not guaranteed to be atomic. Any PMP or page‑fault violation generates the appropriate load/store access‑fault exception, and the stored double‑precision value is written unchanged, preserving NaN payloads.

INSTRUCTION: FSGNJ.D
SUMMARY: FSGNJ.D reads a double‑precision floating‑point value from source register rs1 and writes it to destination register rd, effectively performing a register‑to‑register move. The instruction belongs to the floating‑point group and uses the standard rd, rs1, rs2 fields (rs2 may be ignored for the move operation). It is an unprivileged instruction executable in any privilege mode. No special access‑control checks or exception conditions are defined for this use.

INSTRUCTION: FSGNJ.H
SUMMARY: FSGNJ.H is a half‑precision floating‑point instruction that reads two source FP registers (rs1, rs2) and writes the result to a destination FP register (rd). It forms a half‑precision value whose magnitude comes from rs1 and whose sign bit is taken from rs2, exactly matching the single‑precision sign‑inject behavior for half‑precision data. The instruction is executable in any privilege mode that allows floating‑point operations and is illegal if the F extension is not present. No architectural state other than rd (and the usual floating‑point exception flags) is affected.

INSTRUCTION: FSGNJ.Q
SUMMARY: The instruction reads two quad‑precision floating‑point registers (rs1 and rs2) and writes a quad‑precision result to rd. rd's exponent and fraction fields are copied unchanged from rs1, while its sign field is taken from rs2, thereby applying rs2's sign bit to rs1's magnitude. Execution requires the floating‑point unit to be enabled and is allowed in any privilege mode, raising an illegal‑instruction trap if FP support is not present. No special architectural exceptions are defined beyond the ordinary floating‑point exception flags.

INSTRUCTION: FSGNJ.S
SUMMARY: FSGNJ.S reads two single‑precision floating‑point registers (rs1 and rs2) and writes a single‑precision result to rd. The result's exponent and fraction fields are taken directly from rs1 while its sign bit is taken from rs2; NaN operands are forwarded unchanged, preserving their payload bits. The instruction never sets any floating‑point exception flags and is executable in any privilege mode.

INSTRUCTION: FSGNJN.D
SUMMARY: FSGNJN.D reads a double‑precision value from source register rs1 and a double‑precision value from rs2, and writes the result to destination register rd. The operation copies the magnitude of rs1 and replaces its sign bit with the logical negation of the sign bit of rs2, using the double‑precision sign‑injection encoding (opcode/funct fields for the D‑type variant). The instruction belongs to the standard floating‑point extension and can be executed in any privilege mode without additional access restrictions. No special exception behavior is defined beyond the usual floating‑point exception handling for invalid or NaN inputs.

INSTRUCTION: FSGNJN.H
SUMMARY: FSGNJN.H reads two half‑precision floating‑point registers (rs1 and rs2) and writes a half‑precision result to rd whose magnitude is taken from rs1 and whose sign bit is the logical complement of rs2's sign bit. If either source is a NaN, the result is a canonical half‑precision NaN, and a signaling NaN in either operand additionally raises the invalid‑operation exception. No other floating‑point exceptions are generated and the rounding‑mode field in the encoding is ignored. The instruction is available to any privilege level that permits execution of floating‑point operations and imposes no extra access controls.

INSTRUCTION: FSGNJN.S
SUMMARY: FSGNJN.S reads two single‑precision floating‑point registers, rs1 and rs2, and writes the result to destination register fd. All bits of rs1 are copied to fd except the sign bit, which is set to the logical complement of rs2's sign bit; the NaN payload bits from rs1 are preserved unchanged. The instruction does not raise any floating‑point exception flags and performs no NaN canonicalization. If rs1 and rs2 are the same register, the operation reduces to a simple sign‑bit inversion (floating‑point negation).

INSTRUCTION: FSGNJX.D
SUMMARY: FSGNJX.D reads two double‑precision floating‑point registers (rs1 and rs2) and writes the result to rd. The result's sign bit is the exclusive‑OR of the sign bits of rs1 and rs2, while the exponent and mantissa fields are copied unchanged from rs1. The instruction is executable in any privilege mode that allows floating‑point operations and requires no special access rights. If either source operand is a NaN, the NaN handling follows the behavior defined for the analogous single‑precision sign‑injection instruction.

INSTRUCTION: FSGNJX.H
SUMMARY: The FSGNJX.H instruction operates on half‑precision floating‑point registers, taking two source registers and one destination register. It forms a half‑precision result whose exponent and mantissa are taken unchanged from the first source, while the sign bit is set to the exclusive‑OR of the sign bits of the two sources. The resulting value is written to the destination floating‑point register. The instruction has no special privilege requirements or defined exception behavior beyond normal floating‑point handling.

INSTRUCTION: FSGNJX.S
SUMMARY: FSGNJX.S forms a floating‑point result by copying the magnitude bits from source register rs1 while setting the sign bit to the exclusive‑OR of the sign bits of rs1 and rs2, and writes this value to destination register rd. The instruction operates on the standard floating‑point registers and is executable in any privilege mode. It does not raise or modify any floating‑point exception flags. NaN payload bits are left unchanged; the instruction performs no NaN canonicalization.

INSTRUCTION: FSH
SUMMARY: FSH stores a 16‑bit half‑precision floating‑point value to memory, taking the low 16 bits of the source floating‑point register (width FLEN) as the payload and preserving any non‑canonical NaN payload bits unchanged. The store accesses exactly one 16‑bit memory element and, if the effective address is not naturally aligned, atomicity is not guaranteed. The mew bit (inst[28]) is ignored and does not affect operation. No special privilege or access‑control checks are defined for this instruction.

INSTRUCTION: FSQ
SUMMARY: FSQ stores a 128‑bit floating‑point value from its source register to the effective address, preserving all bits including NaN payloads, and uses a width field of binary 100 to select the 128‑bit memory element width. The instruction is subject to pointer masking for the address calculation, and any mismatch between the selected element size and 128 bits yields an undefined architectural result, leaving the destination register indeterminate. Setting the mew bit (inst[28]) triggers an illegal‑instruction exception, whereas the me w field does not affect execution and the destination register receives the same result as when that field is zero. No other privilege checks are specified beyond the required pointer‑masking.

INSTRUCTION: FSUB.Q
SUMMARY: FSUB.Q reads two quad‑precision floating‑point registers rs1 and rs2, subtracts the value in rs2 from the value in rs1, and writes the 128‑bit result to the floating‑point register rd. The fmt field must be set to binary 11 to select the 128‑bit quad‑precision format for the operation. Execution is subject to the usual floating‑point unit enable and privilege checks, with any floating‑point exception flags raised according to the standard IEEE‑754 rules.

INSTRUCTION: FSUB.S
SUMMARY: FSUB.S reads the single‑precision floating‑point registers rs1 and rs2, subtracts the value in rs2 from rs1, and writes the single‑precision result to destination register rd. The rounding mode is taken from the instruction's rm field, which governs how the exact subtraction result is rounded to a representable single‑precision value. No extra exception behavior is specified beyond the standard floating‑point exception handling that applies to all floating‑point instructions.

INSTRUCTION: FSW
SUMMARY: FSW stores the 32‑bit single‑precision value in floating‑point register rs2 to the memory address computed as integer register rs1 plus a 12‑bit signed offset that is zero‑extended and scaled by 4, with pointer‑masking applied to the effective address. The instruction writes exactly four bytes, preserving all payload bits of the source value (including non‑canonical NaNs), and supports misaligned stores whose effect is defined as the masked byte‑wise accesses that would occur without pointer masking. On a store‑address‑misaligned, access‑fault, or page‑fault exception, a transformed standard FSW (retaining rs2, funct3, and opcode) is written to the trap‑instruction register (mtinst or htinst) before the trap is taken. The compressed form C.FSWSP expands to an equivalent uncompressed FSW and therefore shares the same architectural behavior and exception handling.

INSTRUCTION: HFENCE.GVMA
SUMMARY: HFENCE.GVMA is a hypervisor‑fence instruction that validates the same access permissions as HINVAL.GVMA. It cannot be executed in user mode, where it always raises an illegal‑instruction trap. In HS mode the instruction also traps illegal‑instruction when mstatus.TVM = 1, while TVM has no effect in any other privilege level. When the privilege and TVM conditions are satisfied, the fence executes without further exceptions.

INSTRUCTION: HFENCE.VVMA
SUMMARY: HFENCE.VVMA uses rs2 to supply an address‑space identifier, and any bits of rs2 above the ASID maximum are ignored. The instruction may be executed in S‑mode, HS‑mode, and M‑mode without additional restrictions, and the TVM bits in mstatus or hstatus do not cause a trap. Execution in U‑mode is prohibited and raises an illegal‑instruction exception. When the TVM bit is set, HFENCE.VVMA remains permitted in HS‑mode and still does not trap.

INSTRUCTION: HINVAL.GVMA
SUMMARY: HINVAL.GVMA is a privileged instruction that performs the same guest‑virtual‑address translation invalidation as HFENCE.GVMA, requiring identical permissions and raising the same architectural exceptions as that fence operation. It may be executed only in machine mode or in supervisor/HS mode when the mstatus TVM bit is cleared; execution in user mode or when TVM = 1 triggers an illegal‑instruction trap. When permitted, the instruction uses the usual address registers (e.g., rs1/rs2) to specify the GVMA range to invalidate. All exception conditions defined for HFENCE.GVMA (such as misaligned address or access‑fault) also apply to HINVAL.GVMA.

INSTRUCTION: HINVAL.VVMA
SUMMARY: HINVAL.VVMA performs virtual‑memory invalidation and is subject to the same privilege and permission checks as HFENCE.VVMA. Executing it in U‑mode always raises an illegal‑instruction trap. The instruction is exempt from the mstatus.TVM restriction, and a TVM=1 setting neither prevents its execution nor causes a trap.

INSTRUCTION: HLV.B
SUMMARY: HLV.B loads a byte from a hypervisor address into the integer register specified by rd. The instruction is only defined when rs2 = 0, funct7 = 0110000 and funct3 = 100; any other values make the encoding reserved. Execution in U‑mode requires hstatus.HU = 1, otherwise an illegal‑instruction exception is raised, and if hstatus.V = 1 a virtual‑instruction exception is generated. These privilege checks occur before the load is performed.

INSTRUCTION: HLV.BU
SUMMARY: HLV.BU loads an unsigned byte from the effective address, zero‑extends it to XLEN, and writes the result to the destination register, mirroring the LBU operation. It is permitted in M‑mode or HS‑mode, and in U‑mode only when the hstatus.HU field equals 1. Executing it in U‑mode with hstatus.HU = 0 triggers an illegal‑instruction exception. No other architectural effects are defined.

INSTRUCTION: HLV.D
SUMMARY: HLV.D writes a doubleword to the architectural register specified by the rd field. It may be executed only in M‑mode or HS‑mode, and in U‑mode only when the hstatus.HU bit is set. If executed in U‑mode with hstatus.HU cleared, the instruction triggers an illegal‑instruction exception.

INSTRUCTION: HLV.H
SUMMARY: HLV.H loads a signed half‑word from memory, sign‑extends it to the processor's XLEN, and writes the result into the destination integer register. The instruction is permitted only in machine mode, hypervisor‑supervisor mode, or user mode when the hstatus.HU field is set. If it is fetched in user mode while hstatus.HU is clear, an illegal‑instruction exception is raised. No other privilege checks are specified.

INSTRUCTION: HLV.HU
SUMMARY: HLV.HU computes the effective address by adding a sign‑extended 12‑bit immediate to the value in rs1 and loads a 16‑bit halfword from that address. The loaded halfword is treated as unsigned and zero‑extended into the destination integer register rd. Execution is permitted only in M‑mode or HS‑mode, or in U‑mode when hstatus.HU = 1; otherwise an illegal‑instruction exception is raised. The instruction also raises the same architectural load exceptions (e.g., page‑fault, misaligned access) as other load instructions.

INSTRUCTION: HLV.W
SUMMARY: HLV.W is a hypervisor load‑word instruction that reads a 32‑bit value from memory and writes it to an integer destination register. It can be executed only in M‑mode, HS‑mode, or in U‑mode when the hstatus.HU bit is set. Executing it in U‑mode with hstatus.HU = 0 causes an illegal‑instruction exception, and otherwise it raises the same memory‑access exceptions as any other load instruction.

INSTRUCTION: HLV.WU
SUMMARY: HLV.WU is a 64‑bit‑only load that reads an unsigned 32‑bit word from memory and writes the zero‑extended value to an integer register. It requires read permission during address translation, and when sstatus.MXR = 1 execute‑only pages are treated as readable for both VS and G stages; no execute permission is needed. The instruction is illegal in U‑mode if hstatus.HU = 0 and is undefined on RV32 implementations. Any PMP or access‑fault violation raises the same architectural exceptions as a regular LWU load.

INSTRUCTION: HLVX.HU
SUMMARY: The HLVX.HU instruction writes the loaded half‑word into the integer register specified by rd. It may be executed only in M‑mode or HS‑mode, or in U‑mode when hstatus.HU = 1; otherwise an illegal‑instruction exception is taken. The addressed memory must be marked executable in both translation stages (read permission is not required), and machine‑level PMP cannot be bypassed—an execute‑only PMP region causes an access‑fault. Any fault generated by the load (e.g., page‑fault or access‑fault) is reported as a standard load‑type exception, not as a fetch‑type exception.

INSTRUCTION: HLVX.WU
SUMMARY: HLVX.WU loads a 32‑bit unsigned word from the virtual address formed by adding an immediate to the address in rs1 and writes the result to rd, using execute permission rather than read permission. The instruction is legal only in M‑mode or HS‑mode, or in U‑mode when hstatus.HU = 1; otherwise it raises an illegal‑instruction exception. During translation both stages must grant execute permission, PMP checks are not bypassed, and an execute‑only PMP entry causes an access‑fault exception. It raises the same load‑type faults as ordinary loads (e.g., page‑fault, access‑fault) and pointer masking is never applied, even if MXR is set.

INSTRUCTION: HSV.B
SUMMARY: HSV.B stores the low‑order byte of rs2 to the memory address computed from rs1 and does not produce a destination register result. When hstatus.SPVP = 1, the memory access is performed with an effective privilege mode of VS. If the current mode is U‑mode and hstatus.HU = 0, the instruction raises an illegal‑instruction exception. No additional permission checks or side effects are defined beyond the byte store.

INSTRUCTION: HSV.D
SUMMARY: HSV.D stores the 64‑bit value in rs2 to the address computed by the instruction; the destination register rd is architecturally ignored and must be encoded as zero, and the instruction is identified by opcode 1110011, funct7 0110111, and funct3 100. It is defined only for RV64 and may execute only in M‑mode, HS‑mode, or U‑mode when hstatus.HU=1; executing it in any other mode or in U‑mode with HU=0 raises an illegal‑instruction exception. The HS‑level sstatus.SUM field does not affect address translation, while sstatus.MXR permits loads from execute‑only pages for the translation stages of the access. If the instruction completes without performing a load or store to the computed address, no memory transaction occurs.

INSTRUCTION: HSV.H
SUMMARY: HSV.H is a hypervisor‑view instruction that can be executed only in M‑mode or HS‑mode, or in U‑mode when the hstatus register's HU field is set to 1. If it is invoked from U‑mode while hstatus.HU is 0, an illegal‑instruction exception is generated.

INSTRUCTION: HSV.W
SUMMARY: HSV.W is a hypervisor store instruction that does not write a destination register, with its rd field fixed to zero. The instruction is identified by a funct7 pattern of 0110101 and a funct3 pattern of 100, selecting the word‑size store variant of the Hypervisor Store class. It may be executed only in M‑mode or HS‑mode, or in U‑mode when the hstatus.HU bit is set. Executing it in U‑mode with hstatus.HU clear triggers an illegal‑instruction exception.

INSTRUCTION: JAL
SUMMARY: JAL forms its jump target by sign‑extending the J‑immediate (a multiple of 2 bytes) and adding it to the address of the JAL instruction, requiring the result to be 4‑byte aligned and to lie within ±1 MiB of the current PC. The address of the following instruction (pc+4 for a 32‑bit JAL, pc+2 for a compressed 16‑bit form) is written to the architectural register specified by rd, with writes to x0 having no effect. If the computed target is misaligned the instruction raises an instruction‑address‑misaligned exception; otherwise control transfers unconditionally with no privilege restrictions.

INSTRUCTION: JALR
SUMMARY: The JALR instruction computes its target address by adding the sign‑extended 12‑bit immediate to the value in source register rs1 and then clearing the least‑significant bit; if the result is not 4‑byte aligned an instruction‑address‑misaligned exception is raised (except on implementations with IALIGN = 16). It writes the address of the following instruction (pc+4) to destination register rd unless rd = x0, in which case the link is omitted and the instruction acts as an unconditional jump (or a standard RET when rd = x0, rs1 = x1, imm = 0). When rd = x1 and the immediate is zero, the next PC is set to rs1 and the return address of the following compressed instruction (pc+2) is stored in x1, provided rs1 ≠ x0; if rs1 = x0 the encoding corresponds to C.EBREAK and JALR semantics do not apply. Any software‑check exception generated by the Zicfilp extension has priority between illegal‑instruction and instruction‑access‑fault exceptions.

INSTRUCTION: LB
SUMMARY: LB computes its effective address by adding the contents of rs1 to the sign‑extended 12‑bit immediate offset and reads an 8‑bit byte from that memory location. The loaded byte is sign‑extended to XLEN and written to rd, with the write suppressed only when rd = x0 but any memory‑access exception still being raised. A naturally aligned access is performed atomically and never generates an address‑misaligned exception; misaligned accesses are permitted and yield the same byte that would be obtained after applying per‑byte pointer‑masking. The operation is invariant to endianness, returning the exact byte stored at the addressed location.

INSTRUCTION: LBU
SUMMARY: LBU computes the effective address by adding the sign‑extended immediate offset to the base register rs1 and reads a single byte from that address, applying the same address‑space, permission, and pointer‑masking checks as any other load. The byte is zero‑extended to the processor's XLEN (64 bits on RV64) and written to rd; if rd is x0 the load and any resulting exceptions still occur but the result is discarded. LBU never raises an address‑misaligned exception, supports misaligned accesses, records a synchronous fault in mtinst/htinst, and is defined for RV64 (including the compressed c.lbu form present only when the Zcb extension, which depends on Zca, is implemented).

INSTRUCTION: LD
SUMMARY: The LD instruction is a 64‑bit (double‑word) load that reads eight bytes from the effective address (base register rs1 plus a sign‑extended offset, or sp‑relative as defined for cm.pop/popret) and writes the value to the architectural integer register rd, discarding the result only when rd = x0. The base register (rs1, or x2 when the address is stack‑pointer‑relative) must retain its original value on any trap, and if a split‑address load faults on its second memory operation the first half is still written to rd before the exception is taken. Loads are subject to pointer‑masking according to MPRV/SPVP settings unless MXR is set; misaligned addresses raise a load‑address‑misaligned exception unless they are aligned to 8 bytes (guaranteed atomic) or to 4 bytes (atomic 32‑bit halves). When a trap occurs, the transformed LD stored in mtinst/htinst preserves the original opcode, funct3 and rd, with its offset field encoding the difference between the faulting virtual address and the original address.

INSTRUCTION: LH
SUMMARY: LH computes its effective address by adding the sign‑extended 12‑bit immediate to rs1 and then applying the pointer‑masking transformation before the memory read. It reads a 16‑bit half‑word from that address (or, if the address is not 2‑byte aligned, reads the two constituent bytes after pointer‑masking and assembles them), sign‑extends the value to XLEN, and writes the result to rd (the write is discarded only when rd is x0, though any exceptions still occur). No address‑misaligned exception is raised for either aligned or misaligned accesses, and encodings with only the load‑release (rl) bit set are illegal. Normal load privilege and access‑control checks apply.

INSTRUCTION: LHU
SUMMARY: LHU forms its effective address by adding the contents of rs1 to a 12‑bit immediate (sign‑ or zero‑extended as defined by the implementation) and reads a 16‑bit halfword from that address. The halfword is zero‑extended to the processor's XLEN (64 bits on RV64) and written to the destination register rd, with the write omitted only when rd = x0 while any exceptions are still taken. Misaligned accesses are handled either by hardware‑masked aligned reads or, if unsupported, by raising a load‑address‑misaligned exception (mcause = 4); any trap also causes the faulting instruction to be recorded in the trap‑instruction CSR. The effective address may be subject to pointer‑masking according to the current privilege mode's MPRV/SPVP settings, unless MXR is active, which disables such masking.

INSTRUCTION: LPAD
SUMMARY: The LPAD instruction works with the Expected Landing‑Pad (ELP) mechanism, using register x7 to hold the high bits of the expected landing‑pad label and the instruction's LPL field to supply the label (zero suppresses the check). When the Zicfilp extension is enabled (LPE=1 in the relevant menvcfg/senvcfg/mseccfg CSR) and the ELP state is LP_EXPECTED, a correctly 4‑byte‑aligned LPAD whose LPL is zero or matches x7 clears the ELP state to NO_LP_EXPECTED; any mis‑alignment or label mismatch triggers a software‑check exception (cause 18, "landing pad fault (code=2)"). If the LPE control bits are cleared in any privilege mode, execution of LPAD has no architectural effect and does not modify the ELP state. Software‑check exceptions generated by an LPAD or by fetching a non‑LPAD when LP_EXPECTED are trapped to the current or a higher privilege level.

INSTRUCTION: LR.D
SUMMARY: LR.D loads a 64‑bit value from the address in rs1 into rd and establishes a reservation set that covers the addressed doubleword, invalidating any earlier reservation for the hart (which may hold only one reservation at a time). The instruction is defined solely for RV64 implementations and the address must be naturally 8‑byte aligned; otherwise a misaligned‑address or access‑fault exception is raised. If the aq bit is set, the load participates in an acquire memory‑ordering sequence. Normal privilege and access checks for load instructions apply.

INSTRUCTION: LR.W
SUMMARY: LR.W reads a 32‑bit word from the address in rs1, sign‑extends the value and writes it to rd while establishing a reservation that includes at least the accessed bytes. On RV64 implementations the loaded 32‑bit value is sign‑extended before being placed in rd. If the address in rs1 is not naturally aligned to a four‑byte boundary, the instruction raises an address‑misaligned or access‑fault exception.

INSTRUCTION: LUI
SUMMARY: LUI writes a 32‑bit constant to an integer register rd, loading the 20‑bit U‑immediate into the upper portion and clearing the lower 12 bits, and on RV64I the result is sign‑extended to 64 bits with no special privilege checks. The value serves as the high half of an address, allowing a following JALR, load or store to add a signed 12‑bit offset within the range [−2³¹−2¹¹, 2³¹−2¹¹−1] on RV64I. In call‑offset expansions LUI initializes the link register (x1 or x5) with this pattern, while in tail‑offset expansions it does the same for x7. If an indirect jump or call uses x7 when its low 12 bits are non‑zero, the PC is set to the full x7 value and a misaligned‑address exception is raised if the target is not 2‑byte aligned.

INSTRUCTION: LW
SUMMARY: LW adds the sign‑extended 12‑bit immediate to the value in rs1 to form an effective address, reads a 32‑bit word from that address, sign‑extends it to XLEN on RV64, and writes the result to rd (writes to x0 are discarded but side‑effects and exceptions still occur). The instruction is allowed only in M‑mode, HS‑mode, or U‑mode with hstatus.HU = 1, and any EEI‑protected region or privilege violation triggers a load‑access‑fault. An effective address not divisible by 4 raises a load‑address‑misaligned exception (mcause = 4) unless the implementation supports misaligned accesses, in which case the bytes are assembled as if each aligned sub‑access were performed separately; pointer‑masking that leaves the address unmasked also causes a load‑access‑fault. LW raises the same architectural exceptions as any other load (load‑address‑misaligned, load‑access‑fault, load‑page‑fault, etc.), and these exceptions terminate the load sequence even when rd = x0.

INSTRUCTION: LWU
SUMMARY: The LWU instruction loads a 32‑bit word from the calculated effective memory address and writes the zero‑extended 64‑bit result into the destination integer register. The effective address is formed from the base register plus an immediate offset as with other loads. Any CSR read, CSR write, hardware‑generated CSR write, or DMA controller access caused by the instruction makes the architectural state show the CSR change or sets a DMA‑access indicator. No other side effects are defined beyond the load and the optional CSR/DMA updates.

INSTRUCTION: MAX
SUMMARY: The MAX instruction writes to the destination register rd the greater of the two source registers rs1 and rs2, using IEEE‑754 ordering for single‑precision floating‑point operands (producing a canonical NaN when both inputs are NaN) and standard integer ordering for integer operands. It requires the MIN/MAX control field to be set for the MAX operation, is defined only when the Zbb extension is present, and its semantics are unaffected by the Zvkt extension. Executing MAX generates no architecturally defined exceptions, traps, or permission checks.

INSTRUCTION: MAXU
SUMMARY: MAXU reads unsigned values from source registers rs1 and rs2, compares them with an unsigned less‑than test, and writes the larger (or equal) value to destination register rd. The instruction is defined only for the hart's native XLEN (RV32 or RV64); executing it on an unsupported XLEN raises an illegal‑instruction exception. No architectural state other than rd is modified. It may be executed in any privilege mode that permits normal integer register access.

INSTRUCTION: MIN
SUMMARY: The MIN instruction, defined only for RV64 implementations, reads two XLEN‑bit signed integers from source registers rs1 and rs2, compares them using signed‑less‑than semantics, and writes the smaller operand to destination register rd (rs1's value if rs1 < rs2, otherwise rs2's value, including equality). It is an ordinary integer instruction with no special privilege or access‑control restrictions. Architecturally it behaves identically to the scalar floating‑point MIN operation defined in the RISC‑V F/D/Q extensions. No additional side effects or exception conditions are specified.

INSTRUCTION: MINU
SUMMARY: The MINU instruction reads the unsigned XLEN‑wide values from source registers rs1 and rs2, compares them using unsigned less‑than semantics, and selects the smaller (or equal) operand. The selected unsigned value is written to the destination register rd as a full‑XLEN result, with no other architectural state altered. Execution is unrestricted by privilege level, and when the two source values are equal the same value is written to rd.

INSTRUCTION: MNRET
SUMMARY: MNRET can be executed only while the processor is in Machine mode; execution in any other privilege mode triggers an illegal‑instruction exception. It sets the program counter to the value stored in the mnepc CSR (applying any IALIGN‑32 masking) and resumes execution at the RNMI handler. If the instruction changes the active privilege mode to one less privileged than Machine, it clears the MDT bit, clears MPRV in mstatus, and, when the new mode is User, Virtual‑Supervisor, or Virtual‑User, also clears the SDT bit in sstatus; with the Zicfilp extension present, it additionally writes to the ELP CSR the logical AND of the selected mode's LPE CSR and the MNPELP field of mnstatus. These actions define the high‑level semantics of the MNRET instruction.

INSTRUCTION: MRET
SUMMARY: MRET returns from a machine‑mode trap by restoring the program counter from mepc (with any IALIGN‑32 masking applied) and reinstating the previous privilege mode encoded in MPP (and MPV). It also restores MIE from MPIE, sets MPIE to 1, clears MPV and MPP to the least‑privileged supported mode, and when the target mode is less privileged than machine it forces MPRV to 0 (clearing sstatus.SDT for U/VS/VU and virtual‑mode V when MPP = 3). Execution of MRET is privileged; if it is executed in any mode lower than machine an illegal‑instruction exception is raised, and the handler redirects control back to the pre‑trap privilege level. If the new privilege mode has its LPE bit set, the effective privilege level is taken from the machine‑mode PELP bit.

INSTRUCTION: MUL
SUMMARY: The MUL instruction reads signed XLEN‑bit values from source registers rs1 and rs2, multiplies them to produce a 2 × XLEN‑bit product, and atomically writes the low XLEN bits of that product to destination register rd. It is defined only when the implementation provides and enables either the M extension or the Zmmul extension, and its binary encoding matches the standard M‑extension MUL. The high XLEN bits of the product are discarded with no architectural effect. No additional side effects or privilege restrictions are specified.

INSTRUCTION: MULH
SUMMARY: MULH reads the signed integer values in source registers rs1 and rs2, multiplies them, and writes the upper XLEN bits of the 2·XLEN‑bit product to destination register rd. The instruction is defined by the Zmmul extension but must behave identically to the MULH defined in the M extension, so its architectural effect is the same regardless of which extension supplies it. Execution is permitted in any privilege mode provided the implementing core supports the required extension; otherwise the instruction causes an illegal‑instruction exception. No other architectural state is accessed or modified and the instruction does not raise any other traps.

INSTRUCTION: MULHSU
SUMMARY: MULHSU reads the signed XLEN‑bit value in rs1 and the unsigned XLEN‑bit value in rs2, multiplies them to produce a 2 × XLEN‑bit product, and writes the upper XLEN bits of that product to the destination register rd while discarding the lower XLEN bits. The instruction is usable in all privilege modes with no special access control restrictions. No defined exceptions or fault conditions are associated with its execution.

INSTRUCTION: MULHU
SUMMARY: MULHU reads unsigned values from source registers rs1 and rs2, computes their full 2 × XLEN‑bit product, and writes the upper XLEN bits of that product into destination register rd. The instruction belongs to the standard integer‑multiply group and is encoded with the usual opcode/funct fields, which together define its operation. It is available in any privilege mode that permits integer arithmetic and imposes no special access restrictions. No division, remainder, or extra exceptions are produced beyond the normal illegal‑instruction fault if the M‑extension is not implemented.

INSTRUCTION: MULW
SUMMARY: MULW reads the low‑order 32 bits of the two source registers, multiplies them, and generates a 32‑bit product. The product's low 32 bits are sign‑extended to 64 bits and written to the destination register. Bits above bit 31 of the sources are ignored during the operation. No other architectural state is modified, and the instruction is permitted in all privilege modes.

INSTRUCTION: NTL.P1
SUMMARY: NTL.P1 is a hint‑type instruction that does not read or write any general‑purpose registers, CSRs, or memory; its only architectural effect is to advance the program counter to the following sequential address (and optionally update performance counters). The instruction's encoding contains the standard hint opcode fields, which are subject to the same privilege and reservation checks as other HINT operations. If the executing privilege level or opcode validity fails these checks, the core raises an illegal‑instruction exception with mcause = 2; otherwise execution proceeds with no state change beyond the PC update. Any other exception or unexpected state change violates the specification.

INSTRUCTION: OR
