SUMMARY: The OR instruction is an RV32I base‑integer R‑type operation that reads two source integer registers (rs1, rs2) and writes the result to a destination integer register (rd). It computes a bitwise logical OR, setting each result bit to 1 if the corresponding bit of either source operand is 1, otherwise to 0. The instruction is not subject to privilege restrictions and does not trigger any architectural exceptions.

INSTRUCTION: ORC.B
SUMMARY: ORC.B reads the source register rs and, for each byte of the value, produces an XLEN‑wide result where a byte is 0x00 if the corresponding source byte is zero and 0xFF otherwise; this result (sign‑ or zero‑extended as defined for the instruction) is written to the destination register rd. The instruction does not modify any architectural state other than rd and raises no exceptions. It is usable in any privilege mode without special access restrictions.

INSTRUCTION: ORI
SUMMARY: The ORI instruction reads a 12‑bit immediate, sign‑extends it to the processor's XLEN, and bitwise‑ORs it with the value in integer source register rs1. The resulting XLEN‑wide value is written to integer destination register rd. All integer registers are accessible in any privilege mode, and the operation does not raise any architectural exceptions. No additional access controls or side effects apply.

INSTRUCTION: ORN
SUMMARY: The ORN instruction reads the XLEN‑bit values from source registers rs1 and rs2, forms the bitwise complement of the value in rs2, and computes the bitwise OR of that complement with the value in rs1. The resulting XLEN‑wide word (64 bits on RV64) is written to the destination register rd, with no other architectural state modified and no special privilege or exception handling required.

INSTRUCTION: PACK
SUMMARY: The PACK instruction is defined only for RV64 implementations; on RV32 it raises an illegal‑instruction exception (mcause = 2). It reads the lower XLEN/2 bits of rs1 as the low half and the lower XLEN/2 bits of rs2 as the high half, concatenates them into an XLEN‑bit value, sign‑extends that value, and writes the result to rd. No other architectural state (registers or CSRs) is modified. When rs2 is x0 the effect is equivalent to ZEXT.H, producing a zero‑extended half‑word.

INSTRUCTION: PACKH
SUMMARY: PACKH reads the low‑order byte of rs1 and rs2, places the byte from rs2 as the high half and the byte from rs1 as the low half of a 16‑bit value, and writes that value to rd. The 16‑bit result is zero‑extended, clearing all bits of rd above bit 15. PACKH is defined only for the RV64 ISA variant; implementations that support the Zbkb extension must provide it, while the encoding is reserved for implementations without RV64 support.

INSTRUCTION: PACKW
SUMMARY: PACKW is defined only when the Zbkb extension is implemented; otherwise its encoding is reserved. It reads rs1 and rs2, ignores their upper 32 bits, and extracts each register's low 16 bits. The low 16 bits of rs2 become bits 31‑16 and the low 16 bits of rs1 become bits 15‑0 of a 32‑bit intermediate, which is then sign‑extended to the full XLEN and written to rd (on RV64 the upper 32 bits are the sign extension of bit 31). No special privilege checks apply, and when rs2 = x0 the architectural result is identical to ZEXT.H.

INSTRUCTION: PREFETCH.I
SUMMARY: PREFETCH.I issues an instruction‑cache prefetch without reading or writing any architecturally visible integer or floating‑point registers and without modifying memory state. It uses a base register plus an immediate whose lower five bits must be zero, which guarantees that the effective address is 32‑byte aligned. If any of those low‑order immediate bits are non‑zero, the instruction raises an illegal‑instruction exception (mcause = 2). No additional privilege checks are specified, so the instruction is permitted in any execution mode that allows normal memory accesses.

INSTRUCTION: REM
SUMMARY: The REM instruction computes the signed remainder of the integer in source register rs1 divided by the integer in source register rs2, placing the result in destination register rd. If rs2 is zero, the dividend (rs1) is written to rd; if the division overflows (rs1 is the most‑negative value and rs2 is –1), rd receives zero. For all other cases the remainder satisfies dividend = divisor × quotient + remainder and, when non‑zero, its sign matches the dividend's sign. The instruction is a regular integer operation executable in any privilege mode.

INSTRUCTION: REMU
SUMMARY: REMU reads the unsigned dividend from rs1 and the unsigned divisor from rs2, and writes the unsigned remainder to rd. The result obeys dividend = divisor × unsigned‑quotient + remainder, with the remainder less than the divisor when the divisor is non‑zero. If rs2 is zero, the remainder produced equals the dividend. The instruction never raises an overflow exception and is executable in all privilege modes without special access control.

INSTRUCTION: REMUW
SUMMARY: REMUW reads the lower 32 bits of source register rs1 as an unsigned dividend and the lower 32 bits of rs2 as an unsigned divisor, producing a 32‑bit unsigned remainder that is sign‑extended into the destination register rd. The result satisfies dividend = divisor × quotient + remainder for all non‑zero divisors, and when the divisor is zero the remainder is defined to be the dividend. A divide‑by‑zero exception is raised if the divisor is zero, while no overflow or other exceptions can occur for non‑zero divisors. The instruction is a regular integer operation and is allowed in all privilege modes without special access control.

INSTRUCTION: REMW
SUMMARY: REMW is an RV64 integer instruction that takes the low‑32 bits of rs1 as a signed dividend and the low‑32 bits of rs2 as a signed divisor, computes the signed remainder, and writes the sign‑extended 64‑bit result to rd. If the divisor's low‑32 bits are zero, the result is the sign‑extended low‑32‑bit dividend; if the dividend is –2³¹ and the divisor is –1, the result is zero (sign‑extended). For all other cases the remainder satisfies dividend = divisor × quotient + remainder using signed 32‑bit arithmetic. The instruction belongs to the base integer ISA and is available in all privilege modes without special access restrictions.

INSTRUCTION: REV8
SUMMARY: REV8 is an integer instruction that reads a source integer register (rs1) and writes to a destination integer register (rd) the value obtained by reversing the order of all XLEN/8 bytes of the source. The reversal spans the full XLEN (32 bits on RV32, 64 bits on RV64), leaves each byte's bit pattern unchanged, and does not modify any other architectural state. The instruction is permitted in any privilege mode and has no defined behavior for reversing only a subset of the register width; it must always operate on the complete register. In the vector‑encoding space, a vs1 field value of 01001 selects the REV8 operation.

INSTRUCTION: ROL
SUMMARY: ROL rd, rs1, rs2 rotates the XLEN‑bit value in register rs1 left by a distance equal to the low log₂(XLEN) bits of rs2 (i.e., rs2 modulo XLEN) and writes the result to register rd. The rotation is a circular left shift, defined as (X(rs1) << shamt) | (X(rs1) >> (XLEN‑shamt)) with shamt taken from bits [4:0] when XLEN = 32 or bits [5:0] when XLEN = 64, using unsigned XLEN‑bit arithmetic. The instruction only updates the architectural registers involved and does not raise any exceptions or affect other processor state.

INSTRUCTION: ROLW
SUMMARY: ROLW is defined only for RV64 and rotates the low‑32‑bit word of rs1 left by a shift amount taken from the low 5 bits of rs2, wrapping the bits shifted out of the high end into the low end. The rotation is applied to the zero‑extended low word of rs1, producing a 32‑bit result that is sign‑extended (replicating bit 31) to the full XLEN and written to rd, overwriting the entire destination register. If the extracted shift amount is zero, rd receives the sign‑extended low 32 bits of rs1 unchanged. Any privilege check or exception that would not arise for a normal w‑suffixed instruction triggers an illegal‑instruction exception (mcause = illegal‑instruction).

INSTRUCTION: ROR
SUMMARY: The ROR instruction rotates the XLEN‑bit value in source register rs1 right by a count taken from the low log2(XLEN) bits of source register rs2 (bits 4‑0 when XLEN=32, bits 5‑0 when XLEN=64) and writes the resulting XLEN‑bit value to destination register rd. The rotation is a circular shift on unsigned operands, with bits shifted out of the low end re‑entering at the high end. The encoding is defined only when the Zbb or Zbkb extension is present (otherwise it is reserved) and the instruction completes without raising any exception.

INSTRUCTION: RORI
SUMMARY: RORI is an integer rotate‑right instruction that exists only when the Zbb or Zbk extension is implemented and is usable in all privilege modes. It rotates the full XLEN‑wide value in source register rs1 right by the amount encoded in the shamt immediate (using the low log2(XLEN) bits, e.g., shamt[5:0] on RV64) and writes the XLEN‑wide result to destination register rd. The instruction simply overwrites rd with the rotated value and does not cause any architectural side effects or defined exceptions.

INSTRUCTION: RORIW
SUMMARY: RORIW is a RV64 instruction (requiring the Zbkb extension) that is not defined for RV32. It reads rs1, extracts bits 31‑0, zero‑extends that word to XLEN, and rotates the 32‑bit value right by an immediate shamt (a 5‑bit amount taken modulo 32). The rotated 32‑bit result is sign‑extended (bit 31 replicated to the upper bits) and written to rd. A rotate amount of zero yields the sign‑extended original 32‑bit word; otherwise normal rotation semantics apply.

INSTRUCTION: RORW
SUMMARY: RORW is an RV64‑only instruction that reads the low‑32‑bit word of rs1 and the low‑5‑bit field of rs2 as a rotate‑right amount (mod 32). It rotates that 32‑bit value right with wrap‑around, sign‑extends the 32‑bit result by replicating bit 31 into the upper bits, and writes the XLEN‑wide value to rd, replacing its prior contents. The instruction causes no architectural side effects, performs no privilege or permission checks, and raises an illegal‑instruction exception only on RV32 implementations where the encoding is undefined.

INSTRUCTION: SB
SUMMARY: SB computes its effective address by adding the contents of rs1 to a sign‑extended 12‑bit immediate offset and stores the low‑order 8 bits of rs2 at that byte address. It does not modify any architectural integer register and may be executed in any privilege mode without prerequisite conditions. Pointer masking is applied to the effective address according to the current privilege mode (respecting MPRV and MXR), and while byte stores are naturally aligned, a misaligned store will raise a store‑address‑misaligned exception (mcause = 0x7) when masking is not in effect. Any store‑address‑misaligned, access‑fault, or page‑fault exception caused by SB writes the transformed SB encoding into the trap‑instruction CSR (mtinst/htinst).

INSTRUCTION: SC.D
SUMMARY: SC.D is a RV64‑only store‑conditional that uses rs1 as an eight‑byte‑aligned address, rs2 as the doubleword data to store, and rd to receive a success code (0) or failure code (non‑zero). If rs1 is not naturally aligned the implementation raises an address‑misaligned or access‑fault exception. The store succeeds only when the address remains in the reservation set established by the most recent LR and no other hart, intervening SC, or external write to any byte of that reservation occurs (an external write that does not touch the LR‑accessed bytes may arbitrarily succeed or fail); on failure memory is unchanged and rd is set to a non‑zero value. Executing SC.D always invalidates the executing hart's reservation, and its effect cannot be observed by another hart before the LR that created the reservation is observed.

INSTRUCTION: SC.W
SUMMARY: SC.W writes the 32‑bit word from rs2 to the address in rs1 and writes zero to rd on success, otherwise it writes a non‑zero value to rd without modifying memory. The instruction succeeds only if the executing hart's reservation is still valid—i.e., the address is in the reservation set and no intervening store from another hart, device write, or another SC (in program order) has been observed— and it invalidates any reservation held by the hart regardless of the outcome. Before retiring, SC.W must pass memory‑permission checks and the address in rs1 must be naturally 4‑byte aligned, otherwise an address‑misaligned or access‑fault exception is raised. On RV64 implementations the value placed in rd is sign‑extended.

INSTRUCTION: SCTRCLR
SUMMARY: The SCTRCLR instruction atomically clears all CTR entry registers for every depth, zeros the CCV field, and resets the CtrCycleCounter, causing subsequent reads of ctrsource, ctrtarget, ctrdata, and the counters to return zero until the next qualified control transfer. The first control‑transfer record generated after execution must have its ctrdata.CCV field equal to zero, and any non‑zero CCV in that record constitutes a violation. Execution is privileged: if Smstateen is present and the CTR enable bits for the current privilege level are not set, or if CTR state‑enable restrictions apply in U‑mode, the instruction raises an illegal‑instruction exception; otherwise it completes without raising illegal or virtual‑instruction exceptions.

INSTRUCTION: SD
SUMMARY: SD stores the 64‑bit value from integer register rs2 (or zero if rs2 is x0) to the memory address obtained by sign‑extending the 12‑bit offset and adding it to the base register rs1, with the C.SDSP encoding using the stack pointer x2 plus offset×8 as the base. The operation transfers exactly 8 bytes as a doubleword, performing an atomic 8‑byte store when the effective address is 8‑byte aligned and otherwise as two atomic 4‑byte sub‑stores, the first of which remains architecturally visible if an exception occurs after it. Misaligned addresses that are not a multiple of 8 can raise a store/AMO address‑misaligned exception, and pointer masking must not be applied when MXR is enabled. For RVWMO ordering and exception handling, SD is treated as a misaligned store.

INSTRUCTION: SEXT.B
SUMMARY: SEXT.B reads the low‑order 8 bits of source register rs, treats bit 7 as a sign, and creates an XLEN‑wide value by replicating that sign bit into all higher‑order bits. The XLEN‑wide result replaces the previous contents of destination register rd. Functionally, the operation is equivalent to shifting rs left by (XLEN‑8) bits and then shifting the result right arithmetically by the same amount. The instruction is available in all privilege modes and has no defined exceptions.

INSTRUCTION: SEXT.H
SUMMARY: SEXT.H reads an integer register rs, extracts its lower 16 bits, sign‑extends them to the current XLEN, and writes the XLEN‑wide result to integer register rd. The sign‑extension replicates the original bit 15 into all more‑significant bits, and the operation does not modify any other architectural state, including memory or other registers. If rd is x0 the computed value is discarded and no architectural state changes occur; the instruction is defined for both RV32 and RV64 implementations.

INSTRUCTION: SFENCE.INVAL.IR
SUMMARY: SFENCE.INVAL.IR is a fence that orders and invalidates instruction‑side translation structures. It may be executed only in S‑mode or VS‑mode; attempting it in U‑mode raises an illegal‑instruction exception. Execution in the permitted modes is always allowed regardless of the values of mstatus.TVM or hstatus.VTVM, and even when those bits are set the instruction does not trap because it has only ordering effects and no visible side effects.

INSTRUCTION: SFENCE.VMA
SUMMARY: SFENCE.VMA uses rs1 as the target virtual address (if rs1 does not contain a valid virtual address the instruction has no effect and raises no exception) and rs2 to supply an address‑space identifier, with implementations ignoring any high‑order bits of rs2 when it is non‑zero. The instruction is only permitted in supervisor (or hypervisor‑supervisor) mode when the mstatus.TVM field is cleared; executing it in user mode or in any mode with TVM set triggers an illegal‑instruction exception. When permitted, SFENCE.VMA observes the same execution permissions and raises the same exceptions as SINVAL.VMA, HINVAL.VVMA, and HINVAL.GVMA.

INSTRUCTION: SFENCE.W.INVAL
SUMMARY: SFENCE.W.INVAL is a supervisor‑level fence that invalidates pending writes to memory and does not involve source or destination registers. It is permitted in supervisor or virtual‑supervisor mode irrespective of the mstatus.TVM or hstatus.VTVM settings. Executing it in user mode is illegal and raises an illegal‑instruction exception. No other privilege checks affect its execution.

INSTRUCTION: SH
SUMMARY: SH is a store‑halfword instruction that adds a zero‑extended immediate to the base register rs1, applies the current privilege‑mode pointer‑masking, and writes the low‑order 16 bits of rs2 to the resulting effective address. The store is atomic when the address is naturally aligned (address % 2 = 0); if misaligned, the hardware may split the operation into two byte stores, raise a misaligned‑address exception, or complete silently according to the EEI, and any trap generated must be either contained or fatal. Misaligned‑address, access‑fault, and page‑fault exceptions are defined for SH, while other exceptions do not require mtinst/htinst to retain the transformed instruction encoding. During normal execution mtinst and htinst are not written, and pointer‑masking settings (including MPRV/SPVP) determine the address used for the store.

INSTRUCTION: SH1ADD
SUMMARY: SH1ADD reads the architectural registers X(rs1) and X(rs2), zero‑extending the low 32‑bit word of X(rs1) before any operation. It computes the XLEN‑wide logical left shift of that zero‑extended value by one bit, adds the result to X(rs2) using XLEN‑bit addition modulo 2^XLEN, and writes the XLEN‑bit sum to X(rd), replacing its previous value. No other integer registers or architectural state are changed, and the instruction imposes no privilege or access‑control restrictions.

INSTRUCTION: SH1ADD.UW
SUMMARY: The SH1ADD.UW instruction reads rs1 and rs2, extracts the low‑32 bits of rs1, zero‑extends that value to the processor's XLEN, left‑shifts it by one bit, and adds the result to the full‑XLEN value in rs2; the XLEN‑wide sum is written to rd, with arithmetic performed modulo 2^XLEN. No other architectural state is changed. The instruction is only defined for implementations with XLEN > 32; on an RV32 core it raises an illegal‑instruction exception. It requires no special privilege or access‑control beyond the normal execution permissions.

INSTRUCTION: SH2ADD
SUMMARY: SH2ADD takes rs1 and rs2 as source registers and writes the result to rd. It logically left‑shifts rs1 by two bits (zero‑extending the LSBs and discarding bits beyond XLEN) and adds this shifted value to rs2 using XLEN‑wide two's‑complement modular arithmetic, wrapping on overflow. The instruction updates only rd and does not affect any other architectural state. It is a regular integer instruction with no special privilege or exception behavior.

INSTRUCTION: SH2ADD.UW
SUMMARY: SH2ADD.UW is a RV64‑only instruction that reads rs2 as a 64‑bit base value, extracts the low 32 bits of rs1, zero‑extends them to 64 bits, left‑shifts the extended value by 2 bits, adds the shifted value to the base modulo 2^64, and writes the 64‑bit result to rd. The instruction affects no architectural state other than updating rd and raises only an illegal‑instruction exception if the encoding is undefined for the implementation. It is defined exclusively for implementations with XLEN = 64 and imposes no additional privilege or permission checks.

INSTRUCTION: SH3ADD
SUMMARY: SH3ADD reads integer registers rs1 and rs2, left‑shifts rs2 by three bits, adds the shifted value to rs1 with XLEN‑wide modular arithmetic, and writes the low XLEN bits to destination register rd. The shift amount is fixed at three, inserting zeros into the vacated low‑order bits and discarding overflow bits. The instruction is only defined for XLEN = 64; on any other XLEN it raises an illegal‑instruction exception (mcause = 2). It performs no memory or CSR accesses, does not modify the program counter beyond normal sequential flow, does not trap on overflow, and rs1, rs2, and rd may be the same architectural register.

INSTRUCTION: SH3ADD.UW
SUMMARY: SH3ADD.UW is defined only for RV64 implementations; its encoding is reserved on RV32, causing an illegal‑instruction exception. The instruction reads the low 32 bits of rs1 and rs2, zero‑extends each to the XLEN width, left‑shifts the rs1 operand by three bits, adds the shifted value to the zero‑extended rs2 operand using 64‑bit modular arithmetic, and writes the 64‑bit result to rd. No architectural state other than rd is modified, and there are no additional privilege or exception conditions beyond the standard illegal‑instruction handling.

INSTRUCTION: SHA256SIG0
SUMMARY: The SHA256SIG0 instruction reads the low 32-bit portion of source register rs1, computes a 32-bit result equal to ROTR(rs1,2) xor ROTR(rs1,13) xor ROTR(rs1,22) (equivalently the xor of a 7-bit rotate‑right, an 18-bit rotate‑right, and a logical right shift by 3 bits), and writes this value sign‑extended to XLEN into destination register rd. On RV64 implementations only the low 32 bits are used and the sign‑extended 64-bit result is stored in rd. The instruction has no special privilege or access‑control requirements beyond those of ordinary integer instructions. An implementation that produces a value differing from the defined Sigma0 transformation is considered non‑conforming.

INSTRUCTION: SHA256SIG1
SUMMARY: SHA256SIG1 reads the low 32‑bit value from source register rs1, computes sigma1 = rotr(x,17) xor rotr(x,19) xor (x >> 10), and produces a 32‑bit result. The 32‑bit result is sign-extended to the XLEN of the executing hart (64 bits on RV64) and written to destination register rd. The instruction is available in all privilege levels, updates rd with the sign-extended value, and retires without raising an exception.

INSTRUCTION: SHA256SUM0
SUMMARY: The SHA256SUM0 instruction reads the low‑32 bits of source register rs1, applies the Σ0 transformation (XOR of the value rotated right by 2, 13, and 22 bits), and writes the 32‑bit result to destination register rd sign‑extended to the full XLEN width. Upper bits of rs1 are ignored and the upper XLEN‑32 bits of rd are filled with copies of the result's most‑significant bit. The instruction is usable in all privilege modes with no special access restrictions. Execution retires successfully without raising an exception when rd is a valid destination register.

INSTRUCTION: SHA256SUM1
SUMMARY: SHA256SUM1 reads the low 32 bits of integer register rs1 as the 32‑bit input for the SHA‑256 Σ1 function. It computes Σ1(inb) = ror32(inb, 6) ⊕ ror32(inb, 11) ⊕ ror32(inb, 25), sign‑extends the 32‑bit result to XLEN, and writes it to integer register rd. The instruction is available in all privilege modes, performs no special access checks, and always retires successfully without raising any defined exception.

INSTRUCTION: SHA512SIG0
SUMMARY: SHA512SIG0 is an RV64‑only instruction defined by the RVK extension that computes the SHA‑2‑512 Sigma0 function on a 64‑bit source register rs1 and writes the 64‑bit result to destination register rd. The result equals (V rotr 28) xor (V rotr 34) xor (V rotr 39), the specified Sigma0 transformation. The instruction retires successfully after updating rd, but it is undefined and reserved on RV32 implementations and is only recognized when the RVK extension is present. No other privilege or access restrictions are specified.

INSTRUCTION: SHA512SIG0H
SUMMARY: SHA512SIG0H is an RV32‑only integer instruction; it is undefined on RV64 or any implementation whose XLEN is not 32. The instruction forms a 64‑bit input by placing rs2 in the high 32 bits and rs1 in the low 32 bits, computes the high‑half of the SHA‑512 Σ0 transformation, and writes the 32‑bit (sign‑extended) result to rd. It has no memory side effects and is available in all privilege modes. Execution always retires successfully (RETIRE_SUCCESS) and does not raise an exception under normal operation.

INSTRUCTION: SHA512SIG0L
SUMMARY: The SHA512SIG0L instruction is only defined for RV32 implementations that include the Zknh extension; executing it on any other core or on any RV64 core raises an illegal‑instruction exception. It reads the low 32 bits of source registers rs1 and rs2, computes the low‑half of the SHA‑512 σ₀ function, and writes the sign‑extended 32‑bit result to destination register rd. If the value written to rd does not match the specified σ₀ computation, the instruction fails to retire and triggers an illegal‑instruction exception. No other architectural effects are defined.

INSTRUCTION: SHA512SIG1
SUMMARY: The SHA512SIG1 instruction reads a 64‑bit value from source register rs1, applies the SHA‑512 Σ1 transformation (rotate‑right 19, rotate‑right 61, and logical right‑shift 6), and writes the 64‑bit result to destination register rd. The instruction is available in all privilege modes with no special access restrictions. It never raises an architectural exception; execution always completes successfully.

INSTRUCTION: SHA512SIG1H
SUMMARY: SHA512SIG1H is an RV32‑only integer instruction; it is undefined on RV64 implementations. The instruction treats the concatenation of rs1 (low 32 bits) and rs2 (high 32 bits) as a 64‑bit input to the SHA‑512 Σ1 function and computes the high 32‑bit half of the result using the prescribed bitwise operations. The computed 32‑bit value is sign‑extended and written to rd. The instruction is available in all privilege modes and raises an illegal‑instruction exception on RV64 or any implementation lacking the SHA‑2 extension.

INSTRUCTION: SHA512SIG1L
SUMMARY: The SHA512SIG1L instruction is defined exclusively for RV32 implementations and is not provided on RV64 or RV128 cores, even if the K extension is present. It computes the Sigma1 function and writes the low 32 bits of the result, sign‑extended to 64 bits, into the destination register rd. The operation updates rd with this low half of the Sigma1 transformation. With valid operand registers, the instruction retires without raising any architectural exception.

INSTRUCTION: SHA512SUM0
SUMMARY: SHA512SUM0 reads a 64‑bit value from source register rs1, rotates it right by 28, 34, and 39 bits, XORs the three rotated results, and writes the 64‑bit outcome to destination register rd. The instruction has no special privilege requirements or side‑effects and does not raise any defined exceptions.

INSTRUCTION: SHA512SUM0R
SUMMARY: SHA512SUM0R reads two source registers rs1 and rs2, zero‑extends each to 64 bits and applies the SHA‑512 Σ0 transformation to the combined 64‑bit value. The 64‑bit result is truncated to its low 32 bits, which are written to the destination register rd. The instruction is defined only for RV32 implementations; on RV64 the opcode is reserved and triggers an illegal‑instruction exception. No additional privilege or access‑control restrictions are specified.

INSTRUCTION: SHA512SUM1
SUMMARY: SHA512SUM1 is an RV64‑only instruction that takes two source registers, rotates the 64‑bit value in rs1 right by 14, 18, and 41 bits, XORs those three results, adds the 64‑bit value in rs2, and writes the modulo‑2^64 sum to the destination register rd. The operation is permitted in all privilege modes and must retire without raising any architectural exception when encoded legally. Executing the instruction on an RV32 implementation is undefined. An implementation that produces a result differing from ((rotr(rs1,14) ⊕ rotr(rs1,18) ⊕ rotr(rs1,41)) + rs2) mod 2^64 does not conform to the specified SHA‑512 Σ1 function.

INSTRUCTION: SHA512SUM1R
SUMMARY: The SHA512SUM1R instruction is available only on RV32 implementations and performs the SHA‑2‑512 Sum1 transformation on its source operands, placing the 64‑bit result into the destination register. Its encoding is undefined on RV64, where execution triggers an illegal‑instruction exception; on RV32 the instruction retires normally without raising any exception. No special privilege or access‑control restrictions are specified beyond the implementation‑size constraint. The instruction does not affect any architectural state other than the destination register.

INSTRUCTION: SINVAL.VMA
SUMMARY: SINVAL.VMA is a privileged instruction whose destination register field rd is hard‑wired to x0, so it never writes an architectural register. It may be executed only in S‑mode (or HS‑mode) when mstatus.TVM = 0; executing it in U‑mode or in any privileged mode with TVM = 1 raises an illegal‑instruction exception. When permitted, the instruction invalidates address translations using the same permission checks as SFENCE.VMA. If VTVM = 1, a virtual‑instruction exception is raised for SINVAL.VMA under the same conditions that would raise it for SFENCE.VMA.

INSTRUCTION: SLL
SUMMARY: The SLL instruction reads source registers rs1 and rs2 and writes the shifted result to destination register rd. It performs a logical left shift of the value in rs1 by a shift amount taken from the low‑order bits of rs2 (the lower five bits in RV32I, the lower six bits in RV64I). No special privilege or access‑control checks are required for this operation. Vector logical‑shift‑left instructions (VSLL.VV, VSLL.VX) apply the same element‑wise shift semantics, inserting zeros into the least‑significant bits and producing zero when the shift amount equals or exceeds the element width.

INSTRUCTION: SLLI
SUMMARY: The SLLI instruction shifts the value in source register rs1 left by a shift amount taken from the low‑order bits of the I‑immediate (shamt[4:0] in RV32I, shamt[5:0] in RV64I), with the upper bits of the immediate required to be zero. The shift is logical, inserting zeros into the vacated low‑order positions. The shifted result is written to the destination register rd. The instruction has no special privilege requirements and ignores bit 30 of the encoding.

INSTRUCTION: SLLI.UW
SUMMARY: SLLI.UW reads the source register rs1, extracts its least‑significant 32‑bit word, zero‑extends that word to the processor's XLEN, and logically left‑shifts the extended value by the immediate shift amount (shamt), writing the XLEN‑wide result to destination register rd. The immediate field supplies the shamt directly, and the operation is equivalent to an slli applied to the zero‑extended 32‑bit value of rs1. No special privilege level or access‑control checks are required; the instruction behaves like any other arithmetic‑logic operation and only raises the standard illegal‑instruction exception if the implementation does not support it.

INSTRUCTION: SLLIW
SUMMARY: The SLLIW instruction reads the low‑32‑bit word of the source register, left‑shifts it logically by the amount given in the low‑5 bits of the immediate, sign‑extends the 32‑bit result to 64 bits, and writes the value to the destination register. It is defined only when the immediate's bit 5 is zero; otherwise the encoding is reserved. No special privilege checks are required for its execution. Encodings with imm[5]≠0 do not raise an illegal‑instruction exception but are treated as reserved.

INSTRUCTION: SLLW
SUMMARY: SLLW is defined only for RV64I implementations; executing it on a core that does not support RV64I raises an illegal‑instruction exception (mcause = 2). The instruction reads the low‑32 bits of rs1 and uses the low‑5 bits of rs2 as the shift amount to perform a logical left shift, discarding any bits that overflow. The 32‑bit shift result is sign‑extended to 64 bits and written to rd, overwriting its previous architectural value.

INSTRUCTION: SLT
SUMMARY: SLT compares the signed values in source registers rs1 and rs2 and writes the 1‑bit result into the destination register rd. If rs1 < rs2 the low XLEN bits of rd receive 1; otherwise they receive 0, with any higher bits discarded. The instruction does not generate arithmetic exceptions and imposes no special privilege or access‑control restrictions.

INSTRUCTION: SLTI
SUMMARY: SLTI compares the signed value in source register rs1 with a sign‑extended immediate and writes the result to destination register rd. If rs1 < imm, rd receives the integer 1; otherwise rd receives 0. The instruction does not raise any arithmetic exception and imposes no special privilege or access‑control restrictions. The write targets a standard integer register in the current architectural state.

INSTRUCTION: SLTIU
SUMMARY: SLTIU reads an unsigned integer from source register rs1 and a 12‑bit immediate, sign‑extends the immediate to XLEN bits, and treats the extended value as unsigned. It writes the integer 1 to destination register rd if the unsigned rs1 value is strictly less than the unsigned immediate; otherwise it writes 0. If the encoded immediate equals 1, rd is set to 1 only when rs1 is zero, and to 0 otherwise. The instruction is unprivileged and may be executed in any privilege mode.

INSTRUCTION: SLTU
SUMMARY: SLTU writes a 0 or 1 result into rd based on an unsigned comparison of rs1 and rs2. The comparison is performed on XLEN‑bit values; in RV64I the operands are treated as 64‑bit unsigned integers, and when 32‑bit values are sign‑extended to 64 bits the outcome matches a pure 32‑bit comparison. If rs1 is the zero register, rd receives 1 when the unsigned rs2 value is non‑zero, otherwise 0. No privilege checks or additional side effects are defined.

INSTRUCTION: SM3P0
SUMMARY: The SM3P0 instruction reads a source register (r1), applies the SM3 P0 transform, and writes back the result computed as r1 ⊕ rol32(r1, 9) ⊕ rol32(r1, 17). It is only defined on implementations that include the SM3P0 extension; on a processor without that extension the instruction triggers an illegal‑instruction exception. The instruction may be executed in any privilege mode, but it must retire with RETIRE_SUCCESS; otherwise a trap is taken and mcause is set to a non‑zero value.

INSTRUCTION: SM3P1
SUMMARY: The SM3P1 instruction reads a 32‑bit operand from the low‑32 bits of source register rs1 (r1) and writes the sign‑extended 32‑bit result to destination register rd. The result is computed as r1 XOR rol32(r1,15) XOR rol32(r1,23), i.e., the SM3 P1 transform defined by the SM3 algorithm. Implementations that include the Zksh or Zks extensions must provide SM3P1 for both RV32 and RV64 base ISA variants, and the instruction retires normally when rs1 and rd are valid registers. If any bits above the low‑32 of rs1 are used, rd will contain an incorrect value that differs from the defined SM3P1 result.

INSTRUCTION: SM4ED
SUMMARY: SM4ED reads a 32‑bit word from a source register, extracts the least‑significant byte after shifting right by a shift amount equal to bs × 8, applies the SM4 S‑Box to that byte, and forms a 32‑bit value x whose upper 24 bits are zero. It then computes y = x ⊕ (x<<8) ⊕ (x<<2) ⊕ (x<<18) ⊕ ((x & 0x3F)<<26) ⊕ ((x & 0xC0)<<10), rotates y left by the same shamt to obtain z, and writes z to the destination register (sign‑extended to XLEN on RV64). The instruction retires without exception only when all architectural preconditions hold; otherwise an exception is raised and the instruction does not commit. No special privilege level is required, and the operation involves only register reads and writes.

INSTRUCTION: SM4KS
SUMMARY: The SM4KS instruction reads rs2, extracts a byte selected by the bs field, applies the SM4 S‑box, and forms a zero‑extended 32‑bit intermediate value x; it then computes y by XOR‑masking x with a fixed pattern, rotates y left by bs·8 bits to obtain z, and writes rd with z XOR the low 32 bits of rs1. On RV64 implementations the 32‑bit result is sign‑extended to XLEN before being stored. The instruction has no special privilege or access‑control requirements, and any destination value that does not match the defined computation is a violation.

INSTRUCTION: SRA
SUMMARY: SRA reads integer register rs1 as the value to be shifted and integer register rs2 as the source of the shift amount. The shift amount is taken from the low‑order five bits of rs2 in RV32 or the low‑order six bits of rs2 in RV64, ensuring it is within the operand width. The instruction performs an arithmetic right shift of rs1, replicating the sign bit into the vacated high‑order bits, and writes the sign‑extended result to rd. No privilege checks are required, and the masking of rs2 prevents any shift‑amount exception.

INSTRUCTION: SRAI
SUMMARY: SRAI reads the value from source register rs1 and writes the shifted result to destination register rd. The shift amount is taken from the low‑order bits of the immediate field (five bits in RV32I, six bits in RV64I). The operation performs an arithmetic right shift, copying the original sign bit into all vacated upper bits of the result. The instruction is unprivileged and does not raise any architectural exceptions.

INSTRUCTION: SRAIW
SUMMARY: SRAIW is defined only for RV64I implementations and shifts a 32‑bit source register right arithmetically by an amount taken from the low six bits of the immediate, writing the result to a destination register. Encodings with imm[5]=1 are reserved; they must not raise an illegal‑instruction exception and their result is undefined. The 32‑bit shifted value is sign‑extended to 64 bits before being written back. The instruction has no privilege restrictions and may be executed in any mode.

INSTRUCTION: SRAW
SUMMARY: The SRAW instruction reads the 32‑bit value in source register rs1 and a shift amount taken from the low five bits of source register rs2. It performs an arithmetic right shift of rs1 by that amount, sign‑extends the 32‑bit result to 64 bits, and writes the result to destination register rd. The instruction is allowed in all privilege modes and does not involve memory accesses, so it generates no alignment or page‑fault exceptions. An illegal‑instruction exception is raised only if the processor does not implement the SRAW opcode.

INSTRUCTION: SRET
SUMMARY: SRET returns from a supervisor‑mode trap by loading the program counter from the sepc CSR and restoring the previous privilege level from the SPP field of sstatus, while simultaneously clearing SPP, copying SPIE to SIE, and setting SPIE to 1. It also clears MPRV (and, when the new mode is not M, clears SDT), and if virtualization is disabled (V = 0) restores V from SPV and resets SPV. The instruction is only legal in S‑ or M‑mode; it raises an illegal‑instruction exception when executed in U‑mode, when supervisor mode is not implemented, or when the TSR bit (in mstatus or the TSR field of sstatus) is set. After execution, interrupt‑trap conditions are evaluated immediately.

INSTRUCTION: SRL
SUMMARY: SRL reads the source registers rs1 and rs2, uses the low‑order 6 bits of rs2 as the shift amount (in RV64I), performs a logical right shift of the value in rs1, and writes the zero‑extended result to destination register rd. The operation inserts zeros into the vacated high‑order bits and is available in all privilege modes. The shift amount may also be supplied from a scalar x‑register.

INSTRUCTION: SRLI
SUMMARY: SRLI reads a value from source register rs1, shifts it right by a shift amount taken from the immediate (the lower 6 bits in RV64I), and writes the zero‑filled logical‑right‑shift result into destination register rd. The instruction does not modify any other architectural state and is not privileged, so it can execute in any mode. When it follows an SLLI that has shifted a 16‑bit value left, the SRLI result is zero‑extended to 16 bits. All vacated high‑order bits are filled with zeros.

INSTRUCTION: SRLIW
SUMMARY: SRLIW reads a 32‑bit value from source register rs1, logically right‑shifts it by a shift amount taken from the low six bits of the I‑type immediate, inserts zeros into the vacated upper bits, then sign‑extends the 32‑bit result to 64 bits and writes it to destination register rd. The instruction is part of the RV64I base ISA and may be executed in any privilege mode that permits ordinary integer arithmetic. No architectural exceptions are raised; the shift amount is automatically masked to six bits.

INSTRUCTION: SRLW
SUMMARY: The SRLW instruction reads source register rs1, uses the low‑5 bits of rs2 as the shift amount, and writes the result to destination register rd. The lower 32 bits of rd contain rs1 logically right‑shifted by that amount, and the upper bits of rd are cleared to zero (the 32‑bit result is zero‑extended to the full XLEN). SRLW is an unprivileged integer instruction and is available in all privilege modes. No special exceptions or additional access controls are specified.

INSTRUCTION: SSAMOSWAP.D
SUMMARY: SSAMOSWAP.D atomically exchanges a 64‑bit doubleword at the address in rs1 with the value in rs2, writing the original memory value to rd (the load is discarded when rd = x0). Execution is allowed only when the current privilege mode and the SSE enable fields (menvcfg.SSE and, in U‑mode, senvcfg.SSE) permit shadow‑stack operations; otherwise an illegal‑instruction or virtual‑instruction exception is raised, and the address must be naturally 8‑byte aligned. The memory access must target a shadow‑stack page with read‑write permission, idempotent storage, and appropriate PMP and PMA support, while respecting the standard U/SUM checks (MXR does not affect read permission on such pages); violations generate a store/AMO access‑fault, page‑fault, or guest‑page‑fault as appropriate. When any exception occurs the cause code reports a store/AMO fault and the instruction is classified as a store/AMO operation.

INSTRUCTION: SSAMOSWAP.W
SUMMARY: SSAMOSWAP.W atomically loads a 32‑bit word from the address in rs1, writes the sign‑extended original value to rd, and stores the low 32 bits of rs2 to the same location (address masked and required to be four‑byte aligned). Execution is allowed only in Machine mode, or in lower‑privilege modes when the appropriate SSE enable bits (menvcfg.SSE for non‑M modes and senvcfg.SSE for U‑mode) are set; otherwise an illegal‑instruction exception is raised, and when menvcfg.SSE=1 a virtual‑instruction exception is also generated. The store may target a shadow‑stack page without fault, but any unaligned access, access to a non‑idempotent region, lack of PMP read‑write permission, missing PMA AMOSwap support, a Bare satp translation, or a target outside a shadow‑stack page causes a store/AMO access‑fault exception. The instruction requires the Zicfiss extension; if it is not implemented the core raises an illegal‑instruction exception.

INSTRUCTION: SSPOPCHK.X5
SUMMARY: SSPOPCHK.X5 requires x5 as its rs1 operand and, when the CSR xSSE is 1, loads an XLEN‑wide value from the address in the shadow‑stack pointer register ssp using normal load semantics. It compares the loaded value to x5; a mismatch raises a software‑check exception (cause 18, xtval "shadow stack fault (code=3)"), while a match increments ssp by XLEN/8 only if the load completed without any load/store/AMO access‑fault. Load/store/AMO faults have higher priority than the software‑check exception. The compressed form C.SSPOPCHK x5 expands to the same instruction and exhibits identical behavior.

INSTRUCTION: SSPUSH.X1
SUMMARY: SSPUSH.X1 stores the XLEN‑wide value from integer register x1 to the address held in the architectural shadow‑stack pointer (ssp), using normal XLEN store semantics with an implicit offset of –XLEN/8. After a successful store, ssp is decremented by XLEN/8, and the decrement occurs only after the memory write completes. The instruction is executed only when the architectural xSSE flag is 1; if xSSE is 0, no memory write occurs and ssp is left unchanged. The 16‑bit compressed form C.SSPUSH x1 has identical architectural effect.

INSTRUCTION: SSPUSH.X5
SUMMARY: SSPUSH.X5 pushes the XLEN‑wide value held in register X5 onto the shadow stack. The instruction is effective only when the architectural xSSE bit is 1, causing an ordinary XLEN‑wide store to the address ssp‑(XLEN/8) with an implicit base of ssp. On a successful store the shadow‑stack pointer ssp is decremented by XLEN/8; if the store fails, ssp remains unchanged.

INSTRUCTION: SSRDP
SUMMARY: SSRDP reads the current shadow‑stack pointer (ssp) and writes its value to the integer register rd. The write is conditional on the shadow‑stack extension status bit xSSE: if xSSE = 1 the ssp value is transferred, and if xSSE = 0 the destination is cleared to zero. No other registers are accessed and the operation follows the normal integer‑register write rules.

INSTRUCTION: SUB
SUMMARY: SUB reads the two source registers rs1 and rs2 and writes the computed value to the destination register rd. The result is the XLEN‑bit two's‑complement subtraction rs1 − rs2, performed modulo 2^XLEN, with any overflow discarded and no exception raised. Only rd is updated; no other architectural state changes and the instruction is permitted in all privilege modes.

INSTRUCTION: SUBW
SUMMARY: SUBW subtracts the signed 32‑bit value in rs2 from the signed 32‑bit value in rs1, ignoring the upper 32 bits of both source registers. The 32‑bit subtraction result is sign‑extended to the full XLEN and written to rd, with any overflow discarded. The instruction is available in all privilege modes and has no special access controls. No additional side effects or exceptions are defined beyond the standard write‑back of the sign‑extended result.

INSTRUCTION: SW
SUMMARY: The SW instruction stores the low‑order 32 bits of source register rs2 to the memory location whose effective address is the sum of base register rs1 and the sign‑extended 12‑bit immediate offset. The store writes a full 32‑bit word, with byte ordering determined by the system's endianness (least‑significant byte to the lowest address in little‑endian, most‑significant byte to the lowest address in big‑endian). If the effective address is naturally aligned (a multiple of 4) no misaligned‑address exception is raised; for misaligned addresses the implementation may either perform a non‑atomic store or raise a store‑address‑misaligned exception (mcause = 6 or 0x5) unless the execution environment guarantees invisible handling of misaligned stores. Encodings that do not set the required rl bit are reserved, and when the address is obtained from a CSR the address must be masked if pointer‑masking is enabled for the current privilege mode.

INSTRUCTION: UNZIP
SUMMARY: UNZIP reads the XLEN‑bit value in source register X(rs1), de‑interleaves its bits into two contiguous halves, and writes the result to destination register X(rd) while leaving X(rs1) unchanged. The lower half of X(rd) receives the even‑indexed bits of X(rs1) and the upper half receives the odd‑indexed bits. UNZIP is defined as the inverse of the ZIP instruction, so a ZIP followed by an UNZIP (or vice‑versa) on the same operand restores the original value. The instruction has no privilege restrictions and generates no special exceptions.

INSTRUCTION: VAADD.VV
SUMMARY: VAADD.VV reads element‑wise signed values from source vector registers vs1 and vs2, adds each pair in infinite precision, right‑shifts the sum by one, rounds the result according to the current vector rounding‑mode register vxrm, and writes the rounded average to the corresponding element of destination register vd for all active elements whose mask bit is true. Elements whose mask bit is false are left unchanged in vd. The operation never raises an overflow exception; if the exact sum cannot be represented in the destination element width it is rounded to the nearest representable value. Execution in a privilege mode or vector configuration that would make the unsigned variant VAADDU.VV illegal causes VAADD.VV to raise the same illegal‑instruction or vector exception with the identical mcause code.

INSTRUCTION: VAADDU.VV
SUMMARY: VAADDU.VV reads two source vector registers (vs1, vs2) and a mask register (vm); for each element i with vm[i]=1 it forms the infinite‑precision unsigned sum of the corresponding elements, right‑shifts the sum by one bit, rounds the result according to the current vxrm rounding mode, and writes the rounded value to the destination vector register vd[i]. Elements with vm[i]=0 are left unchanged in vd. The operation never generates overflow and is defined for all operand values. It executes in the normal vector execution context with no special privilege or access‑control restrictions.

INSTRUCTION: VAADDU.VX
SUMMARY: VAADDU.VX reads an unsigned integer scalar from integer register rs1 and each unsigned integer element from source vector register vs2, producing unsigned integer results written to destination vector register vd, with an optional element‑wise mask vm applied. For each element the operation adds the scalar to the vector element, forms an infinite‑precision sum, right‑shifts the sum by one bit, and rounds the shifted value according to the current vxrm rounding‑mode setting. The instruction never generates an overflow condition, so no overflow exception can be raised. It is a regular user‑mode instruction with no special privilege or access‑control requirements.

INSTRUCTION: VADC.VIM
SUMMARY: VADC.VIM computes element‑wise addition of the source vector vs2, an immediate operand, and the corresponding mask bit of the implicit vector register v0, storing the result in vd[i] = vs2[i] + imm + v0.mask[i]. The instruction writes the result to every element of the destination register even if the destination mask bit is clear, and it uses v0.mask bits as the per‑element carry‑in for the addition. No additional privilege checks, access‑control restrictions, or exception conditions are defined by the given semantics.

INSTRUCTION: VADC.VVM
SUMMARY: VADC.VVM is a vector add‑with‑carry that reads source registers vs1 and vs2 and adds the per‑element mask bits of the implicit vector register v0 as the carry‑in, producing a sum for each element up to the current active vector length. The computed result is written to every element of the destination register vd, ignoring any element‑wise mask, and the instruction is only defined when the vector‑mask field vm is cleared (vm = 0). The implicit register v0 may be used solely for carry‑in; it cannot be specified as vd, and any encoding that sets vm = 1, uses v0 as the destination, or violates the usual source/destination overlap constraints is reserved.

INSTRUCTION: VADC.VXM
SUMMARY: VADC.VXM adds the scalar integer in x[rs1] and the mask bit from the implicit v0 register to each element of source vector register vs2, storing the result in destination vector register vd. The mask is used as a per‑element carry‑in, and the instruction writes a result for every element, ignoring the mask when deciding which elements are written back. Encoding the instruction with vd = v0 is reserved and therefore illegal. The destination may be any vector register that satisfies the normal source/destination overlap rules, with no extra privilege restrictions.

INSTRUCTION: VADD.VV
SUMMARY: VADD.VV reads two source vector register groups (vs1 and vs2) and writes the element‑wise integer sum to the destination vector register group (vd) using the current vector length and element width settings. When a mask register (vm) is present, the addition is performed only for element positions where the mask bit is true, leaving all other positions of vd unchanged. The instruction adheres to the standard vector‑unit access permissions and does not generate any special exceptions beyond those defined for ordinary vector operations.

INSTRUCTION: VADD.VX
SUMMARY: VADD.VX adds the scalar integer in integer register rs1 to each element of source vector register vs2 and writes the resulting elements to destination vector register vd. If a mask register vm is supplied, only the elements of vd whose corresponding mask bit is true are updated, while all other elements remain unchanged. The instruction uses standard integer‑ and vector‑register access with no special privilege requirements or additional exception conditions beyond the mask‑controlled element selection.

INSTRUCTION: VAESDF.VS
SUMMARY: The instruction performs a final‑round AES block‑cipher decryption and writes the new round state back to the same destination vector register group (vd) that holds the output. It is only defined for SEW = 32 and requires that the vd register group does not overlap the vs2 scalar element group; any other configuration is a reserved encoding. An illegal‑instruction exception is raised and the instruction retires with RETIRE_FAIL when LMUL × VLEN < EGW. If no exception is triggered, the instruction retires successfully (RETIRE_SUCCESS) with no special privilege requirements.

INSTRUCTION: VAESDF.VV
SUMMARY: VAESDF.VV computes a 128-bit XOR of the corresponding element groups from the source registers and writes the result into element group i of the destination vector register vd, replacing the previous round-state element. The operation is only defined when the product of LMUL and VLEN is at least 128 bits; otherwise the instruction raises an illegal-instruction exception and does not retire. It follows the standard vector-extension access rules, requiring execution in a privilege mode that permits vector instructions.

INSTRUCTION: VAESDM.VS
SUMMARY: VAESDM.VS performs the AES inverse‑MixColumns step, taking the round key from element‑0 of the scalar source register vs2 and writing the 128‑bit result into the corresponding element group of the destination vector register vd to produce the new round state. The instruction is only valid when the effective element width (LMUL × VLEN) is at least 128 bits; otherwise it triggers an illegal‑instruction exception and retires with failure. No special privilege level is required, and the instruction obeys normal user‑mode access rules for vector registers.

INSTRUCTION: VAESDM.VV
SUMMARY: VAESDM.VV treats Vd as the vector register holding 128‑bit AES round‑state element groups and Vs2 as the vector register providing the matching 128‑bit round‑key elements. The instruction is only defined when SEW = 32 and LMUL × VLEN ≥ 128 bits; otherwise the encoding is reserved or an illegal‑instruction exception is raised, and it processes ⌊VL/4⌋ groups starting at ⌊VSTART/4⌋. For each selected group it performs the inverse ShiftRows, then inverse SubBytes, XORs the result with the round‑key from Vs2, applies the inverse MixColumns, and writes the 128‑bit outcome back to the same group in Vd.

INSTRUCTION: VAESEF.VS
SUMMARY: VAESEF.VS is a vector AES‑final‑round encryption instruction that is defined only when the selected element width (SEW) is 32 bits and the vector configuration supplies at least 128 bits of storage (LMUL × VL ≥ 128); any other SEW or insufficient LMUL × VL causes a reserved encoding or an illegal‑instruction exception. For each 128‑bit element group of the destination vector register vd (starting at vstart/4 and processing vl/4 groups), the hardware applies the AES SubBytes and ShiftRows forward transformations to the current state and then XORs the result with the round key taken from element 0 of the scalar element group of source register vs2, writing the result back to the same element group of vd. The encoding is also reserved if the destination register group overlaps the scalar element group of vs2, and no additional privilege checks are specified beyond these architectural legality constraints.

INSTRUCTION: VAESEF.VV
SUMMARY: VAESEF.VV operates on 128‑bit AES round‑state vectors, reading the current state from destination register vd and the round key from source register vs2, and writes the updated state back to vd. The instruction is only defined when the selected element width (SEW) is 32 bits and the effective vector group width (LMUL × VLEN) is at least 128 bits; otherwise the encoding is reserved or an illegal‑instruction exception is raised. For each 128‑bit element group within the active vector length, the hardware applies the AES forward SubBytes, ShiftRows, and XOR with the round key. If all selected groups are processed without violating the width constraints, the instruction retires normally without further exceptions.

INSTRUCTION: VAESEM.VS
SUMMARY: VAESEM.VS is a vector‑AES round instruction that is valid only when the selected element width is 32 bits and, for the .vs form, the destination register group vd does not overlap the scalar element group of the source register vs2. The instruction raises an illegal‑instruction exception with RETIRE_FAIL if LMUL × VLEN is less than the required 128‑bit effective group width. It transforms the input round‑state by applying the AES SubBytes, ShiftRows, and MixColumns forward operations and writes the resulting state to the corresponding element group i of vd. Because the MixColumns output is not XORed with the supplied round key, the value stored in vd differs from the mathematically expected new round state.

INSTRUCTION: VAESEM.VV
SUMMARY: The VAESEM.VV instruction treats each 128‑bit element group in the destination vector register vd as a round‑state and each corresponding element group in vector register vs2 as the round‑key. If the configured LMUL × VLEN is less than 128 bits, the instruction raises an illegal‑instruction exception and fails to retire; otherwise it executes a full AES middle‑round on every element group by applying SubBytes, ShiftRows, MixColumns and XORing the result with the matching round‑key, writing the final state back into vd. The operation requires no special privilege beyond that of ordinary vector instructions. All element groups are processed independently and the original vd contents are overwritten.

INSTRUCTION: VAESKF1.VI
SUMMARY: VAESKF1.VI expands a 128‑bit AES round‑key vector by computing the next round key for each element group and writing the four resulting 32‑bit words into the destination vector register. The instruction is defined only for 32‑bit element width and uses the low‑order immediate bits as a round number, which is remapped for values 0 or >10 before being passed to aes_decode_rcon to obtain the round constant. Execution proceeds only when LMUL × VLEN supplies at least 128 bits of vector storage; otherwise an illegal‑instruction exception is raised. If the generated words are not written to the corresponding element group, that group retains incorrect or unchanged data.

INSTRUCTION: VAESKF2.VI
SUMMARY: VAESKF2.VI is defined only for 32‑bit element width; encodings with any other SEW are reserved. The instruction requires LMUL × VLEN ≥ 128, otherwise it raises an illegal‑instruction exception and retires as a failure. It takes a 5‑bit immediate where the low 4 bits specify a round number that is normalized to the range 2‑14 by inverting bit 3 for values 0‑1 or 15, and uses the destination vector register (vd) as the previous round key and the source vector register (vs2) as the current round key, producing the next round key (four 32‑bit words) back into vd for each element group processed. Processing starts at the element‑group index vstart/4 and proceeds for vl/4 groups, with all intermediate arithmetic performed on 32‑bit words.

INSTRUCTION: VAESZ.VS
SUMMARY: VAESZ.VS is a vector AES‑round instruction that XORs each 128‑bit element group of the destination register group (vd) with the corresponding 128‑bit round‑key element group from the source register group (vs2). It is only defined for an effective element width of 32 bits, an effective group width of 128 bits, an element‑group size of four elements, and the vd and vs2 groups must not overlap. Execution starts at the element‑group index vstart/EGS and proceeds through (vl/EGS)‑1, updating vd and retiring normally if no error occurs. Any mismatch of widths, group size, vstart alignment, insufficient LMUL·VL, or register overlap raises an illegal‑instruction exception and the instruction does not retire.

INSTRUCTION: VAND.VI
SUMMARY: VAND.VI writes the bitwise AND of each active element of source vector register vs2 with the immediate imm into the destination vector register vd. An optional mask register vm may be provided; when present, only elements whose mask bit is set are updated, otherwise all active elements are written. The operation follows the usual vector masking semantics and does not introduce additional architectural exceptions. Supplying an immediate that is the bitwise inverse of a constant produces the same architectural effect as the corresponding VANDN.vi form.

INSTRUCTION: VASUB.VV
SUMMARY: VASUB.VV subtracts each element of the first source vector (vs1) from the corresponding element of the second source vector (vs2), then right‑shifts the infinite‑precision result by one bit with rounding according to the current vxrm setting, and writes the rounded value to the destination vector register (vd). Any result that falls outside the signed‑integer range of the element wraps around modulo the element width. Overflow can only occur when the largest signed element is subtracted by the most negative signed element while vxrm is set to rnu or rne, in which case the wrap‑around rule applies. The instruction is a standard vector arithmetic operation with no special privilege or access‑control restrictions.

INSTRUCTION: VASUB.VX
SUMMARY: VASUB.VX performs an element‑wise signed subtraction of the scalar integer in rs1 from each element of the source vector register vs2, right‑shifts the infinite‑precision result by one bit, rounds it according to the rounding mode in the vxrm CSR, and writes the final value to the corresponding element of the destination vector register vd. The operation is defined as a saturating subtraction, but any arithmetic overflow is ignored and the result wraps modulo the element width. No other architectural state is accessed, and the instruction follows the standard privilege and vector‑unit access rules for vector arithmetic instructions.

INSTRUCTION: VASUBU.VX
SUMMARY: VASUBU.VX reads an unsigned scalar from rs1 and each unsigned element from source vector register vs2, computes the infinite‑precision subtraction of the scalar from each element, right‑shifts the result by one bit, rounds the shifted value according to the unsigned rounding mode encoded in the vxrm field, and then truncates to the element width, wrapping around modulo the element size. The final values are written to the destination vector register vd. The instruction's behavior is governed solely by the vxrm rounding‑mode field; no other control bits affect its operation. It is available to all privilege levels and has no special access‑control restrictions, with overflow beyond the element width simply discarded.

INSTRUCTION: VBREV.V
SUMMARY: VBREV.V reads each SEW‑bit element from the source vector register vs2, reverses the order of its bits, and writes the resulting element to the corresponding position in the destination vector register vd. The bit‑reversal is performed only for element indices from the current vector start index vstart up to vl‑1, and elements outside this range are left unchanged. The operation is applied independently to each active element. No explicit privilege checks or exception conditions are defined for this instruction.

INSTRUCTION: VBREV8.V
SUMMARY: VBREV8.V reads a source vector register, reverses the order of the eight bits in each byte of every element (as defined by the current SEW) while preserving byte positions, and writes the resulting bytes to a destination vector register. The transformation is performed only for element indices between vstart and vl; elements outside this active range remain unchanged. The instruction is valid only when the Zvkb extension is implemented, otherwise its encoding is reserved and triggers an illegal‑instruction trap. If all architectural preconditions are satisfied, the instruction retires normally with no exception.

INSTRUCTION: VCLMUL.VV
SUMMARY: VCLMUL.VV is a vector instruction that reads two source vector registers, treats each active element as a 64‑bit operand, performs a carry‑less multiplication, and writes the low‑64‑bit half of the 128‑bit product into the corresponding element of a destination vector register. The operation is only defined when the selected element width (SEW) is 64 bits; encodings with any other SEW are reserved and will trigger an illegal‑instruction fault. With SEW = 64 the instruction executes and retires normally without raising any architectural exception. It follows the standard vector‑unit access rules and imposes no additional privilege restrictions.

INSTRUCTION: VCLMUL.VX
SUMMARY: VCLMUL.VX is a vector instruction that operates only when the selected element width is 64 bits; other SEW encodings are reserved. For each element index i from the current vstart up to vl‑1, it computes the 128‑bit carry‑less product of the corresponding vector element and the scalar source, and writes the low 64 bits of that product into the destination vector register vd, leaving all other vd elements unchanged. The instruction uses the standard vector registers and respects the current vstart/vl controls, with no special privilege requirements. Execution completes without raising any exceptions and retires normally.

INSTRUCTION: VCLMULH.VV
SUMMARY: VCLMULH.VV computes, for each active element i (vstart ≤ i < vl), the most‑significant 64 bits of the 128‑bit carry‑less product of the corresponding 64‑bit elements of source registers vs1 and vs2, writing the result into destination vector register vd. The instruction is only defined when the selected element width (SEW) is 64; any other SEW makes the encoding reserved and triggers an illegal‑instruction fault. No architectural exceptions are raised provided all active elements are processed according to the algorithm, and the instruction retires normally. No special privilege or access‑control restrictions are specified.

INSTRUCTION: VCLMULH.VX
SUMMARY: VCLMULH.VX multiplies a 64‑bit scalar from integer register rs1 with each 64‑bit element of the vector source register vs2, producing the most‑significant 64 bits of the 128‑bit carry‑less product and writing that value to the matching element of destination vector register vd. The operation is defined only for a vector element width (SEW) of 64 bits; encodings selecting any other SEW are reserved. No special privilege or access‑control restrictions are imposed beyond the normal vector‑instruction rules. If any active element is not processed, the instruction fails to retire with RETIRE_SUCCESS.

INSTRUCTION: VCLZ.V
SUMMARY: VCLZ.V reads each active element of source vector register vs2, using the current SEW to define element width, and writes to the corresponding element of destination vector register vd the count of leading‑zero bits. For a non‑zero element the count is SEW‑1 minus the index of the first 1‑bit encountered from the most‑significant side; for an all‑zero element the result is SEW. It processes elements i with vstart <= i < vl and leaves elements i >= vl unchanged. The instruction is an ordinary unprivileged vector operation and retires with a successful status, raising no defined exceptions.

INSTRUCTION: VCOMPRESS.VM
SUMMARY: VCOMPRESS.VM reads a mask register (vs1) and a source vector register group (vs2), selecting the first vl elements of vs2 whose corresponding mask bits are true and packs them into the low‑order elements of the destination vector register group (vd). Elements of vd that are not written become tail elements according to the current tail‑policy. The instruction is only defined when the vm field is 1 and the destination group does not overlap either the source vector group or the mask register; any overlapping configuration is reserved. If a trap occurs, the architectural vstart value is reported as 0.

INSTRUCTION: VCPOP.M
SUMMARY: VCPOP.M counts the number of 1‑bits in a source mask vector and writes the integer result to the scalar register x[rd]; if a mask register vm is supplied, only elements with vm true are counted. The operation produces a result even when the current vector length is zero, writing zero in that case. Execution is illegal if the vstart CSR is non‑zero, and any trap caused by the instruction reports vstart as zero. The instruction's behavior is independent of the Zvkt extension.

INSTRUCTION: VCPOP.V
SUMMARY: VCPOP.V reads each element of source vector register vs2, counts the number of 1‑bits in the element, and writes that population‑count value to the corresponding element of destination vector register vd using the current SEW width. The instruction processes elements starting at the current vstart index up to the active vector length vl‑1. Results are stored with SEW‑bit width per element. Execution always retires successfully (RETIRE_SUCCESS) with no architectural exception.

INSTRUCTION: VCTZ.V
SUMMARY: VCTZ.V reads each SEW‑sized element from source vector register vs2 and writes to destination vector register vd the count of consecutive zero bits starting at the element's least‑significant bit. Elements with index i < vstart or i ≥ vl yield an unpredictable result; if an element contains no one‑bits the value SEW is written, otherwise the position j of the first one‑bit is written. The operation uses the same element width for source and destination, imposes no special privilege restrictions, and retires without raising an exception after processing all active elements.

INSTRUCTION: VDIV.VV
SUMMARY: VDIV.VV is a signed vector‑vector divide that reads the dividend from source vector register vs2 and the divisor from source vector register vs1, writes the element‑wise quotients to destination vector register vd, and may be masked by vm. For each element where the mask bit is 1, the instruction computes the signed quotient of vs2[i] divided by vs1[i] with truncation toward zero, yielding exactly the same result as the scalar signed‑division operation for all operand values. Elements whose mask bit is 0 are left unchanged in vd. If a divisor element is zero the result is all‑1s (‑1) for that element width, and if the dividend is the most‑negative integer and the divisor is –1 the result is the most‑negative integer (no overflow).

INSTRUCTION: VDIV.VX
SUMMARY: VDIV.VX is a vector‑scalar operation that divides each element of source vector register vs2 by the signed integer in scalar register rs1, writes the signed quotient to the corresponding element of destination vector register vd, and uses the vector mask vm to select active elements. The division follows the standard RISC‑V signed‑division rules, producing exactly the same result as a scalar signed‑divide for each active element, including all edge cases. If rs1 is zero the instruction writes –1 (all‑ones) to each active element, and if an active dividend is the most‑negative representable integer while rs1 is –1 it writes that most‑negative value, matching the defined overflow result; no special privilege level is required beyond normal vector‑instruction access.

INSTRUCTION: VDIVU.VV
SUMMARY: VD is written with the element‑wise unsigned integer quotients of the corresponding elements of VS2 divided by VS1. The instruction reads the source vector registers VS2 (dividend) and VS1 (divisor) and writes the result vector VD, all using the standard vector register file. It executes in unprivileged mode with no special access restrictions. For each element the produced quotient is defined to be identical to the result of the scalar unsigned divide instruction, even for extreme input values.

INSTRUCTION: VDIVU.VX
SUMMARY: VDIVU.VX reads unsigned integer elements from source vector register vs2 and an unsigned integer scalar from rs1, divides each vs2 element by the scalar, and writes the unsigned quotients to the corresponding elements of destination vector register vd, updating only those elements enabled by the vector mask vm. The operation is performed element‑wise under the current vector‑length setting. Results, including the defined outcomes for division‑by‑zero or overflow, must match those of the scalar unsigned‑integer divide instruction. Execution is allowed in any privilege mode that permits vector arithmetic and does not impose additional access restrictions.

INSTRUCTION: VFADD.VV
SUMMARY: VFADD.VV adds the corresponding elements of the two source vector registers vs1 and vs2 and writes the results to the destination vector register. The optional mask register vm controls which destination elements are updated, with masked‑off elements left unchanged. The operation executes under the current privilege level with no special access restrictions mentioned. No exception behavior is defined by the given rules.

INSTRUCTION: VFCVT.F.X.V
SUMMARY: VFCVT.F.X.V reads signed‑integer elements from source vector register vs2 and writes converted floating‑point elements of the same SEW to destination vector register vd. The conversion for all elements follows the dynamic rounding mode specified in the frm field. If an element would cause an overflow or other invalid condition in a scalar signed‑integer‑to‑floating‑point conversion, the same floating‑point exception flags are set in fflags and the element result matches the scalar conversion outcome. The instruction is subject to the normal vector‑instruction privilege rules and requires no special access rights.

INSTRUCTION: VFCVT.F.XU.V
SUMMARY: VFCVT.F.XU.V converts each unsigned‑integer element in source vector register vs2 to a floating‑point element of the same SEW‑width in destination vector register vd. The dynamic rounding mode is taken from the frm field; there is no rounding‑to‑zero variant. The instruction is unprivileged and may be executed in any privilege mode without additional access control. It raises the standard floating‑point exception flags (invalid, overflow, underflow, inexact) exactly as the equivalent scalar unsigned‑integer‑to‑float conversion.

INSTRUCTION: VFCVT.RTZ.X.F.V
SUMMARY: The instruction converts each floating‑point element in source vector register vs2 to a signed integer element of the same width and writes the result to the corresponding element of destination vector register vd. An optional mask register vm may be used; elements whose mask bit is zero are left unchanged in vd. Conversions always round toward zero, ignoring the dynamic rounding mode in frm, and the source and destination element widths (SEW) must be identical. Any overflow, NaN, or inexact result is handled according to the architectural rules for scalar floating‑point‑to‑integer conversions, with no special privilege requirements beyond normal access.

INSTRUCTION: VFCVT.RTZ.XU.F.V
SUMMARY: The instruction reads floating‑point elements from source vector register vs2, converts each to an unsigned integer by truncating toward zero, and writes the results to destination vector register vd. The conversion always uses round‑toward‑zero semantics, ignoring the dynamic rounding mode field frm. It follows the same architectural exception handling as scalar floating‑point‑to‑unsigned‑integer conversions (e.g., overflow, NaN, or out‑of‑range values raise the appropriate flags and produce defined results). No special privilege or access restrictions beyond those of normal vector operations apply.

INSTRUCTION: VFCVT.X.F.V
SUMMARY: The instruction reads a floating‑point vector register and writes a signed‑integer vector register, converting each SEW‑wide element of the source to the corresponding integer element of the destination. All element conversions use the current floating‑point rounding mode held in the frm register and follow the same exception‑generation rules (invalid, overflow, inexact, etc.) defined for the scalar floating‑point‑to‑signed‑integer conversion instructions. No special privilege or access‑control checks beyond normal vector‑instruction permissions are required.

INSTRUCTION: VFCVT.XU.F.V
SUMMARY: The instruction reads SEW‑wide floating‑point elements from source vector register vs2 and writes the corresponding unsigned‑integer results of the same SEW into destination vector register vd. It performs the conversion using the dynamic rounding mode specified by the frm field. Any NaN, overflow, or inexact condition is reported and handled exactly as for the scalar floating‑point‑to‑unsigned‑integer conversion instructions. The operation is unprivileged and can be executed in any privilege mode that permits vector instructions.

INSTRUCTION: VFDIV.VF
SUMMARY: VFDIV.VF divides each element of the source vector register vs2 by the scalar floating‑point value in scalar register rs1, writing the results to destination vector register vd. An optional mask register vm can be supplied; elements with a cleared mask bit are not written and the corresponding vd elements retain their prior values. If no mask is provided, all element positions are treated as active and a result is produced for every element of vd. The instruction follows the standard privilege and access‑control checks applied to vector floating‑point operations.

INSTRUCTION: VFDIV.VV
SUMMARY: VFDIV.VV divides each element of source vector register vs2 (dividend) by the corresponding element of source vector register vs1 (divisor) and writes the results to destination vector register vd. An optional mask register vm may be supplied, and when present the standard vector‑mask semantics determine which element results are written. The instruction follows the normal vector‑execution privilege model with no additional access restrictions. No special exception behavior is defined beyond the base ISA's standard floating‑point exception handling.

INSTRUCTION: VFIRST.M
SUMMARY: VFIRST.M reads the mask vector vs2 and writes an integer result to the destination integer register x[rd]; it returns the index of the first active element (value 1) or –1 if no element is active, including when the current vector length vl is zero. The instruction always writes a result, and the result is –1 in the zero‑length case. Execution is illegal if the architectural vstart register is non‑zero, and any trap generated by the instruction reports vstart as zero. The operation is unaffected by the Zvkt extension and carries no special privilege requirements beyond normal integer‑register access.

INSTRUCTION: VFMACC.VF
SUMMARY: VFMACC.VF operates on a scalar floating‑point value supplied in integer register rs1, a source vector in vs2, and a destination vector vd, optionally gated by a mask register vm. For each element i where vm[i] is true (or for all i when no mask is present), the scalar is multiplied by vs2[i], the product is added to the existing vd[i], the fused result is rounded once using the current floating‑point rounding mode (frm), and the rounded sum overwrites vd[i]. Elements masked off are left unchanged in the destination vector.

INSTRUCTION: VFMACC.VV
SUMMARY: VFMACC.VV uses a destination vector register (vd) as both accumulator and result, reading two source vectors of widened FP32 values (vs1 and vs2) and a mask register (vm) to control element updates. For each element i < VL with vm[i] true, it computes the fused multiply‑accumulate (vs1[i] * vs2[i]) + vd[i], rounds the sum according to the current frm rounding mode, and writes the rounded value back to vd[i]; elements with vm[i] false or i ≥ VL are left unchanged. The operation raises the standard IEEE‑754 floating‑point exceptions (overflow, underflow, inexact, invalid) under the same conditions as vfwmaccbf16.vv.
