It is a regular vector‑extension instruction and may be executed in any privilege mode that permits vector register access.

INSTRUCTION: VFMADD.VF
SUMMARY: VFMADD.VF is a vector‑scalar fused multiply‑add that uses each element of the destination vector register as the multiplicand, multiplies it by a scalar source, adds a second vector source, and writes the fused result back to the same destination register. The operation is performed in a single‑width fused manner, so the intermediate product and sum are not rounded until the final result is stored, yielding only one rounding step per element. The instruction follows the normal vector‑extension privilege checks and can raise only the standard floating‑point exception flags after the final rounding.

INSTRUCTION: VFMAX.VF
SUMMARY: VFMAX.VF computes the element‑wise maximumNumber between each active element of source vector register vs2 and the scalar floating‑point value in register rs1, storing the results in destination vector register vd. An element is active only if it is within the current vector length and, when a mask register vm is present, its corresponding mask bit is set. The maximumNumber operation follows the definition given for scalar floating‑point maximum instructions in the RISC‑V F/D/Q extensions (v2.2). The instruction is subject to the same privilege and access checks as other vector‑floating‑point operations, with no additional exceptions beyond those defined for the maximumNumber semantics.

INSTRUCTION: VFMAX.VV
SUMMARY: VFMAX.VV reads element‑wise from source vector registers vs1 and vs2 and writes the selected value to destination vector register vd, processing only the elements marked active by the current VL/VM configuration. For each active element it computes the floating‑point maximumNumber, selecting the greater operand according to the scalar F/D/Q maximum rule (including the prescribed NaN handling). The instruction follows the normal vector‑extension access permissions (usable in any mode where the V‑extension is enabled) and raises the same floating‑point exception flags that the equivalent scalar maximum instruction would generate.

INSTRUCTION: VFMERGE.VFM
SUMMARY: VFMERGE.VFM merges a scalar floating‑point register (rs1) with a vector register (vs2) into a destination vector register (vd) on a per‑element basis. It is encoded with vm = 0 and a vs2 field that is not v0, causing the instruction to operate on every active element i where vstart ≤ i < vl, independent of mask control. For each such element, a mask bit of 0 copies vs2[i] to vd[i], while a mask bit of 1 copies the scalar rs1 value to vd[i]. No special privilege level or additional exception behavior is required beyond the normal vector‑length and mask semantics.

INSTRUCTION: VFMIN.VF
SUMMARY: VFMIN.VF reads a source vector register group (vs) and a scalar floating‑point register (fs) and writes the result to a destination vector register group (vd). For each element whose mask is active, the hardware computes the floating‑point minimumNumber of the vector element and the scalar and stores that value in the corresponding element of vd; elements whose mask is inactive are left unchanged. The operation follows the scalar floating‑point minimumNumber definition from the RISC‑V F/D/Q extensions, including the specified NaN handling. Execution is allowed in any privilege mode that grants vector and floating‑point access, and the instruction is illegal if the required extensions are not implemented.

INSTRUCTION: VFMIN.VV
SUMMARY: VFMIN.VV reads vector registers vs1 and vs2 and writes the result to vector register vd, using mask register vm to select active elements. For each element where the mask bit is true, the instruction computes the floating‑point minimum of the corresponding elements of vs2 and vs1 and stores it in the matching element of vd; elements with a false mask bit are left unchanged. The minimum calculation follows the same NaN, signed‑zero, and infinity handling as the scalar floating‑point minimum defined in the RISC‑V F/D/Q spec version 2.2. It is an ordinary unprivileged vector operation and raises no architectural exceptions beyond those inherent to the scalar min semantics.

INSTRUCTION: VFMSAC.VV
SUMMARY: VFMSAC.VV is a vector floating‑point instruction that reads two source vector registers (vs1 and vs2) and a destination vector register (vd) as the accumulator, computes for each element i the product vs1[i]·vs2[i] minus the original vd[i] value, and writes the result back into vd[i]. The instruction uses the standard vector register fields (vd, vs1, vs2) and operates under normal floating‑point and vector privilege levels; no special privileged mode is required. It may raise the usual floating‑point exception flags (e.g., overflow, underflow, invalid) if any element's computation triggers them. No other architectural side effects are defined.

INSTRUCTION: VFMSUB.VF
SUMMARY: VFMSUB.VF uses the scalar floating‑point register f[rs1] as the multiplicand combined with each element of the vector source register vs2 and writes the fused multiply‑subtract result to the corresponding element of the vector destination register vd. For every element i within the current vector length whose mask bit is true, vd[i] is updated to (f[rs1] * vd[i]) − vs2[i] as a single fused operation rounded according to the active floating‑point rounding mode; elements with a false mask or out of range are left unchanged. The fused operation performs no intermediate rounding and any NaN produced is propagated to the destination element in accordance with IEEE‑754 rules. The instruction executes in the normal privilege mode for vector floating‑point instructions and raises no architectural exceptions beyond the standard NaN and rounding behavior.

INSTRUCTION: VFMUL.VF
SUMMARY: VFMUL.VF multiplies each element of source vector register vs2 by the scalar floating‑point value in register rs1, writing the results to destination vector register vd. The element width of vd and vs2 must match the width of the scalar in rs1; a width mismatch triggers an illegal‑instruction exception. When a mask register is active (vm = 1), only elements with a set mask bit are updated, leaving the remaining elements of vd unchanged, and the instruction is executable in normal (non‑privileged) mode.

INSTRUCTION: VFMUL.VV
SUMMARY: VFMUL.VV reads floating‑point elements from source vector registers vs2 and vs1, multiplies each pair element‑wise, and writes the products to destination vector register vd. A mask register vm controls the writes: elements with a true mask bit are updated with the result, while elements with a false mask bit retain their previous vd values. The operation follows the normal vector‑execution access rules and imposes no extra privilege or access‑control restrictions. No exceptional behavior is defined by the given semantics.

INSTRUCTION: VFMV.F.S
SUMMARY: VFMV.F.S reads element 0 of the source vector register vs2 and writes that SEW‑wide value into the scalar floating‑point register f[rd], ignoring LMUL and any vector‑register grouping. The operation is performed unconditionally, even when vstart ≥ vl or vl = 0. Encodings with the mask disabled (vm = 0) are reserved and must raise an illegal‑instruction exception.

INSTRUCTION: VFMV.S.F
SUMMARY: VFMV.S.F copies the value from a specified scalar floating‑point register into element 0 of the destination vector register. The instruction ignores LMUL and any vector register grouping, and it performs no write when the architectural register vstart is ≥ vl or when vl = 0. No other elements of the destination vector register group are modified, and the operation requires only the normal privileges for accessing vector and floating‑point registers.

INSTRUCTION: VFMV.V.F
SUMMARY: VFMV.V.F copies the floating‑point value held in the scalar register rs1 into every element of the destination vector register group vd. The instruction is unmasked, so the vm field is fixed to 1 and all elements of vd are active. The vs2 field must be v0; any encoding with a different vs2 value is reserved and triggers an illegal‑instruction exception. No special privilege or access‑control checks beyond normal execution permissions are required.

INSTRUCTION: VFNCVT.F.F.W
SUMMARY: VFNCVT.F.F.W reads each double‑width floating‑point element from the source vector register group vs2 and writes a single‑width floating‑point result to the corresponding element of the destination vector register group vd. The write occurs only for element indices whose mask bit in vm is 1; masked‑off elements retain their prior value. For a given vector length, one result element is produced per active index, and when EEW = 16 the source bits are interpreted as binary16 values for conversion. No special privilege checks or defined exception conditions are associated with this instruction.

INSTRUCTION: VFNCVT.F.X.W
SUMMARY: VFNCVT.F.X.W reads each double‑width signed‑integer element from source vector register vs2, converts it to a single‑width floating‑point value, and writes the result to the corresponding element of destination vector register vd. The conversion is a narrowing operation, so the floating‑point element width is half that of the source integer element. The instruction obeys the same vector‑register overlap constraints as other narrowing vector instructions. When no rounding mode is encoded, the implementation‑defined default rounding mode for narrowing conversions is applied.

INSTRUCTION: VFNCVT.F.XU.W
SUMMARY: VFNCVT.F.XU.W reads each element of the source vector register vs2 as an unsigned integer whose width is twice that of the destination floating‑point element and converts it to a single‑width floating‑point value written to the corresponding element of destination register vd. When a mask register vm is present, only elements with a true mask bit are updated; unmasked elements of vd remain unchanged. The instruction obeys the usual vector‑register overlap constraints for narrowing operations, disallowing any prohibited overlap between vd and vs2. No special privilege level is required beyond that of ordinary vector instructions.

INSTRUCTION: VFNCVT.ROD.F.F.W
SUMMARY: VFNCVT.ROD.F.F.W reads a source vector of wide floating‑point elements and writes a destination vector of half‑width floating‑point elements, performing a narrowing conversion with a fixed round‑towards‑odd mode. The instruction follows the same vector‑register overlap constraints as other narrowing operations and does not require special privilege, being available in the normal execution environment. If a source element exceeds the representable range of the destination format, it is saturated to the largest finite value of the same sign, and the conversion sets the appropriate floating‑point exception flags. When used in a multi‑step halving sequence, any intermediate step that does not use VFNCVT.ROD.F.F.W will yield final values and exception flags that differ from a single direct conversion.

INSTRUCTION: VFNCVT.RTZ.X.F.W
SUMMARY: VFNCVT.RTZ.X.F.W reads each double‑width floating‑point element in source vector register vs2, converts it to a signed integer of half the element width using round‑toward‑zero truncation, and writes the result to the corresponding element of destination vector register vd. The conversion follows the standard truncating rounding mode. Overlap between vd and vs2 is permitted only in the patterns allowed for narrowing vector instructions; any other overlap triggers an illegal‑instruction fault. The instruction is a regular user‑mode vector operation with no special privilege restrictions.

INSTRUCTION: VFNCVT.RTZ.XU.F.W
SUMMARY: VFNCVT.RTZ.XU.F.W converts each double‑width floating‑point element in source vector register vs2 to an unsigned integer of half the width, truncating toward zero, and writes the result to the corresponding element of destination register vd. The instruction follows the standard narrowing‑instruction overlap rules, prohibiting hazardous overlaps between vd and vs2. It operates under the current vector length (VL) and element‑width (SEW) settings, processing as many elements as permitted by those configuration fields. The operation is available in any privilege mode that permits vector instructions and triggers an illegal‑instruction exception if the required element width is unsupported.

INSTRUCTION: VFNCVT.XU.F.W
SUMMARY: VFNCVT.XU.F.W reads a source vector register containing double‑width floating‑point elements, converts each element to an unsigned integer of the destination element width, and writes the results into a destination vector register. The instruction follows the same vector register overlap constraints as other narrowing vector operations, disallowing illegal source‑destination overlap. No special privilege or access‑control checks are defined beyond the standard permissions for vector instructions. No extra exception behavior is specified beyond the usual floating‑point‑to‑integer conversion rules.

INSTRUCTION: VFNCVTBF16.F.F.W
SUMMARY: The VFNCVTBF16.F.F.W instruction reads each 32‑bit floating‑point element from the source vector register (vs2), converts it to bfloat16 using the rounding mode in the frm CSR, and writes the result to the corresponding element of the destination vector register (vd) with a selected element width of 16 bits. It is defined only on implementations that support the Zve32f vector extension and the Zvfbfmin BF16 conversion extension; encodings with any SEW other than 16 are reserved. The conversion may raise floating‑point exceptions: overflow if the magnitude exceeds the bfloat16 range, underflow for non‑zero results that round to sub‑normal, and inexact when the value cannot be represented exactly. No additional privilege checks are required beyond the standard vector‑instruction access rules.

INSTRUCTION: VFNMACC.VF
SUMMARY: VFNMACC.VF computes for each active element i the fused negative multiply‑accumulate result – (f[rs1] * vs2[i]) – vd[i] using the element's floating‑point format. The destination vector register vd provides the subtrahend operand and is overwritten with this result for elements where the mask vm[i] is true, while masked‑off elements retain their prior values. No additional permission checks, privilege restrictions, or exception behavior are introduced beyond the standard floating‑point exceptions and rounding rules for the given element type.

INSTRUCTION: VFNMADD.VF
SUMMARY: VFNMADD.VF performs a floating‑point negate‑multiply‑add, writing the result back to the register that provides the first multiplicand and thus overwriting that operand. It reads the second multiplicand and the addend from their respective source registers and produces the result in the destination register, with no special privilege or access‑control requirements beyond those of standard floating‑point instructions.

INSTRUCTION: VFNMADD.VV
SUMMARY: VFNMADD.VV writes its result into the destination vector register vd, replacing the original first‑multiplicand operand for each element. The operation is performed element‑wise on all vector elements up to the current vector length (VL). No special access‑control, privilege restrictions, or exception behavior are defined by the given rules.

INSTRUCTION: VFNMSAC.VV
SUMMARY: VFNMSAC.VV reads source vector registers vs1 and vs2 and a destination vector register vd, applying the current mask to each element. For each active element it computes -(vs1[i] * vs2[i]) + vd[i] using the element's floating‑point format and writes the result back to the same element of vd, while masked‑off elements of vd remain unchanged. The instruction follows the standard vector‑register access rules and does not require privileged mode. No additional exception conditions are defined beyond the usual floating‑point exception handling.

INSTRUCTION: VFNMSUB.VF
SUMMARY: VFNMSUB.VF reads each element of the destination vector register vd, masks each element with vm, and for mask‑on elements computes −(f[rs1] × original vd[i]) + vs2[i] using the current floating‑point rounding mode, writing the result back to vd while mask‑off elements remain unchanged. The instruction processes all elements up to the current vector length and updates the floating‑point exception flags for each element that is computed. Only the modified elements of vd are written; no other registers, memory locations, or architectural state are affected. The operation is allowed in any unprivileged mode and follows the prevailing vector and floating‑point configurations.

INSTRUCTION: VFNMSUB.VV
SUMMARY: The VFNMSUB.VV instruction performs a fused multiply‑negate‑add on each vector element, computing vd[i] = –(vs1[i] * original_vd[i]) + vs2[i]. The destination register vd supplies the first multiplicand and is overwritten with the result, while vs1 provides the second multiplicand and vs2 provides the addend. When a mask register is present, only elements whose mask bit is one are updated; masked‑off elements retain their original vd value. The operation is permitted in normal vector execution contexts without special privilege requirements.

INSTRUCTION: VFREC7.V
SUMMARY: VFREC7.V is a vector floating‑point instruction that computes a seven‑bit reciprocal for each element, preserving the operand's sign. It returns infinities for zero inputs (raising divide‑by‑zero), propagates NaNs (signalling NaNs raise the invalid‑operation exception), converts large‑magnitude normal inputs (|x| ≥ 2^(B‑1)) to subnormal results, and converts subnormal inputs with magnitude ≥ 2^(–(B+1)) to normal results; smaller subnormals produce infinite outputs whose exact value follows the active rounding mode and raise overflow and inexact exceptions. Negative‑infinity input yields negative zero with no exception. No special privilege or register‑access constraints beyond normal vector‑register usage are specified.

INSTRUCTION: VFREDMAX.VS
SUMMARY: VFREDMAX.VS reduces the elements of the source vector register (vs2) to a single maximum value and writes that result to the destination scalar floating‑point register (fd). The operation uses the current vector configuration (e.g., vtype, vl) to determine which elements participate in the reduction. It can be executed in any mode where the vector and floating‑point extensions are enabled; no additional privilege checks are required. No special exception behavior is defined beyond the standard illegal‑instruction fault if the necessary extensions are unavailable.

INSTRUCTION: VFREDMIN.VS
SUMMARY: VFREDMIN.VS reduces the active elements of vector register group vs2, using the scalar value in the first element of vs1 as the initial minimum accumulator, and writes the final minimum to the first element of destination register vd while leaving the remaining elements of vd unchanged. The reduction processes only elements with indices less than the current VL and includes an element only when the corresponding mask bit in vm is set to one. The comparison follows the floating‑point element type of the operands, and an illegal‑instruction exception is raised if that type is not a supported floating‑point format for the minimum operation.

INSTRUCTION: VFREDOSUM.VS
SUMMARY: VFREDOSUM.VS reads the source vector groups and computes an ordered sum reduction of all contained elements. The scalar result of this reduction is written to the destination floating‑point register vd. The instruction follows the normal vector‑instruction access rules, incurs no special privilege restrictions, and generates only the standard illegal‑instruction or vector‑length exceptions defined by the base ISA.

INSTRUCTION: VFRSQRT7.V
SUMMARY: VFRSQRT7.V computes an approximate reciprocal‑square‑root for each vector element, inserting a seven‑bit table entry into the most‑significant bits of the mantissa (with the remaining fraction bits cleared), deriving the exponent as floor((3·B‑1‑e)/2), and copying the source sign to the result. It produces a canonical NaN for negative finite inputs or signaling NaNs (raising the invalid‑operation exception), generates ±∞ for signed zeros (raising divide‑by‑zero), yields +0 for +∞, and returns a normal finite estimate for positive finite inputs without raising exceptions. All results are independent of the current dynamic rounding mode, and positive normal or subnormal inputs always produce a normal (non‑subnormal) result. The instruction is part of the vector extension and imposes no special privilege or access‑control restrictions.

INSTRUCTION: VFSGNJ.VF
SUMMARY: VFSGNJ.VF copies the sign bit from the scalar floating‑point register rs1 to each active element of the destination vector register vd, while the remaining mantissa and exponent bits are taken from the corresponding element of the vector source register vs2. An optional mask register vm determines which elements are active; if vm is omitted all elements are treated as active, and inactive masked elements retain their original vd contents. The instruction performs only vector‑scalar reads and a vector write and does not require privileged execution. No architecturally defined exceptions are raised by this operation.

INSTRUCTION: VFSGNJN.VF
SUMMARY: VFSGNJN.VF reads a scalar floating‑point register rs1, a source vector register vs2, and a mask register vm, and writes its result to a destination vector register vd. For each element whose mask bit is true, it copies all bits except the sign bit from the corresponding element of vs2 and sets the sign bit to the logical negation of rs1's sign bit, while elements with a false mask remain unchanged. The instruction is available in all privilege modes and does not generate architectural exceptions under normal operation.

INSTRUCTION: VFSGNJN.VV
SUMMARY: VFSGNJN.VV writes a vector result to the destination register, using the sign bits from source vector register vs1 and the magnitude bits from source vector register vs2. For each element i, the sign bit of the destination element is the logical complement of the sign bit of vs1[i], while all other bits are copied directly from vs2[i]. If vs1 and vs2 refer to the same register, the instruction produces the element‑wise negation of that vector. The operation belongs to the vector‑floating‑point ISA, requires the V and F extensions, and does not raise any architectural exceptions under normal execution.

INSTRUCTION: VFSGNJX.VF
SUMMARY: VFSGNJX.VF copies the sign bit of the scalar floating‑point register rs1 into each active element of the destination vector register vd while preserving all other bits from the corresponding element of source vector register vs2, using the current SEW to define the sign‑bit position. Elements whose mask bit vm is clear are left unchanged. The operation is applied only to elements within the active vector length and does not generate any architectural exceptions. It is executable in any privilege mode that permits vector instructions.

INSTRUCTION: VFSGNJX.VV
SUMMARY: VFSGNJX.VV writes each element of the destination floating‑point vector register with the bits of the corresponding element from the vs2 source vector, copying all bits except the sign bit, which is set according to the operation's sign‑injection rule. When the two source registers are the same, the sign bit is forced positive, so the instruction yields the absolute (magnitude) value of the source elements. The instruction is available in all privilege modes and has no special access‑control restrictions. No additional exception behavior is defined beyond the normal floating‑point exception handling.

INSTRUCTION: VFSLIDE1DOWN.VF
SUMMARY: VFSLIDE1DOWN.VF shifts each active floating‑point element of the source vector group vs2 one position down, writes the scalar from floating‑point register f[rs1] into the last active element, and respects the current vector length vl, start index vstart and mask v0.mask; elements before vstart remain unchanged, masked‑off elements are left unspecified, and elements beyond vl are handled by the tail policy up to VLMAX. The destination vector register vd is written for indices i with vstart ≤ i < vl‑1 when the mask is enabled, and the scalar is stored to vd[vl‑1] only if that mask bit is enabled. Mask‑disabled updates are mask‑agnostic, do not raise exceptions, and the instruction never modifies registers beyond the architectural limit VLMAX. Its element‑wise behavior mirrors the integer‑register version of VFSLIDE1DOWN and requires only the normal privilege level for vector‑unit operations.

INSTRUCTION: VFSQRT.V
SUMMARY: VFSQRT.V is a vector‑floating‑point instruction that computes the square root of each element in source vector register vs2 and writes the result to the corresponding element of destination register vd. An optional mask register vm selects active element positions; where the mask is false the destination element is left unchanged. The operation executes in the normal user‑level vector environment and is not privileged. Any floating‑point exception conditions (e.g., NaN or negative operand) follow the standard IEEE‑754 handling defined for vector FP operations.

INSTRUCTION: VFSUB.VF
SUMMARY: VFSUB.VF subtracts the scalar floating‑point value in register f[rs1] from each element of source vector register vs2 and writes the element‑wise results to destination vector register vd. The instruction reads the scalar FP register f[rs1] and the vector register vs2, and updates vector register vd. It is only executable when the Vector extension is enabled and the current privilege level permits vector operations. No additional exception behavior is defined beyond the standard illegal‑instruction handling.

INSTRUCTION: VFWADD.VF
SUMMARY: VFWADD.VF performs a widening floating‑point addition by reading each SEW‑wide element from source vector register vs2 and a scalar floating‑point value from register rs1, applying IEEE‑754 addition. The 2 × SEW‑wide results are written element‑wise to destination vector register vd, while elements for which the mask vm is false remain unchanged. The instruction follows the normal vector‑floating‑point access rules and raises no special exceptions beyond the standard architectural checks.

INSTRUCTION: VFWADD.WF
SUMMARY: VFWADD.WF is a vector‑widening floating‑point add that reads a SEW‑wide scalar from floating‑point register rs1 and adds it to each SEW‑wide element of source vector vs2, producing 2 × SEW results stored in the destination vector register group vd. The operation is element‑wise and widens the sum to 2 × SEW precision; an optional mask vm selects active elements, leaving masked‑off destinations unchanged. It accesses rs1, vs2, and vd in the standard vector floating‑point execution context with no special privilege requirements beyond those of ordinary vector FP instructions. Any floating‑point exceptions follow the usual FP exception handling semantics.

INSTRUCTION: VFWADD.WV
SUMMARY: VFWADD.WV reads corresponding SEW‑wide floating‑point elements from source vector registers vs1 and vs2, adds each pair, and writes the 2 × SEW result to the matching element position of destination vector register vd. The destination register element width is exactly twice that of the source registers, and the operation processes all elements that are active according to the current vector length. The instruction is a standard vector‑vector operation with no special privilege or access‑control constraints beyond those of ordinary vector instructions. No additional exception conditions are defined in the provided rules.

INSTRUCTION: VFWCVT.F.F.V
SUMMARY: VFWCVT.F.F.V reads a source vector register containing binary16 (EEW = 16) floating‑point elements and writes a destination vector register with corresponding binary32 elements, obeying the same register‑overlap rules as other widening vector operations. Each source element is converted to a double‑width IEEE‑754 value that exactly represents the original, and the conversion must not raise any floating‑point exception flags beyond those that would be set by performing one or more successive widening steps. The instruction is unrestricted by privilege level and follows the standard vector‑instruction access controls. Any deviation in element values or extra flags indicates a mis‑implementation.

INSTRUCTION: VFWCVT.F.X.V
SUMMARY: The VFWCVT.F.X.V instruction reads a source vector register (vs2) containing signed integer elements and writes a destination vector register (vd) with double‑width IEEE‑754 floating‑point elements, one per source element. It is executable in any privilege mode that permits vector operations and uses the standard vector‑type fields (vd, vs2, vm). If vd overlaps vs2 in a way that violates the widening‑vector overlap rule, the instruction raises an illegal‑instruction exception (mcause set to illegal‑instruction). Each result element exactly represents the original integer because a double‑width floating‑point format can represent every single‑width integer precisely.

INSTRUCTION: VFWCVT.F.XU.V
SUMMARY: VFWCVT.F.XU.V reads each unsigned integer element from the source vector register vs2, widens it to a double‑precision IEEE‑754 floating‑point value, and writes the result to the corresponding element of the destination vector register vd, optionally gated by the vector mask vm. The instruction is illegal if the source and destination vector register groups overlap in a way that violates the widening‑instruction overlap constraints. The produced floating‑point result must exactly represent the input integer value, and the operation does not set any floating‑point exception flags. Execution is permitted in all standard privilege modes and is subject only to the mask‑controlled element writes.

INSTRUCTION: VFWCVT.RTZ.X.F.V
SUMMARY: VFWCVT.RTZ.X.F.V reads each element of the source vector register vs2, which contains single‑precision floating‑point values, and writes a double‑width signed‑integer result to the destination vector register vd. The conversion applies round‑toward‑zero semantics, discarding the fractional part of every element. The operation must obey the same vector‑register overlap constraints that apply to all widening vector instructions. It is an unprivileged vector instruction and requires only the normal vector execution permissions.

INSTRUCTION: VFWCVT.RTZ.XU.F.V
SUMMARY: The VFWCVT.RTZ.XU.F.V instruction reads floating‑point elements from source vector register vs2, widens each element to an unsigned integer type whose bit‑width is exactly twice that of the source, and writes the truncated (round‑toward‑zero) results to the corresponding elements of destination vector register vd. The conversion is performed element‑wise across the active vector length. No special privilege or access‑control constraints are specified beyond those of ordinary vector instructions. No additional exception conditions are defined beyond the standard handling of illegal element values.

INSTRUCTION: VFWCVT.X.F.V
SUMMARY: VFWCVT.X.F.V reads each element of source vector register vs2, interprets it as a floating‑point value, converts it to a signed integer of twice the original element width, and writes the results to the corresponding elements of destination vector register vd. The conversion may be implemented as a cascade of widening steps, provided the final results are identical to a single‑step conversion and no extra exception flags are generated beyond those defined for the conversion. If the source and destination registers overlap in a manner prohibited for widening operations, the hardware raises an illegal‑instruction exception with mcause = 2. The instruction follows the normal vector‑unit access‑control rules and is executable in any privilege mode that permits vector operations.

INSTRUCTION: VFWCVT.XU.F.V
SUMMARY: VFWCVT.XU.F.V reads each element of the source vector register vs2, interprets it as a floating‑point value, converts it to an unsigned integer of twice the element width, and writes the results to the destination vector register vd. The instruction is a widening vector operation and therefore inherits the standard vector‑register overlap rules, prohibiting any illegal overlap between vd and vs2 that is disallowed for widening conversions. It operates under the same privilege and access controls as other vector arithmetic instructions, requiring the vector unit to be enabled and the appropriate execution mode. No other exceptional behavior is defined beyond the overlap constraint.

INSTRUCTION: VFWCVTBF16.F.F.V
SUMMARY: The VFWCVTBF16.F.F.V instruction widens each BF16 element in a source vector register to a 32‑bit floating‑point element in a destination vector register, preserving the original numeric value. It performs an exact conversion; for normal or infinite BF16 inputs the resulting FP32 bits are the BF16 bits shifted left by 16 with the lower half cleared. The encoding is only defined when the selected element width (SEW) is 16, otherwise it is reserved. No special privilege or access‑control restrictions are specified, and behavior for non‑normal/Infinity inputs is not defined by these rules.

INSTRUCTION: VFWMACC.VF
SUMMARY: VFWMACC.VF is a vector‑floating‑point fused multiply‑add that updates each masked element of the destination vector register vd with the product of the scalar floating‑point register f[rs1] and the corresponding element of source vector register vs2, added to the existing 2 × SEW‑wide element in vd. The scalar operand f[rs1] and the source element vs2[i] are treated as SEW‑wide values, while the addend and result are 2 × SEW‑wide, and the operation overwrites the original addend. The instruction uses the vector mask vm to select active lanes and is encoded as a standard vector‑floating‑point operation accessible in all privilege modes. It raises only the usual floating‑point exception flags (e.g., overflow, invalid) and does not generate any additional architectural traps.

INSTRUCTION: VFWMACC.VV
SUMMARY: VFWMACC.VV reads SEW‑wide elements from source vector registers vs1 and vs2, computes the floating‑point product for each element i, adds that product to the existing 2×SEW‑wide value in destination register vd[i], and writes the fused result back to vd[i]. The destination register vd therefore holds a 2×SEW‑wide floating‑point addend that is overwritten with the multiply‑add result for every element. The instruction accesses the vector registers vs1, vs2, and vd and follows the normal privilege model for vector operations, with no additional access‑control restrictions. No specific exception conditions are defined beyond the standard floating‑point exception handling of the underlying architecture.

INSTRUCTION: VFWMACCBF16.VF
SUMMARY: VFWMACCBF16.VF performs a masked vector‑scalar multiply‑accumulate where the BF16 scalar in rs1 is multiplied with each BF16 element of vs2, the unrounded products are added to the corresponding 32‑bit floating‑point elements of vd, and the sums are rounded according to the rounding mode in the frm register before being written back to the masked elements of vd (unmasked elements remain unchanged). The instruction is defined only for a selected element width (SEW) of 16; any other SEW makes the encoding reserved and causes an illegal‑instruction fault. Operands are rs1 (scalar source), vs2 (vector source), vd (vector destination), vm (mask), and frm (rounding‑mode control), and the operation is architecturally equivalent to the sequence fcvt.s.bf16 T1, rs1; vfwcvtbf16.f.f.v T2, vs2, vm; vfmacc.vf vd, T1, T2, vm. No special privilege level is required beyond normal vector‑extension access.

INSTRUCTION: VFWMACCBF16.VV
SUMMARY: VFWMACCBF16.VV is defined only for a selected element width of 16 bits; any other SEW makes the encoding reserved and triggers an illegal‑instruction exception. The instruction reads BF16 elements from the vector source registers vs1 and vs2, reads the corresponding FP32 accumulator element from the destination register vd, forms an unrounded product of each BF16 pair, adds it to the accumulator, rounds the sum according to the current frm rounding mode, and writes the rounded FP32 result back to vd under the active mask vm. Architecturally it is equivalent to converting the BF16 inputs to FP32 with vfwcvtbf16.f.f.v and then performing a vector fused multiply‑add with vfmacc.vv, using the same mask.

INSTRUCTION: VFWMSAC.VF
SUMMARY: The VFWMSAC.VF instruction treats rs1 as a scalar floating‑point register, vs2 as the vector source, and vd as the vector destination. For each element i it computes (f[rs1] × vs2[i]) − vd[i] using SEW‑wide multiplication and a double‑width subtraction, producing a 2 × SEW result. The 2 × SEW result overwrites the corresponding element of vd. No special privilege checks or exception conditions are defined beyond the standard vector instruction model.

INSTRUCTION: VFWMSAC.VV
SUMMARY: The VFWMSAC.VV instruction reads vector registers vs1 and vs2 and a wide addend in destination register vd, treating each element as a 2 × SEW floating‑point value. For each element i it computes (vs1[i] × vs2[i]) − vd[i] and writes the 2 × SEW result back into vd[i], overwriting the previous content. The operation is a widening fused multiply‑add/subtract that updates the destination register in place. No additional privilege checks or exception conditions are defined by the given rules.

INSTRUCTION: VFWMUL.VF
SUMMARY: VFWMUL.VF multiplies each element of the source vector register vs2 by the scalar floating‑point value held in register rs1, produces the product in a floating‑point format twice the precision of the vs2 elements, and writes the widened result to the corresponding element of the destination vector register vd. When a mask register vm is supplied, the multiplication is performed only for element positions where the mask bit is set, leaving the other destination elements unchanged. The instruction reads rs1 as a scalar FP operand and vs2 as the vector multiplicand, and writes the results to vd. It operates under the normal privilege and access rules for vector‑floating‑point instructions and defines no additional exception behavior.

INSTRUCTION: VFWMUL.VV
SUMMARY: VFWMUL.VV multiplies paired elements from two source vector registers, producing a result vector whose elements are twice the width of the source elements. Each destination element holds the correctly rounded product of the corresponding source elements. The instruction writes these results to the designated destination vector register. No additional access‑control or exception behavior is defined by the provided rule.

INSTRUCTION: VFWNMSAC.VF
SUMMARY: VFWNMSAC.VF performs a fused multiply‑negate‑add on a scalar floating‑point register rs1 and each element of vector source vs2, computing ‑(rs1 * vs2[i]) + vd[i] and writing the result into the corresponding element of the destination vector vd. The destination elements are twice the SEW width, and the operation overwrites the wide addend field in vd, discarding its previous value. It is a regular user‑mode vector floating‑point instruction with no special privilege requirements. No additional architectural exceptions are defined beyond the standard floating‑point exception handling.

INSTRUCTION: VFWNMSAC.VV
SUMMARY: The VFWNMSAC.VV instruction reads vector registers vs1 and vs2 as SEW‑wide floating‑point values and uses the existing contents of vd as the wide addend. For each element enabled by the mask vm, it computes vd[i] = ‑(vs1[i] * vs2[i]) + vd[i] with fused floating‑point arithmetic, overwriting vd; elements where vm is false are left unchanged. The instruction operates in the regular vector execution environment with no special privilege or access‑control requirements. No explicit exception behavior is specified beyond the standard floating‑point exception handling of the fused operation.

INSTRUCTION: VFWREDOSUM.VS
SUMMARY: VFWREDOSUM.VS reduces a source vector (vs2) to a single element by promoting each SEW‑width element to twice the SEW, accumulating in a 2*SEW‑bit accumulator, and writes the double‑width result to the destination vector register. Elements masked off by the vector mask vm are ignored, and any NaN encountered propagates its payload so the final result is a NaN with the appropriate payload. The instruction follows the normal vector execution model and does not impose additional privilege or access‑control constraints beyond those of other vector operations. Exceptions and special cases mirror the behavior of vfredosum.vs.

INSTRUCTION: VFWSUB.VF
SUMMARY: VFWSUB.VF subtracts each SEW‑width element of source vector register vs2 from the SEW‑width scalar floating‑point value in rs1, producing 2 × SEW‑width results written to destination vector register vd. The destination register must be configured for element width 2 × SEW, while vs2 and rs1 are configured for SEW width. If a mask register vm is supplied, only elements with a true mask bit are updated; otherwise all elements of vd are written. An illegal‑instruction exception is raised when rs1 does not denote a scalar floating‑point register or when vs2 or vd are not specified as proper vector register groups.

INSTRUCTION: VFWSUB.VV
SUMMARY: VFWSUB.VV reads two source vector registers (vs1 and vs2) as floating‑point elements, subtracts each vs1 element from the corresponding vs2 element, and writes the widened (2 × SEW) result into the destination vector register vd. The instruction respects an optional mask register vm, leaving any masked‑off destination elements unchanged. It operates under the normal vector‑unit access rules and does not require privileged execution. Any architectural exception follows the standard floating‑point/vector exception handling model.

INSTRUCTION: VFWSUB.WV
SUMMARY: VFWSUB.WV subtracts each element of source vector register vs1 from the corresponding element of source vector register vs2, producing a result element that is twice the standard element width (2*SEW). The widened subtraction results are written to the matching elements of destination vector register vd. An optional mask register vm can gate the operation; for elements where the mask bit is clear, the destination element in vd is not written and retains its previous value. If vm is omitted, all elements are treated as active and the subtraction is performed on every element.

INSTRUCTION: VGHSH.VV
SUMMARY: VGHSH.VV is a vector instruction that computes a GHASH partial product, writing to the destination register the value ((Yi XOR Xi) * H) reduced modulo the polynomial x^128 + x^7 + x^2 + x + 1, with each byte of the source operands bit‑reversed before the carry‑less multiply and the result bit‑reversed before being stored. The operation works on 128‑bit effective elements, uses an element‑group size of 4, requires SEW=32, and processes elements from floor(vstart/EGS) to floor(vl/EGS)-1, inheriting the VGMUL.VV algorithm (vs1 hard‑wired to zero) including the conditional XOR 0x87 reduction step. It raises an illegal‑instruction exception with RETIRE_FAIL if SEW is not 32 or if LMUL*VLEN is less than 128 bits. No special privilege or access‑control restrictions apply beyond the normal unprivileged vector instruction semantics.

INSTRUCTION: VGMUL.VV
SUMMARY: VGMUL.VV reads 128‑bit elements from the destination vector register Vd and source register Vs2, treats each as a GF(2) polynomial, computes their carry‑less product reduced modulo the GHASH irreducible polynomial, and writes the 128‑bit result back to the same element of Vd while leaving all other vector registers unchanged. The instruction is defined only for element groups exactly 128 bits wide; it requires LMUL × VLEN ≥ 128 bits and a vstart CSR that is a multiple of four, otherwise an illegal‑instruction exception is raised before any state changes. For each element the bits within each byte of both operands are reversed before multiplication, the carry‑less product is computed, then the bits within each byte of the 128‑bit product are reversed again prior to storing. Any violation of size, LMUL/VLEN, or vstart constraints triggers an illegal‑instruction exception (mcause = 2) with no architectural side effects.

INSTRUCTION: VID.V
SUMMARY: VID.V writes a sequential element index starting at 0 into each active element of the destination vector register group for positions 0 through VL‑1, using the element width defined by the current SEW. The instruction may be masked; masking only controls which elements are written and does not modify the index values themselves. The source‑register field VS2 is hard‑wired to v0, and any encoding with VS2 ≠ v0 is reserved. If an index value exceeds the destination element's SEW, it is truncated to the least‑significant SEW bits.

INSTRUCTION: VIOTA.M
SUMMARY: VIOTA.M consumes a vector mask register as its source and writes to a destination vector register group the prefix‑sum of enabled mask bits for each element index. When the instruction is masked, only source elements whose corresponding mask bit is set contribute to the sum, and the resulting count is zero‑extended or truncated to fit the destination element width (SEW). Execution with a non‑zero vstart is illegal, while any trap forces vstart to be reported as zero and causes the operation to restart from the first element on resume. The instruction is unaffected by the Zvkt extension and imposes no additional privilege restrictions.

INSTRUCTION: VL1RE16.V
SUMMARY: VL1RE16.V is an unmasked unit‑stride load that reads VLEN/16 half‑words from the memory address in scalar register rs1 into a single destination vector register (a one‑register vector group). Its effective vector length is evl = VLEN/16; writes are not suppressed when vstart ≥ vl, but an illegal‑instruction exception is raised if vstart ≥ evl. A misaligned‑address exception occurs if the base address is not naturally aligned to the larger of 2 bytes (EEW/8) or SEWMIN/8 bytes. No additional masking or privilege checks beyond the normal vector‑unit access rules are required.

INSTRUCTION: VL1RE32.V
SUMMARY: VL1RE32.V loads a destination vector‑register group (which must be aligned as a vector register group) with raw 32‑bit elements from memory, storing the bytes exactly as fetched. The instruction is only valid when the nf field encodes NFIELDS = 1 and the NFIELDS field is 1, 2, 4, or 8; any other encoding raises an illegal‑instruction exception. It computes its effective vector length as VLEN / 32 independent of vtype or vl, and is treated as reserved when vstart ≥ VLEN / 32. A misaligned address exception may be raised if the base address is not naturally aligned to the larger of 4 bytes (EEW/8) or SEWMIN/8.

INSTRUCTION: VL1RE64.V
SUMMARY: VL1RE64.V performs a whole‑register load from the memory address held in the scalar integer register rs1 into a destination vector register group, which must be aligned as a vector register group. The instruction is reserved if the destination is not properly aligned or if the architectural vstart register is greater than or equal to evl. It transfers VLEN/64 doublewords (VLEN/8 bytes), i.e., vlenb bytes, writing the data to the destination using the same byte layout as in memory regardless of the element width. A misaligned address exception is raised when the base address is not naturally aligned to the larger of 8 bytes or the implementation's minimum SEW, and normal privilege checks apply.

INSTRUCTION: VL1RE8.V
SUMMARY: VL1RE8.V reads VLEN/8 bytes from the address held in scalar register a0 and writes them sequentially into the vector register group whose first register is v3, preserving the memory byte order. The instruction requires the nf field to specify a power‑of‑2 sized, properly aligned vector group and NFIELDS to be 1; any other setting renders the encoding reserved. Execution is also reserved if the architectural vstart index is greater than or equal to evl (VLEN/8). No additional privilege or access‑control checks beyond the standard vector‑unit constraints are defined.

INSTRUCTION: VL2RE32.V
SUMMARY: VL2RE32.V loads a two‑register vector group (v2‑v3) with 2 × VLEN/32 32‑bit elements from the memory address held in the scalar register specified by rs1 (e.g., a0). The instruction is only valid when the encoded register‑group size is a power‑of‑two and properly aligned, and the NFIELDS field is one of {1,2,4,8}; otherwise the encoding is reserved. It performs an unmasked unit‑stride load, transfers 2 × VLEN/8 bytes (value obtainable from vlenb), and does not suppress writes when vstart ≥ vl, though it is reserved if vstart ≥ evl. No special privilege or access‑control restrictions beyond the standard architectural rules apply.

INSTRUCTION: VL2RE64.V
SUMMARY: VL2RE64.V loads a vector register group (e.g., v2‑v3) with 2 * VLEN/64 doublewords from the memory address in rs1 (a0), using the EEW field as a hint but preserving the byte layout from memory. The instruction is defined only for NFIELDS values of 1, 2, 4, or 8 and requires the destination registers to be aligned as a vector register group; any other NFIELDS or a misaligned destination makes the encoding reserved. Its effective vector length is evl = NFIELDS * VLEN/EEW, independent of the current vtype/vl, and execution is reserved when vstart >= evl (the usual vstart >= vl rule does not apply). A misaligned address exception may be raised if the base address is not naturally aligned to the larger of EEW/8 bytes or the implementation's minimum SEW size (SEWMIN/8), otherwise normal privilege checks apply.

INSTRUCTION: VL2RE8.V
SUMMARY: VL2RE8.V loads a memory block whose size equals 2 × VLEN/8 bytes (the value read from the vlenb CSR) from the address held in scalar register a0 into two consecutive vector registers starting with the specified destination (e.g., v2 and v3). The lowest‑numbered destination register receives the lowest‑addressed portion of the block, and each subsequent register receives the next contiguous segment. Unlike normal vector loads, the instruction does not become a no‑op when vstart ≥ vl; it is reserved (causing a trap) if vstart ≥ evl. No special privilege or additional access‑control checks are defined beyond the standard memory‑access permissions.

INSTRUCTION: VL4RE16.V
SUMMARY: VL4RE16.V loads a whole vector register group of four consecutive registers, using the NFIELDS field (restricted to 1, 2, 4, or 8 and requiring power‑of‑two alignment of the destination group). The effective vector length is NFIELDS × VLEN / EEW and is independent of the current vtype or vl settings. When multiple registers are loaded, the lowest‑numbered destination register receives the lowest‑addressed bytes and each subsequent register receives the next contiguous block, preserving the in‑memory byte layout regardless of EEW. The instruction is reserved if vstart ≥ evl, does not apply the usual "no write when vstart ≥ vl" rule, and does not raise an illegal‑instruction exception when vstart exceeds the element index for the current vtype.

INSTRUCTION: VL4RE32.V
SUMMARY: VL4RE32.V loads a vector register group of four consecutive registers starting at the destination base register, copying the memory byte layout directly into the registers. Both the element-width field (mew) and the register-count field must encode a 32-bit element and a group of four registers aligned on a vector-register-group boundary; otherwise the encoding is reserved and an illegal-instruction exception is raised for an invalid width. The operation transfers 4 * VLEN/8 bytes (four 32-bit words) and defines its effective vector length as evl = 4 * VLEN/32, independent of vtype or the current vl setting. If the architectural vstart is greater than or equal to evl the instruction is reserved, and the usual rule that no elements are written when vstart >= vl does not apply.

INSTRUCTION: VL4RE64.V
SUMMARY: VL4RE64.V loads the vector register group v4‑v7 (four consecutive registers) with double‑word elements from memory, using the scalar register rs1 as the base address for unit‑stride accesses and transferring 4 × vlenb bytes. The registers are aligned as a group so that v4 receives the lowest‑addressed elements and the subsequent registers map to contiguous memory locations. The instruction is reserved if the architectural vstart register is ≥ evl, but unlike most vector loads it still writes elements when vstart ≥ vl. No other privilege restrictions are specified.

INSTRUCTION: VL4RE8.V
SUMMARY: VL4RE8.V transfers a block of bytes from the memory address held in the specified scalar base register into the consecutive vector registers v4‑v7. The instruction ignores the current vtype and vl settings, and the EEW field does not affect the stored byte pattern. Execution is illegal unless the NFIELDS field equals 1, 2, 4, or 8, causing an illegal‑instruction exception, and the operation is reserved when the architectural vstart register is greater than or equal to the computed evl. Unlike normal loads, VL4RE8.V does not suppress writes when vstart ≥ vl.

INSTRUCTION: VL8RE16.V
SUMMARY: VL8RE16.V loads a unit‑stride vector from the memory address in scalar register rs1 into a destination vector register group, writing the same data regardless of the encoded EEW. The instruction requires the NFIELDS field to be 8; any other value raises an illegal‑instruction exception, and it is reserved when the architectural vstart register is greater than or equal to the computed effective vector length evl = 8 × VLEN / EEW. The evl is calculated independently of the current vtype and vl settings, and the usual rule that suppresses writes when vstart ≥ vl does not apply. The operation is unmasked and follows the standard architectural privilege checks.

INSTRUCTION: VL8RE32.V
SUMMARY: VL8RE32.V loads the eight‑wide vector register group v8‑v15 with 32‑bit elements from memory starting at the address held in scalar register rs1. The instruction is illegal if the encoded register count is not a power of two, if the registers are not aligned as a proper vector group, or if the NFIELDS field is not 1, 2, 4, or 8. It computes an effective vector length evl = 8 × VLEN / 32 and uses evl instead of the current vl and vtype settings. The normal vstart ≥ vl check is bypassed, but the instruction traps when vstart ≥ evl.

INSTRUCTION: VL8RE64.V
SUMMARY: VL8RE64.V is an unmasked unit‑stride load that takes the base address from the scalar register rs1 and writes eight double‑word elements into the aligned vector register group v8‑v15 (nf = 8, a power‑of‑2 group). The instruction is reserved if the architectural register vstart ≥ evl, but it does not become a no‑op when vstart ≥ vl and it is not reserved when vstart exceeds the largest element index for the current vtype. The byte layout in the destination registers mirrors the memory layout exactly, independent of EEW.

INSTRUCTION: VL8RE8.V
SUMMARY: VL8RE8.V is a whole‑register, unmasked unit‑stride load that writes the byte‑exact contents of memory into the destination register group v8‑v15, consuming 8 × VLEN/8 bytes. The instruction requires nf = 8, a power‑of‑two register count aligned as a vector register group, and it ignores the current vtype and vl, using the effective vector length while leaving vl and vtype unchanged. Execution is illegal (reserved) if vstart ≥ evl (i.e., ≥ VLEN) or if the base address in rs1 is not naturally aligned to at least SEWMIN/8 bytes, in which case a misaligned‑address exception may be raised. No mask is applied to the memory operation and the in‑register byte order matches the memory layout.

INSTRUCTION: VLE16.V
SUMMARY: VLE16.V loads 16‑bit elements from memory starting at the address in integer register rs1, advancing the address by 2 bytes for each element (unit‑stride) and writes them into the destination vector register vd. The vm mask bit controls each element write: a loaded element updates vd only when the corresponding mask bit is 1; otherwise the existing element in vd is preserved. The vd field selects the vector register that receives the loaded data. The instruction operates without additional privilege checks or special exception conditions beyond standard memory‑access faults.

INSTRUCTION: VLE16FF.V
SUMMARY: VLE16FF.V loads 16‑bit elements from the memory address in rs1 into the destination vector register group vd, using the current vector length (VL) and starting index (VSTART) to control how many elements are processed. The instruction obeys normal load privilege checks and can raise a synchronous exception only on element 0; if such an exception occurs, VL is left unchanged and the trap is taken. Exceptions on any later element are not taken, and VL is reduced to the index of the first failing element, while an exception‑free execution may shorten VL after processing at least one element when VSTART = 0 and VL > 0.

INSTRUCTION: VLE32.V
SUMMARY: VLE32.V loads a sequence of 32‑bit elements from memory starting at the address in integer register rs1, using a 4‑byte unit stride for successive elements. Each element successfully read is written to the corresponding element of the destination vector register group vd only when the element's mask bit in vm is 1; elements with a mask bit of 0 remain unchanged. The instruction does not modify any integer register, including rs1, during its operation.

INSTRUCTION: VLE32FF.V
SUMMARY: VLE32FF.V is a vector load that transfers 32‑bit elements from the memory address in rs1 into a destination vector register, using the current vl and vstart registers to control the operation. It functions like a regular load, but a synchronous exception on element 0 causes a trap while leaving vl unchanged, whereas an exception on any later element does not trap and instead reduces vl to the index of that element. If vstart is zero and vl is non‑zero, the instruction must process at least one element. No special privilege or access‑control checks beyond those of ordinary vector loads apply.

INSTRUCTION: VLE64.V
SUMMARY: VLE64.V reads a sequence of 64‑bit elements from memory beginning at the address in integer register rs1, using a fixed 8‑byte stride, and writes the values into the destination vector register group vd. For each element i, the vector mask vm determines whether the load is performed; elements for which the mask bit is 0 remain unchanged in vd. The instruction follows the normal vector‑extension privilege checks and raises the standard memory‑access exceptions on fault, with no additional architectural side effects.

INSTRUCTION: VLE8.V
SUMMARY: VLE8.V loads an 8‑bit element from memory for each active element using a unit‑stride address that starts at the byte address in scalar register rs1 and increments by one byte per element. The loaded byte is written directly into the corresponding element of destination vector register vd without widening or sign‑extension. If a mask operand (v0.t) is present, only elements with a true mask bit are updated, while masked‑off elements retain their previous vd contents; otherwise the instruction performs an unmasked load of all elements. The operation has no special privilege requirements and defines no architectural exceptions.

INSTRUCTION: VLE8FF.V
SUMMARY: VLE8FF.V performs a vector load of 8‑bit elements, using the vector length register (vl) to limit the number of elements processed and the vector start register (vstart) to indicate the starting index. A synchronous exception on element 0 is taken without modifying vl, whereas an exception on any later element is not taken and instead shortens vl to the faulting element's index. When no exception occurs and vstart = 0 with vl > 0, the implementation must load at least one element and may reduce vl to reflect processing fewer than the original elements. If a debug data‑watchpoint would trigger on any element after the first, the debug trap is delivered and vl is left unchanged.

INSTRUCTION: VLM.V
SUMMARY: VLM.V loads a mask vector from memory with a unit‑stride byte load (EEW = 8) into the destination vector register, computing its effective length as ceil(vl/8) (EMUL = 1). The vstart CSR is interpreted as a byte offset, and the operation must obey the tail‑agnostic policy, leaving elements beyond the current VL unspecified. Execution raises an illegal‑instruction exception when the vill bit in vtype is set. No additional privilege or access‑control checks are defined beyond the illegal‑instruction condition.

INSTRUCTION: VLOXEI16.V
SUMMARY: VLOXEI16.V performs a vector load where each element's address is formed by adding a 16‑bit index taken from the corresponding element of source vector register vs2 to the base address held in integer register rs1. The loaded value, whose width equals the currently configured standard element width (SEW), is written to the matching element of destination vector register vd. The element‑wise mask register vm controls the operation: for mask bits that are clear the destination element is left unchanged and no memory access is issued for that element. All memory accesses are subject to the normal load permission checks, and any fault (e.g., page‑fault or mis‑aligned access) aborts the instruction before any masked‑off elements are updated.

INSTRUCTION: VLOXEI32.V
SUMMARY: VLOXEI32.V performs an ordered 32‑bit indexed load of elements whose width is defined by the current SEW, while the index element width (EEW) is fixed by the static encoding and follows the width of the index elements, not the loaded data. The destination vector register (vd) receives the loaded values, the source vector register (vs2) provides per‑element byte offsets applied to the base address, and the mask register (vm) determines which elements are loaded. No special privilege or access‑control checks are defined beyond the standard vector‑unit rules, and the instruction exhibits no unique exception behavior beyond that of a normal indexed load.

INSTRUCTION: VLOXEI64.V
SUMMARY: VLOXEI64.V loads a vector register with data elements from memory by sequentially accessing addresses formed from a base register plus 64‑bit indices supplied by an index‑vector register. The EEW field encoded in the instruction determines the element width of the index vector only; the width of the loaded data elements is taken from the destination vector's configured SEW. The instruction uses a base address register, an index‑vector register, and a destination vector register and is subject to the normal memory‑access checks with no special privilege requirements. Any fault such as a page fault or mis‑aligned address triggers a standard load exception and aborts the ordered load.

INSTRUCTION: VLOXEI8.V
SUMMARY: VLOXEI8.V loads vector elements of the current SEW in order using 8‑bit indices to address each element. The instruction's behavior is identical to its alias VSXEI8.V; no additional register fields, privilege checks, or exception conditions are defined by the given rules.

INSTRUCTION: VLSE16.V
SUMMARY: VLSE16.V loads 16‑bit elements into a destination vector register (vd) using a base address in rs1 and a stride taken from rs2, where the stride may be negative or zero. If rs2 is not x0 and contains zero, the implementation must issue a separate memory access for each active element, with the order of accesses undefined. Omitting any required load causes the corresponding element of vd to differ from memory, and when the target region is non‑idempotent (e.g., memory‑mapped I/O) every element load must be performed to preserve the device's observable state. The instruction is subject to the usual privilege and access‑control checks for vector loads.

INSTRUCTION: VLSE32.V
SUMMARY: The VLSE32.V instruction loads 32‑bit elements into a destination vector register, which must mirror the exact contents of the addressed memory locations. If any required memory read is omitted, the vector register will contain values that differ from the actual memory data. When the instruction accesses a non‑idempotent memory region, eliminating the read prevents the associated memory‑mapped I/O counter from being incremented, indicating a violation. Proper access permissions must be honored so that required reads are not optimized away in privileged or I/O contexts.

INSTRUCTION: VLSE64.V
SUMMARY: VLSE64.V loads 64‑bit elements into a destination vector register using a base address and a signed stride that may be negative, zero, or positive. The element accesses are unordered, so if the underlying memory is modified between accesses the register may contain any mixture of old and new values. An implementation must appear to perform each prescribed load and may not omit any element load even for non‑idempotent memory regions, lest observable side‑effects be lost. The instruction is a regular user‑mode vector load with no special privilege restrictions.

INSTRUCTION: VLSE8.V
SUMMARY: VLSE8.V takes rs1 as the base address and rs2 (if not x0) as a signed stride register, allowing positive, zero, or negative stride values. If rs2≠x0 and the stride fetched from the address in rs2 is zero, the instruction performs a separate byte load for each active element, with the per‑element accesses permitted to occur in any order. The implementation must appear to execute each memory access, and for non‑idempotent addresses it must issue an explicit load transaction; omitting the load would prevent side‑effects such as updates to access‑counter CSRs. Normal load‑access permission checks apply, so the instruction is only executable in privilege modes that permit ordinary memory reads.

INSTRUCTION: VLSEG2E32.V
SUMMARY: VLSEG2E32.V performs a unit‑stride segment load that streams packed contiguous memory segments into a consecutive pair of vector registers forming a register group. The first field of each segment is written to the destination register vd, and the second field is written to the next register in the same group. The operation uses the current VL and element‑width settings established by the most recent vsetvli instruction and raises an illegal‑instruction exception if NFIELDS is not 2 or EEW is not 32 bits; otherwise it executes in any privilege mode that permits vector instructions.

INSTRUCTION: VLSEG8E8.V
SUMMARY: VLSEG8E8.V loads a strided segment of eight 8‑bit fields from memory starting at the address in rs1 into a group of eight consecutive vector registers whose first element is vd, with vd receiving the first loaded byte. The operation uses unit‑stride addressing and applies the optional mask vm to each element load. The instruction defines NFIELDS = 8 and an element width of 8 bits. No specific privilege or exception behavior is defined by the provided rules.

INSTRUCTION: VLSSEG3E8.V
SUMMARY: VLSSEG3E8.V loads three 8‑bit elements per segment into a consecutive vector register group starting at destination register vd, storing the first, second, and third elements into vd, vd+1, and vd+2 respectively. For each element index i, the bytes are fetched from memory addresses rs1 + i·rs2, rs1 + i·rs2 + 1, and rs1 + i·rs2 + 2, where rs2 is a signed byte‑stride that may be negative, zero, or positive. The three field accesses for each element can be performed in any order, and overlapping source addresses do not affect the architectural result. No special privilege or access‑control restrictions are specified beyond the normal memory‑access permissions.

INSTRUCTION: VLUXEI16.V
SUMMARY: VLUXEI16.V loads 16‑bit SEW elements from memory into the destination vector register vd, using a per‑element address formed by adding the byte offset stored in the corresponding element of source vector register vs2 to the base address in integer register rs1. The operation is masked by vm: only elements whose mask bit is set are written, while cleared bits leave the existing vd contents unchanged. Loads are architecturally atomic; a memory write that occurs after one element has been fetched but before a later element is fetched does not affect the value loaded for the later element. The instruction executes in normal privilege mode with no special access‑control restrictions beyond the usual memory protection.

INSTRUCTION: VLUXEI32.V
SUMMARY: VLUXEI32.V loads SEW‑sized elements into a destination vector register by performing an unordered indexed memory fetch, where each element address is formed from a base‑address register and a 32‑bit element index supplied in a source register. The instruction's statically encoded EEW field determines how the index is interpreted, not the width of the loaded data. Execution is allowed in any privilege mode that permits vector memory accesses, subject to the usual vector‑load access checks. Any fault arising from the indexed address (e.g., page fault or misaligned access) is signaled as a standard load exception.

INSTRUCTION: VLUXEI64.V
SUMMARY: VLUXEI64.V reads a vector of 64‑bit index elements from the indexed source register, adds each to the 64‑bit base address in the base register, and unordered‑loads the resulting addresses into the destination vector register using the current SEW. The instruction groups the index‑vector field, base‑address field, and destination‑vector field as its primary operands. Execution requires the Vector extension to be enabled and is permitted in any privilege mode that allows memory accesses, but it will trap on mis‑aligned or page‑fault addresses. No other side effects are defined beyond the load and any standard memory‑access exceptions.

INSTRUCTION: VLUXEI8.V
SUMMARY: VLUXEI8.V uses the integer register rs1 as a base address and the source vector register vs2, whose elements contain 8‑bit indices, to load SEW‑sized elements into the destination vector register vd. For each element i the effective address is formed by sign‑extending vs2[i] and adding it to rs1, and the byte at that address is fetched. The fetched byte is written to vd[i] only when the corresponding mask bit vm[i] is 1; otherwise vd[i] remains unchanged.

INSTRUCTION: VLUXSEG3EI8.V
SUMMARY: VLUXSEG3EI8.V uses a scalar base register (rs1) and an index vector register group (vs2) whose elements are byte offsets; for each active element i it loads three consecutive bytes from memory addresses rs1 + vs2[i] into successive destination vector registers, forming a three‑field segment. The destination vector register group must have EEW = SEW and EMUL = LMUL, satisfy EMUL × NFIELDS ≤ 8, and must not overlap the index register group. If the index vector's EEW encoding or EMUL scaling is invalid the instruction raises an illegal‑instruction exception; otherwise any memory fault occurring partway through the indexed segment load can be restarted because the EMUL × NFIELDS constraint is met.

INSTRUCTION: VMACC.VV
SUMMARY: VMACC.VV is a destructive overwrite‑addend integer vector instruction that multiplies corresponding elements of source registers vs1 and vs2, takes the low half of each product, adds it to the original value in destination register vd, and writes the sum back to vd for all element positions where the mask register vm is set; elements with vm cleared are left unchanged. The instruction uses three vector registers (vs1, vs2, vd) and a mask register (vm), with vd serving both as the addend and the result register. Execution is subject only to the standard integer‑vector privilege and legality checks, so any illegal‑instruction or privilege violation that would apply to a regular integer vector operation raises the same exception for VMACC.VV. No additional VMACC.VV‑specific traps or exceptions are defined.

INSTRUCTION: VMACC.VX
SUMMARY: VMACC.VX reads a scalar integer register (rs1) and a vector source register (vs2) and writes the result to a destination vector register (vd). For each element i whose corresponding mask bit vm is 1, it adds the low‑XLEN bits of the product rs1 × vs2[i] to the original vd[i]; elements with a mask bit of 0 are left unchanged. The destination register vd is overwritten with the new values, destroying the previous addends. The instruction follows the standard vector‑extension privilege checks and has no additional exception conditions beyond those defined for ordinary vector operations.

INSTRUCTION: VMADC.VI
SUMMARY: VMADC.VI adds the immediate operand to each element of the source vector register vs2, producing a carry‑out for every active element without using a carry‑in. The resulting carry‑out bits are written into the mask field of the destination vector register vd, overwriting all mask bits for the active vector length. The instruction ignores the current mask in v0.mask and updates every element regardless of mask state. Only vs2 and the immediate are read as sources; the destination mask register is not read.

INSTRUCTION: VMADC.VIM
SUMMARY: VMADC.VIM adds each element of the source vector vs2, the encoded immediate, and the corresponding v0 mask bit (carry‑in) and stores the resulting carry‑out as a mask bit in the destination register vd, which is interpreted as a mask register. Because the instruction is encoded with vm=0, it processes and writes back all body elements regardless of any existing element mask. If the operation is not tail‑agnostic, mask bits for element indices ≥ VL are set to non‑zero after execution. The result may be written to any destination vector register that satisfies the architectural source‑destination overlap constraints, with no special privilege or access‑control requirements beyond the standard vector register rules.

INSTRUCTION: VMADC.VV
SUMMARY: VMADC.VV adds each active element of the source vector registers vs1 and vs2 using the current SEW, generates a carry‑out per element, and writes those carry bits into the mask bits of the destination register vd. It updates every body element of vd regardless of element‑wise masking, and when tail‑agnostic handling is not applied the mask bits at indices ≥ VL are set to a defined value (e.g., zero) that can be observed. The instruction is defined only for the unmasked vm field value of 1, and no external carry‑in is added to the sum. Unlike related instructions, the encoding does not reserve vd = v0, so v0 may be used as the destination mask register without special treatment.

INSTRUCTION: VMADC.VX
SUMMARY: VMADC.VX adds the scalar integer in rs1 to each active element of vs2 and writes the resulting carry‑out into the corresponding mask bit of the destination vector register vd. The operation is unmasked (no carry‑in) and always updates the mask field of vd for all body elements, regardless of any mask register. For indices ≥ VL the mask bits are architecturally undefined and no exception is raised. The instruction follows normal vector‑register access rules and does not require privileged execution.

INSTRUCTION: VMADC.VXM
SUMMARY: VMADC.VXM adds a broadcast scalar from integer register rs1 to each element of source vector vs2, optionally including the corresponding mask bit from the implicit mask register v0 as a carry‑in when the instruction is masked (vm = 0); the resulting carry‑out for each element is written into the mask layout of the destination vector register vd. The operation updates all body elements of vd regardless of the mask setting, while for indices < VL the mask bits must equal the result obtained with tail elements zeroed, and mask bits for indices ≥ VL are undefined. Carry‑in bits are taken exclusively from v0's mask layout, and no other registers contribute to the carry. The instruction is a normal user‑level operation with no special privilege or access‑control restrictions.

INSTRUCTION: VMADD.VV
SUMMARY: VMADD.VV performs an element‑wise multiply‑add on vector registers, using the low XLEN bits of the product of each masked vs1 element and the original vd element, then adding the corresponding vs2 element, and writes the result back to vd. For elements where the mask vm is false, the original vd element is left unchanged. The destination register vd is overwritten with the computed results, destroying the original multiplicand values. No additional privilege checks or exceptional conditions are specified beyond the standard mask‑controlled operation.

INSTRUCTION: VMADD.VX
SUMMARY: VMADD.VX reads a vector register (the first multiplicand) and a scalar register, performs an element‑wise multiply‑add, and writes the result back into the same destination vector register vd, overwriting its original contents. The operation follows the normal vector‑unit access rules and is permitted in any privilege mode that allows vector instructions. No special exceptions are defined beyond the standard illegal‑instruction handling for a disabled or mis‑configured vector unit.

INSTRUCTION: VMAND.MM
SUMMARY: VMAND.MM takes two source mask registers, computes a per‑element logical AND of their mask bits, and writes the result to a destination mask register. The instruction is always executed unmasked, does not modify the vlmul field of vtype, and encodings with the vm bit cleared are reserved. Elements with indices less than the current vstart are left unchanged, elements at or beyond the current vector length are updated according to the tail‑agnostic policy, and after execution the architectural vstart register is reset to zero.

INSTRUCTION: VMANDN.MM
SUMMARY: VMANDN.MM computes a mask where each destination element equals the logical AND of the second source mask with the complement of the first source mask, with the source order defined by the instruction form. The destination register may alias either source register, and the operation respects the current vstart index—elements below vstart remain unchanged and vstart is reset to zero after execution. The instruction does not modify vlmul, updates tail elements according to the tail‑agnostic policy, and is always executed with vm=1 (unmasked), with encodings that set vm=0 reserved. No special privilege or access‑control restrictions beyond normal vector register access apply.

INSTRUCTION: VMAX.VV
SUMMARY: VMAX.VV reads signed integers from source vector registers vs1 and vs2, computes the element‑wise signed maximum, and writes the result to the corresponding elements of destination vector register vd. The operation is masked by register vm: for each element i, the computed maximum is stored only if the mask bit i is 1 (or if no mask is supplied); otherwise the original vd[i] remains unchanged. The instruction works exclusively on vector‑vector data and does not impose any additional privilege or access‑control requirements beyond the standard vector register access rules. No other exception behavior is defined beyond the mask‑controlled element write semantics.

INSTRUCTION: VMAX.VX
SUMMARY: VMAX.VX computes the element‑wise signed‑integer maximum between a source vector register and a scalar integer register, writing the result to a destination vector register. It operates in any privilege mode that has the vector extension enabled and imposes no extra access restrictions. The instruction follows the standard vector‑extension legality checks and raises only the usual illegal‑instruction or unsupported‑operation exceptions.

INSTRUCTION: VMAXU.VV
SUMMARY: VMAXU.VV reads two source vector registers, vs1 and vs2, and writes the element‑wise unsigned maximum of each corresponding pair into the destination vector register vd. The operation is performed in vector‑vector mode, requiring both operands to be full‑width vector registers and producing a full‑width vector result. No special privilege or access‑control restrictions are imposed beyond the normal vector‑unit permissions. The instruction does not define any additional exception behavior beyond the standard vector‑operation exceptions.

INSTRUCTION: VMAXU.VX
