SUMMARY: VMAXU.VX computes the element‑wise unsigned maximum between each element of the source vector register vs2 and the unsigned value read from integer register rs1, writing the result to the destination vector register vd. The operation is masked by the vector mask register vm, updating vd[i] only when the corresponding mask bit is 1 and preserving the prior value when the mask bit is 0. The scalar operand from rs1 is treated as an unsigned integer for all comparisons. The instruction is allowed in all privilege modes and does not generate any special architectural exceptions beyond standard vector‑length handling.

INSTRUCTION: VMERGE.VIM
SUMMARY: VMERGE.VIM merges an immediate value with a source vector vs2 into the destination register vd under the control of the mask register v0, using the supplied immediate imm for mask‑selected elements. The instruction is encoded as a masked operation with the vm field fixed to zero, so mask bits are taken from v0.mask[i]. For each element i in the active range vstart ≤ i < vl, vd[i] receives imm when the mask bit is 1 and vs2[i] when the mask bit is 0; elements outside this range remain unchanged. Execution follows the normal vector‑length and privilege rules with no additional restrictions.

INSTRUCTION: VMERGE.VVM
SUMMARY: VMERGE.VVM merges two source vector registers (vs1 and vs2) into a destination register (vd) under control of a mask register (e.g., v0), processing all element indices from vstart up to the current vector length vl. For each processed element, a mask bit of zero selects the corresponding element from vs2, while a mask bit of one selects the element from vs1. The instruction obeys the normal vector‑register access rules and does not require any privileged mode. No additional exceptions are defined beyond the standard bounds checks for vstart and vl.

INSTRUCTION: VMFEQ.VF
SUMMARY: VMFEQ.VF compares each element of the source vector vs2 with the scalar floating‑point value in rs1, writing a mask bit of 1 for equal, non‑NaN results and 0 otherwise; a signaling NaN in either operand raises an invalid‑operation exception. The resulting mask is stored in the destination mask register vd using the standard mask‑element layout, and vd may alias the source mask register v0. For indices i ≥ VL, the mask bits are unspecified (tail‑agnostic). No additional privilege or access restrictions beyond normal vector‑instruction rules are imposed.

INSTRUCTION: VMFEQ.VV
SUMMARY: VMFEQ.VV performs an element‑wise equality comparison between two source vector registers and writes the resulting boolean vector to the destination register identified by the vd field. The operation is predicated by the mask register indicated by the vm field, so only elements with an active mask bit are processed and stored. Execution requires normal vector‑unit privileges; no special privilege level is required. No architectural exceptions are defined for this instruction.

INSTRUCTION: VMFGE.VF
SUMMARY: VMFGE.VF writes a mask vector (vd) with one bit per element indicating whether each active element of source vector vs2 is greater‑than‑or‑equal to the scalar floating‑point value in rs1, and vd may alias the source mask register v0. An optional mask register vm can gate element‑wise execution, leaving destination bits unchanged for masked‑off elements. The instruction raises an invalid‑operation exception if any operand involved in a comparison is a NaN, and in that case the corresponding destination mask bit is written as 0. No special privilege or access‑control restrictions apply beyond normal vector‑instruction execution.

INSTRUCTION: VMFGT.VF
SUMMARY: VMFGT.VF compares each element of vector register vs2 with the scalar floating‑point value in rs1, producing a Boolean mask in destination mask register vd. For each element i < VL, vd[i] is set to 1 if vs2[i] > rs1 and neither operand is NaN, otherwise it is cleared to 0; elements i ≥ VL are forced to 0. Writes to vd occur only where the corresponding mask bit in the input mask register vm is active, and the instruction has no special privilege requirements. An invalid‑operation floating‑point exception is raised if any operand of a comparison is a signaling or quiet NaN.

INSTRUCTION: VMFLE.VF
SUMMARY: VMFLE.VF writes a mask result to a destination mask register (which may be the same register as the source mask register vm) based on element‑wise comparisons of a source vector register against a scalar floating‑point register. The floating‑point less‑than‑or‑equal operation is applied only to elements where the corresponding vm bit is true, leaving other mask bits unchanged. Elements beyond the effective vector length are written as 0 under a tail‑agnostic policy. Any operand that is NaN raises the invalid‑operation exception and forces the resulting mask bit to 0; otherwise a mask bit is set to 1 only when the vector element is ≤ the scalar, and to 0 when it is greater.

INSTRUCTION: VMFLE.VV
SUMMARY: VMFLE.VV compares each active element i of the two source vector registers vs2 and vs1, testing whether vs2[i] ≤ vs1[i], and writes a mask element of 1 for true and 0 for false into the destination mask register vd, which uses the standard mask‑vector element layout. The destination mask register may be the same physical register as the source register v0, and the operation is tail‑agnostic, ignoring elements beyond the current VL. If any element involves a signaling or quiet NaN, the instruction raises an invalid‑operation exception; otherwise, elements with a NaN operand produce a mask value of 0. No special privilege or access‑control checks are required beyond normal vector‑register permissions.

INSTRUCTION: VMFLT.VF
SUMMARY: VMFLT.VF compares each element of source vector register vs2 with the scalar floating‑point register rs1 and writes a boolean mask to a single destination vector register, using the element layout defined in Section 31.4.5. For each active element (as controlled by an optional mask operand vm), the destination mask bit is set to 1 only when the vs2 element is less than rs1 and neither operand is NaN; otherwise the bit is cleared to 0. The instruction raises an invalid‑operation exception whenever any element comparison involves a signaling or quiet NaN, and the corresponding mask bits are forced to 0. Elements masked off by vm remain unchanged, and the operation is available in all privilege modes with no special access restrictions.

INSTRUCTION: VMFLT.VV
SUMMARY: VMFLT.VV compares each corresponding element of the source vector registers vs2 and vs1 and writes the per‑element boolean result into the destination mask register vd, using the standard mask‑vector element layout. The operation is tail‑agnostic because the destination is a mask register. An invalid‑operation exception is raised if any source element is a signaling or quiet NaN; otherwise a mask bit is cleared (0) when either operand is NaN and set (1) when the vs2 element is less than the vs1 element. No special privilege or access‑control checks beyond normal vector‑unit constraints are required.

INSTRUCTION: VMFNE.VV
SUMMARY: VMFNE.VV compares each active element of the two source floating‑point vectors (vs1, vs2) and writes a Boolean result into the corresponding element of the destination mask register vd, with element‑wise activity gated by the mask register vm. A destination mask element is set to 1 if either source element is a NaN (quiet or signaling) or if the two non‑NaN elements differ; it is set to 0 only when the non‑NaN elements are equal. The instruction raises an invalid‑operation exception when any source element is a signaling NaN, while quiet NaNs are treated as ordinary NaNs without raising an exception. The result mask is stored in the standard vector‑mask layout, and for implementations that are not tail‑agnostic the mask bits for indices ≥ VL are defined by the same element‑wise comparison rather than left undefined.

INSTRUCTION: VMIN.VV
SUMMARY: The VMIN.VV vector instruction takes two source vector registers (vs1 and vs2) and writes to a destination vector register (vd) the element‑wise signed integer minimum of the corresponding elements. If a mask register (vm) is omitted, the result is stored to every element of vd; when a mask is present, only elements with a mask bit of 1 are updated, leaving other elements unchanged. The operation follows the normal vector‑extension privilege model with no extra access‑control requirements. No special exception behavior is defined beyond the standard vector‑length handling.

INSTRUCTION: VMIN.VX
SUMMARY: VMIN.VX computes the signed integer minimum of the scalar in rs1 and each element of the vector source register vs2, placing the result in the corresponding element of destination register vd. The operation applies to every element when the mask vm is omitted; otherwise it is performed only for elements where vm is true. Elements for which vm is false retain their original value in vd. No special privilege or access‑control checks are required beyond the standard vector execution rules.

INSTRUCTION: VMINU.VV
SUMMARY: VMINU.VV reads two source vector registers (vs1 and vs2) and writes the element‑wise unsigned minimum of each pair to the destination vector register (vd). The element‑wise computation is masked by the vm register, updating only those elements whose mask bit is true while leaving the others unchanged. The instruction is a regular vector operation available in all privilege modes and requires the vector unit to be enabled; otherwise an illegal‑instruction exception is raised. No other exceptions beyond the standard vector‑length or alignment checks are defined.

INSTRUCTION: VMNAND.MM
SUMMARY: VMNAND.MM computes a destination mask register by applying a logical NAND to each corresponding mask element of the two source mask registers, producing a logical NOT when the sources are the same. The instruction always executes unmasked (vm = 1), may write the result to either source register, leaves elements with indices < vstart unchanged and resets vstart to zero on completion. Elements with indices ≥ vl are updated according to the tail‑agnostic policy, operate on a single mask register independent of the vlmul setting, and do not modify the vlmul field in vtype. No special privilege checks are required and any encoding with vm = 0 is reserved.

INSTRUCTION: VMNOR.MM
SUMMARY: VMNOR.MM operates exclusively on mask registers; using any source or destination register with elements wider than one bit triggers an illegal‑instruction exception. The instruction leaves vlmul unchanged, treats a vm field of zero as reserved, executes unmasked with no inactive elements, and permits the destination register to alias either source register. Elements with indices less than vstart are preserved, vstart is reset to zero after execution, and elements with indices greater than or equal to vl are updated according to the tail‑agnostic policy.

INSTRUCTION: VMOR.MM
SUMMARY: VMOR.MM is a mask‑only vector instruction that writes to a destination mask register (vd) without altering the VL/MUL configuration (vlmul). It sets each mask bit to the logical OR of the corresponding bits in the two source mask registers (vs1, vs2), leaving elements with indices below the current vstart unchanged and resetting vstart to zero after the instruction completes. The operation executes unmasked (no element is inactive) and any result bits whose index is ≥ vl are cleared to 0 in the destination mask. If the vm field is decoded as zero, an illegal‑instruction exception is raised.

INSTRUCTION: VMORN.MM
SUMMARY: VMORN.MM writes a mask register vd where each element is the logical OR of the corresponding element of vs2 with the logical NOT of the corresponding element of vs1, using only the least‑significant bit of each element as a single‑bit value. The operation always uses a single vector‑register width regardless of vlmul, does not change vlmul, and allows vd to alias either source register. Elements with index <i vstart are left unchanged, vstart is reset to zero after execution, and for indices ≥ vl the tail‑agnostic policy still stores the computed result (no element is masked to zero). VMORN.MM is defined only for encodings with the mask bit vm set to 1 (encodings with vm = 0 are reserved) and follows the normal privilege rules for vector instructions.

INSTRUCTION: VMSBC.VV
SUMMARY: VMSBC.VV subtracts each element of source vector register vs1 from the corresponding element of vs2 without a borrow‑in and writes the resulting borrow‑out as a mask bit into the mask field of the destination vector register vd. For every body element the mask bit is set to 1 if the full‑precision difference vs2[i]–vs1[i] is negative, otherwise 0; tail elements are left unchanged. The instruction updates mask bits for all body elements regardless of element‑masking, and the bits are stored according to vd's mask‑register layout. When the vector mask vm is 1 (unmasked execution) the operation does not read any mask bits of v0 and never uses a borrow‑in.

INSTRUCTION: VMSBC.VVM
SUMMARY: VMSBC.VVM computes element‑wise subtraction of the source vector register vs1 from vs2, incorporating a borrow‑in taken exclusively from the corresponding mask bit of the implicit v0 register. The resulting borrow‑out for each element is written to the mask field of the destination vector register vd, which must satisfy the architectural source‑destination overlap restrictions. The instruction is a fully‑masked operation (vm = 0), so the mask register does not inhibit any element processing, and for every element index i ≥ VL the destination mask bit is cleared to zero. No special privilege or access restrictions apply beyond the normal vector register constraints.

INSTRUCTION: VMSBC.VX
SUMMARY: VMSBC.VX reads a signed scalar integer from integer register rs1, sign‑extends it to the element width, and subtracts it from each element of source vector register vs2 to generate a per‑element borrow flag. The borrow flag, defined as 1 when the untruncated difference is negative, is written as a single‑bit mask to the corresponding bit of the destination mask register vd using the standard mask‑register layout, and mask bits are produced for all vector elements regardless of any element‑wise masking. The instruction produces only this mask register as its architectural result and does not write any SEW‑width data. No special privilege checks or additional exception conditions are defined beyond the normal architectural rules.

INSTRUCTION: VMSBC.VXM
SUMMARY: VMSBC.VXM subtracts the scalar x[rs1] from each active element of source vector register vs2, using the mask bit of the implicit v0 register as the per‑element borrow‑in, and writes the resulting borrow‑out into the mask field of destination vector register vd while discarding the data result. After execution the mask bits of vd for indices beyond the current VL are undefined. If any source operand (vs2 or the implicit v0 mask) aliases the destination mask register, the instruction raises an illegal‑instruction exception. No other privilege or access restrictions are imposed by the specification.

INSTRUCTION: VMSBF.M
SUMMARY: VMSBF.M operates only on mask registers, requiring a mask source (vs2) and a distinct mask destination (vd); using any non‑mask register, using the same register for source and destination, or using vd = v0 when masking is active triggers an illegal‑instruction exception. The instruction ignores the vta setting and treats destination tail elements as tail‑agnostic, and it is legal only when the vstart field equals zero, otherwise it raises an illegal‑instruction exception and reports vstart = 0 in the trap record. The computed mask sets each active destination element to true for positions preceding the first true source mask element and to false for that element and all later active positions; if the source contains no true bits, all active destination elements become true. This behavior is commonly used to create a mask that is true up to the first zero‑byte flag generated by vmseq.vi.

INSTRUCTION: VMSEQ.VI
SUMMARY: VMSEQ.VI performs an element‑wise comparison of a source vector register against an encoded immediate; for each element it writes a 1 into the corresponding position of the destination mask register when the comparison is true and a 0 otherwise. The destination register holds the mask result, and when it is the special mask register v0 the new mask bits are AND‑ed with the pre‑existing bits of v0, preserving masked‑off elements (mask‑undisturbed semantics). Bits of the destination mask for element indices greater than or equal to the current vector length (VL) are architecturally undefined on read. The instruction has no special privilege or access‑control requirements beyond normal vector‑unit execution.

INSTRUCTION: VMSEQ.VV
SUMMARY: VMSEQ.VV performs an element‑wise equality comparison of two source vector registers and writes the resulting boolean mask to a single destination vector register that holds mask bits. Each mask element is set to 1 when the comparison is true and to 0 otherwise, and the operation writes every element, following a tail‑agnostic policy. If the destination register is v0, the newly computed mask is ANDed with the existing contents of v0, preserving any bits that were previously zero (mask‑undisturbed behavior). The instruction has no special privilege restrictions and accesses only the indicated vector registers.

INSTRUCTION: VMSEQ.VX
SUMMARY: VMSEQ.VX compares each element of source vector register vs2 with the scalar integer held in integer register rs1 and writes a 1 to the corresponding element of the destination mask register vd when they are equal, otherwise writes 0. The resulting mask is placed in a single vector register using the standard mask‑vector element layout, and any elements with index i ≥ VL are architecturally undefined, allowing the implementation to leave tail elements unchanged. If the destination register is the special mask register v0, the newly computed mask is logically ANDed with the prior contents of v0, preserving zero bits (mask‑undisturbed behavior). The instruction executes under normal vector privilege and accesses only the specified integer and vector registers.

INSTRUCTION: VMSGT.VI
SUMMARY: VMSGT.VI compares each element of a source vector register with a signed immediate and writes a mask element of 1 to the corresponding position of a destination mask register when the source element is greater, otherwise writes 0. The resulting mask vector is stored in a single vector register using the standard mask layout, and the destination register may be the same as the source mask register v0. If a mask operand vm is provided, elements whose mask bit is 0 retain their pre‑instruction value; otherwise all elements are written, and elements beyond the current vector length are ignored under the tail‑agnostic policy.

INSTRUCTION: VMSGT.VX
SUMMARY: VMSGT.VX compares each element of the source vector register vs2 with the signed scalar integer in rs1, writing a mask element of 1 when the vector element is greater than the scalar and 0 otherwise. The resulting mask bits are stored in a single destination vector mask register following the standard mask element layout. No extra privilege checks or architectural exceptions are defined beyond the normal vector‑unit access rules.

INSTRUCTION: VMSGTU.VX
SUMMARY: VMSGTU.VX compares each element of source vector register vs2 with the scalar integer in rs1 using an unsigned‑greater‑than test and writes the result as a mask element (1 for true, 0 for false) into the destination mask register vd. The destination mask vector occupies a single vector register and may alias the source mask register v0; when vd = v0 only the elements enabled by the active mask are updated, leaving disabled elements unchanged (mask‑undisturbed). Elements of the destination mask beyond the effective vector length are not defined by the architecture, so reads of those positions may return any value. No special privilege or access‑control restrictions are specified for this instruction.

INSTRUCTION: VMSIF.M
SUMMARY: VMSIF.M takes a source mask register and produces a destination mask register that is true for every element from the vector start through the first element whose source mask bit is true, and false for all later elements; elements beyond the current VL are cleared to zero (tail‑agnostic). Writing a tail element to any value other than its original, all‑ones, or a value permitted by the agnostic policy leaves that element architecturally undefined. The instruction is only defined when the architectural register vstart is zero; execution with vstart ≠ 0 raises an illegal‑instruction exception. If a trap occurs, vstart must be zero at trap entry, and the cleared‑tail state is observable by reading the destination mask.

INSTRUCTION: VMSLE.VI
SUMMARY: VMSLE.VI compares each element of a source vector with a sign‑extended 5‑bit immediate and writes a 1 to the corresponding element of a destination mask register when the element is less‑than‑or‑equal to the immediate, otherwise writes 0. The resulting mask vector is stored in a single vector register using the standard element layout, and the same register may be used simultaneously as source and destination (e.g., v0). The immediate field is sign‑extended from 5 bits and must be in the range –16 to +15. No special privilege checks or additional exception conditions are defined beyond the immediate‑range requirement.

INSTRUCTION: VMSLE.VV
SUMMARY: VMSLE.VV compares each active element of the source vector registers vs2 and vs1 as signed integers and writes a mask bit of 1 to the corresponding element of the destination mask register vd when vs2[i] ≤ vs1[i], otherwise 0. The operation follows a tail‑agnostic policy, ignoring elements beyond the current vector length, and when vd is the special register v0 the newly computed mask bits are AND‑ed with the existing bits of v0 to preserve undisturbed bits. No special privilege checks are required beyond those for ordinary integer‑compare instructions, and the instruction does not raise any architectural exceptions.

INSTRUCTION: VMSLEU.VI
SUMMARY: VMSLEU.VI compares each element of a source vector register to a 5‑bit unsigned immediate, writing 1 to the corresponding element of a destination mask register when the source element is ≤ the immediate and 0 otherwise, and stores the result mask in a single vector register using the standard element layout. The immediate is sign‑extended from 5 bits and must be in 0‑15; when the sign‑extension high bit is set the permitted unsigned values are (2 × SEW − 16) … (2 × SEW − 1), and any immediate outside the effective VMSLTU.VI range (1‑16) triggers an illegal‑instruction exception. Elements beyond the current vector length are treated as masked off (tail‑agnostic). If the destination register is v0, the new mask bits are ANDed with the existing mask bits (mask‑undisturbed), and the instruction executes under normal privilege with no special access restrictions.

INSTRUCTION: VMSLEU.VV
SUMMARY: VMSLEU.VV compares each element i of source vector register vs2 (left operand) with the corresponding element of vs1 (right operand) as unsigned integers, writing a mask element of 1 to the destination mask register when vs2[i] ≤ vs1[i] and 0 otherwise. The resulting mask vector is stored in a single destination mask register, which may be the same register as v0; when v0 is used as the destination, only mask bits that are already set are updated, leaving cleared bits unchanged. For element positions i ≥ VL (when VL < VLMAX), the mask bits retain their previous values.

INSTRUCTION: VMSLEU.VX
SUMMARY: VMSLEU.VX compares each element of source vector register vs2 with the unsigned value in scalar integer register rs1, writing a 1 to the corresponding element of the destination mask register when vs2 ≤ rs1 and a 0 otherwise. The resulting mask vector is stored in a single vector register using the mask‑element format and the operation is tail‑agnostic. If the destination register is v0, the newly generated mask is ANDed with the existing mask bits, preserving any previously set bits (mask‑undisturbed behavior).

INSTRUCTION: VMSLT.VV
SUMMARY: VMSLT.VV performs an element‑wise signed‑less‑than comparison between the corresponding elements of two source vector registers and writes a mask bit (1 for true, 0 for false) to each element of a single destination mask register. The operation respects the active mask operand (vm), updating only elements whose mask bit is true and leaving other elements unchanged. The destination mask register may be the same as a source register, including v0, and when v0 is the destination the new mask is logically AND‑ed with the existing mask in v0 so a bit stays set only if it was previously set and the comparison is true. No special privilege or access‑control restrictions beyond the normal vector‑unit permissions are defined.

INSTRUCTION: VMSLT.VX
SUMMARY: VMSLT.VX compares each element of the source vector register vs2 with the signed integer in scalar register rs1, producing a per‑element true/false result. The results are written as mask bits (1 for true, 0 for false) into the destination mask register vd, which follows the standard mask‑vector layout, and vd may be the same register used as a source mask (allowing vd = v0). An optional mask operand vm limits updates to elements where vm is true, leaving other elements unchanged. When vd is v0, a mask‑undisturbed policy is applied, AND‑ing the newly computed mask with the existing bits of v0 for elements where vm is false; the instruction operates in normal (non‑privileged) mode.

INSTRUCTION: VMSLTU.VV
SUMMARY: VMSLTU.VV reads two vector registers (vs1, vs2) as unsigned integers, compares each element i of vs2 with the corresponding element of vs1, and produces a mask bit 1 if vs2[i] < vs1[i] otherwise 0. A destination mask element vd[i] is written only when the corresponding mask bit vm[i] is true; if vm[i] is false the existing vd[i] remains unchanged. The destination mask register may alias the mask source register, and when vd is the special mask register v0 the computed bits are logically ANDed with v0's prior contents, preserving any bits not set by the comparison.

INSTRUCTION: VMSLTU.VX
SUMMARY: VMSLTU.VX compares each element of the source vector register vs2 with the unsigned value in scalar integer register rs1, writing a 1 into the corresponding element of the destination mask register vd when the comparison is true and a 0 otherwise. The resulting mask is stored in a single vector register whose element layout follows the definition in Section 31.4.5, and the instruction permits vd and vs2 to be the same register. Execution requires the usual vector‑unit permission checks; if any privilege or legality condition that would fault VMSLT.VX or VMSLEU.VX is violated, VMSLTU.VX raises the same exception (e.g., illegal‑instruction) with identical architectural effects.

INSTRUCTION: VMSNE.VI
SUMMARY: VMSNE.VI compares each element of the source vector register (vs2) with a sign‑extended immediate, producing a mask element that is 1 when the values are not equal and 0 otherwise. The resulting mask vector is written to a single destination vector register, which may be the same register (v0) used as the source mask. The operation follows a tail‑agnostic policy, ignoring elements beyond the current vector length, and when the destination is v0 it updates only mask bits that are set, leaving cleared bits unchanged (mask‑undisturbed behavior). No special privilege or access‑control restrictions are defined for this instruction.

INSTRUCTION: VMSNE.VV
SUMMARY: VMSNE.VV computes a per‑element integer‑not‑equal comparison and writes a 1 for true and 0 for false into a mask stored in a single vector register using the standard element‑width layout. For element indices i ≥ VL the resulting mask bits are undefined, allowing the hardware to leave previous bits unchanged. When the destination is mask register v0, elements not written retain their prior mask bits, effectively ANDing the new result with the existing mask, and the instruction follows the normal vector register access rules.

INSTRUCTION: VMSNE.VX
SUMMARY: VMSNE.VX compares each active element of source vector register vs2 with the integer in scalar register rs1, producing a per‑element not‑equal result. The results are written to destination mask register vd as 1 for true and 0 for false using the vector‑mask layout, and if vd is v0 the new bits are ANDed with the existing mask bits to preserve any zeros. An optional mask operand vm can mask the operation, leaving destination elements unchanged where the corresponding vm bit is zero; the instruction has no special privilege or access‑control requirements beyond normal vector register access.

INSTRUCTION: VMSOF.M
SUMMARY: VMSOF.M writes a mask‑destination vector with tail elements treated as tail‑agnostic and mask‑tail elements treated as agnostic, permitting bit‑granular writes without architectural significance. It inspects the source mask register: if any bit is set, only the lowest‑index element of the destination mask is set to one and all other elements are cleared; if no bits are set, the entire destination mask is cleared. An illegal‑instruction exception is raised when the architectural vstart field is non‑zero or when a tail element is written with a value outside the allowed set. On any trap, the architectural vstart reported to the handler is forced to zero.

INSTRUCTION: VMUL.VV
SUMMARY: VMUL.VV multiplies corresponding SEW‑bit elements from two source vector registers, producing a signed 2 × SEW‑bit product and writes the low SEW bits of each product to the destination vector register. The operation is gated by the mask register vm when present; otherwise all elements are processed. No rounding, scaling, saturation, or rounding‑mode fields affect the result. The instruction imposes no special privilege or exception requirements beyond normal vector execution.

INSTRUCTION: VMUL.VX
SUMMARY: VMUL.VX multiplies each SEW‑bit element of the source vector register vs2 by the signed SEW‑bit scalar in integer register rs1, producing a signed 2 × SEW‑bit product per element. The low SEW‑bit half of each product is written to the corresponding element of the destination vector register vd. The instruction uses the standard vector execution context and imposes no additional privilege or access restrictions beyond those of ordinary vector instructions. No special exceptions are defined for this operation.

INSTRUCTION: VMULH.VV
SUMMARY: VMULH.VV multiplies each signed SEW‑bit element of the two source vector registers (vs1 and vs2) and writes the upper SEW bits of the 2×SEW product to the corresponding element of the destination vector register vd. The result is a plain high‑half signed product with no scaling, rounding, saturation, or overflow signalling, matching the high‑word behavior of the scalar vmulh.vx instruction. The instruction is undefined for 64‑bit element widths in any Zve64* extension. Any trap taken by VMULH.VV in a Zve* extension must be a precise trap.

INSTRUCTION: VMULH.VX
SUMMARY: VMULH.VX multiplies each SEW‑bit signed element of source vector register vs2 by the signed scalar in integer register rs1, forming a 2 × SEW product and writing the high SEW bits to the corresponding element of destination vector register vd. The operation is masked by the vector mask vm, leaving destination elements unchanged where the mask bit is clear, and it performs a plain fractional signed multiply with no scaling, rounding, or saturation. As a member of the mulh class, it writes only the high word of each product to vd and obeys the normal privilege and access rules for vector instructions. On any exception a precise trap is taken: mcause receives the exception code, mepc is set to the VMULH.VX instruction address, and vd is not updated.

INSTRUCTION: VMULHSU.VV
SUMMARY: VMULHSU.VV multiplies each pair of corresponding vector elements, interpreting the element from vs2 as signed and the element from vs1 as unsigned, and writes the most‑significant SEW bits of the 2·SEW‑bit product into the matching element of the destination vector register vd. The operation returns the high half of the product directly, without any scaling, rounding, saturation, or overflow detection. In any Zve64* configuration that allows 64‑bit element width, executing VMULHSU.VV raises an illegal‑instruction exception (mcause = 2). Any trap taken by the instruction is precise.

INSTRUCTION: VMULHSU.VX
SUMMARY: VMULHSU.VX computes, for each element i, the upper SEW bits of the 2 × SEW‑bit product of the signed SEW‑bit value in vector source register vs2[i] and the unsigned SEW‑bit value in scalar register rs1, storing that upper half in the corresponding element of destination vector register vd. The operation is a plain fractional multiply with no scaling, rounding, or saturation, and it never raises an overflow exception. The instruction is undefined for element width EEW = 64 in any Zve64* configuration. If any Zve*‑defined trap (e.g., illegal‑instruction fault, misaligned access) occurs, the processor raises the appropriate exception, updates mcause and mepc, and transfers control to the trap handler according to the Zve* trap model.

INSTRUCTION: VMULHU.VV
SUMMARY: VMULHU.VV computes an unsigned multiplication of each SEW‑wide element of source vectors vs2 and vs1, producing a 2×SEW product and writing the most‑significant SEW bits of each product to the corresponding element of destination vector vd. When a mask vm is specified, only active elements are updated; inactive elements of vd retain their previous values. The instruction is unavailable for EEW = 64 in the Zve64x, Zve64f, and Zve64d extensions, generates no overflow or arithmetic exceptions, and any trap taken is precise.

INSTRUCTION: VMULHU.VX
SUMMARY: VMULHU.VX computes, for each element of a source vector, the unsigned product with a scalar integer and writes the upper SEW‑bit half of the 2 × SEW‑bit result into the corresponding element of the destination vector register. The operation provides no scaling, rounding, or saturation and never raises an overflow exception. It is defined only for element widths of 8, 16, and 32 bits in the Zve64d and Zve32x extensions and is unavailable for 64‑bit elements in Zve64x and Zve64f. No special privilege or access‑control checks are required beyond normal vector‑unit permissions.

INSTRUCTION: VMV.S.X
SUMMARY: VMV.S.X moves the value from a scalar integer register into element 0 of a destination vector register group. The element width (SEW) determines whether the scalar value is truncated, zero‑extended, or sign‑extended relative to the integer register width (XLEN). Elements with indices greater than 0 up to VLEN/SEW are handled as tail elements according to the current tail‑agnostic or tail‑undisturbed policy, and the operation is suppressed when vstart ≥ vl or when vl = 0. The instruction executes in normal user privilege with no special access checks.

INSTRUCTION: VMV.V.I
SUMMARY: The VMV.V.I instruction writes a scalar immediate into every element of a destination vector register. The immediate field supplies the value to be replicated across all vector elements. If the immediate is zero, the destination register is guaranteed to contain zero in every element after execution. No other privilege checks or exception conditions are defined beyond this behavior.

INSTRUCTION: VMV.X.S
SUMMARY: VMV.X.S moves a scalar between an integer x register and element 0 of a vector register, ignoring LMUL and vector‑register grouping. The SEW‑wide source element is copied to the destination integer register; if SEW > XLEN only the least‑significant XLEN bits are retained, while if SEW < XLEN the value is sign‑extended to XLEN bits. The copy occurs unconditionally, even when the current vector length (vl) is zero. No special privilege or access‑control restrictions apply beyond normal execution permissions.

INSTRUCTION: VMV1R.V
SUMMARY: VMV1R.V moves the entire VLEN‑bit vector from source register vs2 to destination register vd, acting as a single‑register (NREG=1) operation with EEW equal to SEW and effective length evl = VLEN/SEW. The instruction bypasses the usual "no write when vstart ≥ vl" rule but is reserved if vstart ≥ evl, while it remains valid when vstart exceeds the largest element index for the current vtype. When vd equals vs2 the instruction has no effect, and the 3‑bit immediate must be zero (any non‑zero encoding is reserved). Execution requires the vector mask bit vm = 1; otherwise an illegal‑instruction exception is raised.

INSTRUCTION: VMV4R.V
SUMMARY: VMV4R.V copies the full contents of each whole vector register (or register group) from the source to the destination, operating with EEW = SEW, EMUL = NREG and an effective length of EMUL × VLEN / SEW. The instruction is only defined when nr = 4, the immediate's low three bits are 3 (so NREG ∈ {1,2,4,8} and simm[4:0] ∈ {0,1,3,7}), and both source and destination registers are aligned to the group size; any other encoding triggers an illegal‑instruction or is reserved. Execution is reserved if vstart ≥ evl, but the operation does not suppress writes when vstart ≥ vl and does not become reserved for other out‑of‑range vstart values. If the destination register equals the source register, the instruction makes no register‑state changes.

INSTRUCTION: VMV8R.V
SUMMARY: VMV8R.V copies the full VLEN bits from each of eight consecutive source vector registers to the corresponding eight consecutive destination vector registers, using the current SEW with an EMUL value of 8, which yields an effective vector length of (8·VLEN)/SEW. The instruction is a no‑op when the destination register number equals the source register number. Source and destination registers must be aligned on an eight‑register boundary appropriate to the register‑group size, and the low three bits of the immediate field must be 7; any encoding that violates these constraints is reserved. No special privilege or access control is required beyond normal vector register access, and the operation does not modify any other architectural state.

INSTRUCTION: VMXNOR.MM
SUMMARY: VMXNOR.MM is a mask‑vector operation that computes the element‑wise XNOR (logical NOT of XOR) of two source mask registers and writes the result to a destination mask register, which may be the same as either source. The instruction is always unmasked (vm must be 1, otherwise an illegal‑instruction exception is raised), operates on a single vector register independent of the vlmul setting, and does not modify the architectural vlmul register. Elements with indices below vstart remain unchanged, vstart is cleared to zero after execution, and elements with indices ≥ vl are still written with the computed result. The pseudoinstruction vmset.m vd is an alias for VMXNOR.MM vd,vd,vd, setting all mask bits in vd to one.

INSTRUCTION: VMXOR.MM
SUMMARY: VMXOR.MM computes the element‑wise exclusive‑OR of two mask registers, writing the result to the destination mask register which also serves as the first source operand. It always reads and writes a single vector register irrespective of vlmul, leaves mask bits with indices < vstart unchanged, clears vstart after execution, and writes tail elements according to the tail‑agnostic policy. The destination may alias either source, and when all three operands are the same register the resulting mask is cleared (all bits zero). The instruction does not modify the vlmul field and treats all elements as active regardless of any mask control.

INSTRUCTION: VNCLIP.WI
SUMMARY: VNCLIP.WI performs element‑wise clipping on a vector and updates the vxsat CSR to indicate saturation; if any destination element saturates, the vxsat bit is set. The vxsat register therefore serves as a status flag for saturation events generated by this instruction. No additional privilege checks or exception conditions are defined beyond the vxsat update.

INSTRUCTION: VNCLIP.WV
SUMMARY: VNCLIP.WV performs its element‑wise computations using the rounding mode specified in the vxrm CSR. If any result saturates, the instruction sets the vxsat bit in the vxsat register to indicate saturation. Both the vxrm and vxsat registers are part of the vector extension's control‑status registers and are accessible in the same privilege mode as other vector instructions. No additional exceptions are defined beyond the saturation flag.

INSTRUCTION: VNCLIP.WX
SUMMARY: VNCLIP.WX reads the shift amount from the low lg₂(2 × SEW) bits of integer register rs1 and, using the rounding mode in the vxrm CSR, rounds each element of source vector vs2 after right‑shifting by that amount. The rounded, shifted values are then saturated to the signed range of the half‑width destination element, discarding any bits beyond that width, and written to vd. If any element saturates, the vxsat bit in the vxsat CSR is set. The instruction is executable in any privilege mode that permits vector and CSR access, and its only side‑effect beyond normal writes is setting vxsat on saturation.

INSTRUCTION: VNCLIPU.WI
SUMMARY: VNCLIPU.WI reads a source vector register and a zero‑extended 5‑bit immediate, writes the narrowed result to a destination vector register, and uses the low lg2(2*SEW) bits of the immediate as a per‑element right‑shift amount (low 6 bits when narrowing from 64‑bit to 32‑bit). The rounding mode is obtained from the vxrm CSR and applied to the least‑significant bit of each destination element before any saturation. After rounding and shifting the value is treated as unsigned; if it exceeds the maximum unsigned value representable in the destination element width it is saturated to that maximum and the vxsat CSR bit is set when any element saturates. The instruction is unprivileged, requires the vector extension to be enabled, and accesses the vxrm and vxsat CSRs, which are readable/writable in all privilege modes.

INSTRUCTION: VNCLIPU.WV
SUMMARY: VNCLIPU.WV is a vector‑narrowing operation that right‑shifts each vs2 element by the low lg2(2×SEW) bits of the corresponding vs1 element (six bits when narrowing from 64‑bit to 32‑bit), rounds the shifted value using the rounding mode specified in the vxrm CSR, and then clips the unsigned result to the destination element width. The rounded value is treated as unsigned; if it exceeds the maximum unsigned value representable in the destination width it is saturated to that maximum and the vxsat flag in the vxsat register is set. The instruction writes the clipped (and possibly saturated) unsigned result to each destination element, requires the vector extension to be enabled, and generates no other architectural exceptions.

INSTRUCTION: VNCLIPU.WX
SUMMARY: VNCLIPU.WX reads each element of the source vector register vs2, rounds it to an unsigned integer using the rounding mode specified in the vxrm CSR, and writes the result to the corresponding element of the destination vector register group. Rounding is performed around the least‑significant bit of each destination element and precedes any saturation. If any element saturates to its maximum unsigned value, the vxsat bit in the vxsat CSR is set. The instruction is a standard vector operation with no special privilege checks and raises no architectural traps beyond the vxsat status update.

INSTRUCTION: VNMSAC.VV
SUMMARY: VNMSAC.VV is a vector‑floating‑point multiply‑subtract‑accumulate instruction that processes elements up to the current active vector length. For each active element it computes the low‑half of the product of the corresponding elements in vs1 and vs2, negates that low‑half product, adds the result to the original value in vd, and overwrites vd with the new value. When a mask vm is present, only elements with a set mask bit are updated; all other elements of vd remain unchanged, and the instruction never uses the high half of the 2×XLEN product. It is available in all privilege modes and raises no exceptions beyond standard vector‑length violations.

INSTRUCTION: VNMSAC.VX
SUMMARY: VNMSAC.VX reads a scalar integer from rs1, a vector operand from vs2, and a destination vector vd. For each element i it computes the low‑half of the product rs1 × vs2[i], negates that product, adds it to the original vd[i], and writes the result back to vd[i]. The instruction follows the normal vector‑extension access rules and may be executed in any privilege mode that permits vector instructions. No additional architectural exceptions are defined beyond the standard vector‑length and illegal‑instruction traps.

INSTRUCTION: VNMSUB.VV
SUMMARY: VNMSUB.VV is a vector arithmetic instruction that reads source vectors vs1 and vs2 and a destination vector vd, using the vector mask vm to select elements. For each masked element i, it computes ‑(low‑half(vs1[i] * vd[i])) + vs2[i] and writes the result to vd[i]; elements not selected by vm remain unchanged. The instruction is executable in any privilege mode without additional access checks. No architectural exceptions are defined beyond the normal illegal‑instruction fault if the encoding is unsupported.

INSTRUCTION: VNMSUB.VX
SUMMARY: VNMSUB.VX is a destructive vector‑scalar multiply‑subtract instruction that overwrites the destination vector register with the computed result. For each active element i it evaluates vd[i] = -(x[rs1] * vd[i]) + vs2[i], using only the low‑order half of the integer product before negation. If a mask operand vm is present, only elements with a true mask bit are written; otherwise all elements up to the current active vector length are updated. No additional exception behavior is defined beyond the standard vector‑length and mask handling.

INSTRUCTION: VNSRA.WI
SUMMARY: VNSRA.WI reads each 2 × SEW element of the source vector register vs2, extracts its lower SEW‑wide half, and writes the result as a SEW‑wide element to the destination vector register vd. The shift amount comes from a zero‑extended 5‑bit immediate (uimm) encoded in the instruction, with the low lg₂(2 × SEW) bits selected (six bits when SEW = 64). The extracted half‑width field is arithmetic‑right‑shifted by this effective amount and then sign‑extended to produce the final SEW‑wide destination element. No special privilege checks or defined architectural exceptions are associated with this operation.

INSTRUCTION: VNSRA.WV
SUMMARY: VNSRA.WV reads a double‑width source vector group (vs2) and, when present, a same‑width shift‑amount vector (vs1), and writes a single‑width result vector group (vd) whose element width matches the current SEW and LMUL settings. For each element the double‑width source value is first narrowed to its lower half, then arithmetic‑right‑shifted by the low lg₂(2 × SEW) bits of the corresponding shift‑amount element, and the result is sign‑extended to fill the SEW‑wide destination element. If the narrowing step is omitted the instruction shifts the full‑width value, yielding an incorrect result; no special privilege or access‑control restrictions are specified.

INSTRUCTION: VNSRA.WX
SUMMARY: The instruction reads a vector register group vs2 whose elements must be exactly twice the destination element width (SEW); otherwise it raises an illegal‑instruction exception. A scalar register rs1 supplies the arithmetic right‑shift count, using only the low lg₂(2 × SEW) bits (six bits when narrowing 64‑bit to 32‑bit). Each selected 2 × SEW element of vs2 is sign‑extended, right‑shifted by that count, and the low SEW bits of the result are written to the corresponding element of destination group vd. Writes are masked by vm, so elements with a cleared mask bit retain their prior values.

INSTRUCTION: VNSRL.WI
SUMMARY: VNSRL.WI reads a source vector register group (vs2) and writes to a destination vector register group (vd), using the low bits of a 5‑bit unsigned immediate as a logical right‑shift amount (6 bits when the source EEW is 64 bits and the destination EEW is 32 bits). The shifted source elements are zero‑extended and the lower SEW bits are written to the corresponding destination elements, with each element operation predicated by vm in the usual mask‑ and tail‑agnostic manner. The instruction raises an illegal‑instruction exception if the source element width is not exactly twice the destination width, if any register other than the group's lowest‑numbered register is encoded, or if overlapping source and destination groups do not satisfy the permitted overlap rules (including the special case where differing EEW forces mask‑ and tail‑agnostic execution).

INSTRUCTION: VNSRL.WV
SUMMARY: VNSRL.WV reads a vector‑group of shift‑amount elements from vs1 and a vector‑group of 2 × SEW source elements from vs2, producing SEW‑wide results in the destination vector‑group vd under the control of mask vm. For each element whose mask bit is true, the low lg₂(2 × SEW) bits of the corresponding vs1 element specify a logical right‑shift amount; the 2 × SEW source element is shifted right with zeros inserted, and the low SEW bits of the shifted value are zero‑extended to form the destination element. Elements with a false mask bit remain unchanged. The instruction completes normally only if all standard vector‑operation checks (supported element width, vtype settings, sufficient VL, etc.) pass; otherwise it raises the same illegal‑instruction or vector‑length exception as other vector instructions.

INSTRUCTION: VNSRL.WX
SUMMARY: VNSRL.WX reads a vector source register group vs2 and a scalar integer register rs1, using the low lg₂(2·SEW) bits of rs1 ( six bits when SEW = 64 and the destination width is 32 bits) as the logical‑right‑shift amount for each source element. Each vs2 element is logically right‑shifted by that amount and the zero‑extended lower half of the result is written to the corresponding element of the destination vector register group vd, whose element width is half the source width. If a mask register vm is present, elements with a cleared mask bit are left unchanged in vd. When rs1 is the zero register x0, the instruction skips the shift and simply halves the integer width of each source element to form the destination elements.

INSTRUCTION: VREDAND.VS
SUMMARY: VREDAND.VS reduces a vector by performing a logical AND between the scalar element 0 of source register vs1 and each masked element of source register vs2, producing a single element result. The operation uses the current vector mask vm to select active elements of vs2, and all operands (vs1, vs2, vd) must share the same SEW width. The final reduction value is written to element 0 of destination register vd, with all other elements of vd left unchanged. No special privilege checks or exception conditions are defined for this instruction.

INSTRUCTION: VREDMAX.VS
SUMMARY: The VREDMAX.VS instruction reads the scalar element vs1[0] and compares it with each active element of vector register vs2, where activity is defined by the mask vm (true mask bits participate, false bits are ignored). The selected element width (SEW) must be identical for vs1, vs2, and the destination register vd. The maximum of these values is written to element 0 of vd, and all other elements of vd remain unspecified. No special privilege or access‑control restrictions apply beyond normal vector register permissions.

INSTRUCTION: VREDMAXU.VS
SUMMARY: VREDMAXU.VS is a vector‑reduction operation that yields a single unsigned maximum value. It reads the scalar element vs1[0] and every element of the source vector vs2, compares them as unsigned integers, and writes the resulting maximum into element 0 of the destination vector vd using the same SEW as the operands. The instruction is defined only when the SEW of vd, vs2, and the scalar element vs1[0] are identical; otherwise it raises an illegal‑instruction exception (mcause = 2). Execution follows the normal privilege model for vector instructions (available in user and supervisor modes).

INSTRUCTION: VREDMIN.VS
SUMMARY: VREDMIN.VS reduces a vector by computing the minimum of the scalar element vs1[0] and all active elements of source vector vs2, storing the result in element 0 of destination vector vd while leaving the other elements of vd unchanged. The instruction requires identical selected‑element width (SEW) for vs1, vs2, and vd, and only those vs2 elements whose corresponding mask bit in vm is set are treated as active. It operates under the normal vector execution privilege and does not raise any special architectural exceptions beyond those of standard vector instructions.

INSTRUCTION: VREDMINU.VS
SUMMARY: VREDMINU.VS performs an unsigned‑minimum reduction, writing the result to element 0 of the destination vector register (vd) while reading the scalar element 0 of source vector register vs1 and the active elements of source vector register vs2 as selected by the mask register vm. All three operands must have the same standard element width; a mismatch raises an illegal‑instruction exception. Any arithmetic overflow that occurs during the reduction wraps around in the same manner as single‑width reduction instructions. The instruction is executable in any privilege mode provided the vector extension is enabled.

INSTRUCTION: VREDOR.VS
SUMMARY: VREDOR.VS performs a vector‑element reduction that computes the bitwise OR of the first element of vs1 with all mask‑active elements of vs2, using vm to select the active elements. The operation requires vd, vs1, and vs2 to share the same standard element width as defined for single‑width reductions. The resulting OR value is written only to element 0 of the destination register vd; all other elements of vd remain unchanged. No special privilege checks or exception conditions are specified beyond the mask‑controlled element selection.

INSTRUCTION: VREDSUM.VS
SUMMARY: VREDSUM.VS reduces a vector by adding each mask‑selected element of source vector vs2 to an accumulator initialized with vs1[0] and writes the final sum to element 0 of destination vector vd. The instruction requires that vd, vs1, and vs2 share the same standard element width, uses the mask vm to enable elements, and performs SEW‑sized integer addition that wraps modulo 2^SEW on overflow. Only vd[0] is updated; no other vd elements change, and the operation carries no special privilege or exception behavior beyond normal vector‑unit rules.

INSTRUCTION: VREDXOR.VS
SUMMARY: The VREDXOR.VS instruction reduces a vector by computing the bitwise XOR of the first element of source register vs1 with the XOR of all active elements of source register vs2, and writes the result to element 0 of destination register vd. The selected element width (SEW) for vs1, vs2, and vd must be identical, and the result is produced in that same SEW. No special privilege level or access‑control checks are defined beyond the standard vector instruction requirements. The operation does not raise exceptions beyond those already defined for vector execution.

INSTRUCTION: VREM.VV
SUMMARY: VREM.VV reads the two source vector registers vs2 and vs1 and writes the element‑wise signed remainder to the destination vector register vd. For every element the produced remainder is identical to the scalar signed‑remainder of the corresponding vs2 element divided by the vs1 element, and this equivalence holds even for extreme operand values. The instruction follows the normal vector‑unit access rules and does not introduce any special privilege requirements or additional exception conditions beyond those already defined for vector operations.

INSTRUCTION: VREM.VX
SUMMARY: VREM.VX reads a signed scalar integer from integer register rs1 and a vector of signed integers from vector register vs2, computes the signed remainder of each element of vs2 divided by the scalar, and writes the resulting signed integers to destination vector register vd. The instruction accesses the normal integer and vector register files and requires no special privilege or access‑control restrictions, behaving as an ordinary unprivileged vector arithmetic operation. For every element, the remainder produced must be identical to the scalar signed‑remainder result defined by the base RISC‑V ISA, even for extreme operand values. Any condition that would raise a scalar remainder exception (e.g., divisor zero or overflow) triggers the same exception for the vector operation.

INSTRUCTION: VREMU.VV
SUMMARY: VREMU.VV is a vector‑vector instruction that computes an element‑wise unsigned integer remainder of each element in source vector register vs2 divided by the corresponding element in source vector register vs1. The computed remainders are written to the destination vector register vd only for elements whose mask bit in vm is active; elements with an inactive mask retain their previous vd value. The result matches the scalar unsigned remainder for every possible input, and the instruction executes in normal (unprivileged) mode without any special exception behavior beyond the defined semantics.

INSTRUCTION: VREMU.VX
SUMMARY: VREMU.VX treats vs2 as a vector of unsigned integers and rs1 as a scalar unsigned integer; for each element i it computes the unsigned remainder vs2[i] % rs1 and writes the result to the corresponding element of vd, leaving vd[i] unchanged when the mask bit vm[i] is clear. The operation observes the same architectural semantics, exceptions, and side‑effects (including division‑by‑zero and overflow) as the scalar unsigned remainder instruction. Execution of VREMU.VX is subject to the same privilege and permission checks as the scalar remainder operation. Masking and element‑wise writes are performed only for elements that are enabled by vm.

INSTRUCTION: VREV8.V
SUMMARY: VREV8.V reads each SEW‑wide element from source vector register vs2, reverses the order of its bytes, and writes the resulting element to the corresponding index of destination vector register vd. When a mask is present, elements whose mask bit is clear are left unchanged, and elements with index ≥ VL are not written, preserving their prior values; execution also respects the vstart field, processing elements from vstart up to VL‑1. The instruction is defined for any SEW that is a multiple of eight bits, and for SEW = 8 the element remains unchanged because it consists of a single byte. VREV8.V modifies only the destination vector register and does not affect any other architectural state.

INSTRUCTION: VRGATHER.VI
SUMMARY: VRGATHER.VI writes each element vd[i] with the element vs2[uimm] selected by the zero‑extended immediate index, producing zero when the unsigned index is ≥ VLMAX and never truncating the index even if XLEN > SEW. When a mask register vm is supplied, destination elements with a cleared mask bit may remain unchanged or take any value, while elements beyond the current vector length vl are handled according to the active tail policy. The instruction can read any source element with index < VLMAX regardless of vl, but writes only to destination elements i < vl. The encoding is reserved if the destination register group overlaps the source register group, and the operation follows the normal privilege and access rules for vector instructions.

INSTRUCTION: VRGATHER.VV
SUMMARY: VRGATHER.VV reads a data vector (first source register group) and an index vector (second source register group) of identical element width and LMUL, optionally using a mask register, and writes the gathered results to a distinct destination register group. For each element i < vl, if the mask bit is true (or the mask‑agnostic policy applies) the element at index vs1[i] is read; if vs1[i] < VLMAX the corresponding data element is written, otherwise zero is written (and for SEW = 8 an out‑of‑range index produces an unpredictable result). Elements i ≥ vl are handled according to the configured tail policy, and mask‑inactive elements follow the mask‑undisturbed or mask‑agnostic policy. The instruction has no special privilege restrictions and the destination register must not overlap any source register; overlapping encodings are reserved.

INSTRUCTION: VRGATHER.VX
SUMMARY: VRGATHER.VX reads an unsigned index from a scalar register (or immediate) and uses it to select a single element from the source vector, writing that element to each masked destination element; if the index ≥ VLMAX the destination element is set to zero. Mask bits control which elements are written, with false mask bits leaving the destination unchanged, and only the first vl elements are updated according to the current tail policy. The index is treated as an XLEN‑bit value without truncation when XLEN > SEW. Encoding is illegal when the destination vector register group overlaps any source vector register group.

INSTRUCTION: VRGATHEREI16.VV
SUMMARY: VRGATHEREI16.VV is a non‑privileged vector instruction that reads a vector of unsigned 16‑bit indices from vs1 and a source vector from vs2, producing each element of the destination group vd as vs2[vs1[i]] when the index is less than VLMAX, otherwise zero. The operation processes up to the current vector length vl, and elements beyond vl are handled according to the active tail policy. When a mask is active, masked‑off elements must either retain their prior value or receive the same value they would have without masking; any other result is illegal. The destination register group must be distinct from both source groups, and any overlap makes the encoding reserved.

INSTRUCTION: VROL.VV
SUMMARY: VROL.VV reads a rotate amount from each element of source vector register vs1 and left‑rotates the corresponding element of source vector register vs2, writing the result to destination vector register vd. The rotate amount is masked with (SEW‑1), i.e., only the low log₂(SEW) bits of each vs1 element are used, and the operation is performed only for element indices i in the active range [vstart, vl), leaving other vd elements unchanged. The instruction is a standard vector‑extension operation and may be executed in any privilege mode that has the V extension enabled. If the instruction fails to retire successfully, an exception is raised and vd does not contain the expected rotated values.

INSTRUCTION: VROL.VX
SUMMARY: VROL.VX reads a scalar rotate amount from integer register rs1, masks it to the low log₂(SEW) bits, and applies that same amount to left‑rotate each active element of source vector register vs2, writing the results to the corresponding elements of destination vector register vd. The rotation is performed modulo the element width SEW, so bits shifted out of the most‑significant position reappear at the least‑significant position. Elements outside the active range vstart…vl‑1 are left unchanged, and the instruction completes normally without raising an architectural exception in any privilege mode that permits vector operations.

INSTRUCTION: VROR.VI
SUMMARY: VROR.VI reads a source vector register and writes the rotated result to a destination vector register, using an immediate rotate amount that is the modular negation of the intended left‑rotate count, thereby producing the same architectural effect as a rotate‑left‑immediate would. Because no VROL.VI immediate encoding exists, this instruction is the sole means to express such rotations. It operates in unprivileged mode with normal vector‑register read/write access and does not raise any special exceptions beyond the standard illegal‑instruction trap for out‑of‑range immediates.

INSTRUCTION: VROR.VV
SUMMARY: VROR.VV rotates each element of the source vector register vs2 right by a per‑element amount taken from the corresponding element of the source register vs1. The rotation amount is masked to the low log2(SEW) bits, and the operation is available in all privilege modes without additional access restrictions.

INSTRUCTION: VROR.VX
SUMMARY: VROR.VX reads a source vector register (vs2) and an integer register (rs1), using the low log₂(SEW) bits of rs1 as a masked rotate amount. For each active element index i from vstart to vl‑1, it right‑rotates the SEW‑sized element vs2[i] by that amount and writes the result to the destination vector register (vd). All element operations use the selected SEW (EEW = SEW) and the instruction does not modify any other architectural state. The instruction completes without raising an exception, retires with RETIRE_SUCCESS, and requires no special privilege.

INSTRUCTION: VRSUB.VI
SUMMARY: VRSUB.VI subtracts each element of the source vector register vs2 from an immediate scalar operand, storing the result in the destination vector register vd. The element‑wise computation runs for all indices i from 0 to the current vector length − 1, using a mask register when present: masked‑off elements leave vd[i] unchanged, while unmasked elements receive imm − vs2[i]. If no mask register is provided, the subtraction is applied to every element of the active vector length, and no explicit privilege or access restrictions are defined.

INSTRUCTION: VS1R.V
SUMMARY: VS1R.V is a vector‑store instruction that transfers the contents of a single vector register to memory. The number of elements written is computed as evl = VLEN / EEW and is used regardless of the current vtype configuration or vl setting. Unlike normal vector operations, the store proceeds even when vstart ≥ vl or when vstart exceeds the largest element index for the current type. The register's lowest‑numbered element is written to the lowest address, with successive elements placed at increasing addresses starting from the address in rs1, subject to the usual privilege and memory‑access checks for stores.

INSTRUCTION: VS2R.V
SUMMARY: VS2R.V stores a group of vector registers to memory, using the scalar register rs1 as the base address and placing the lowest‑numbered vector register at the lowest address with successive registers stored contiguously. The NFIELDS field selects the group size (restricted to 1, 2, 4, or 8 and must be a power of two and aligned to the starting register) and defines the effective vector length as evl = NFIELDS × VLEN ÷ EEW, independent of the current vtype and vl settings. The instruction performs an unmasked unit‑stride store of 8‑bit elements; stores are not suppressed when vstart ≥ vl, though encoding is illegal if the architectural vstart is ≥ evl. A misaligned address exception is generated if the base address is not naturally aligned to the larger of EEW/8 and SEWMIN/8 bytes.

INSTRUCTION: VS8R.V
SUMMARY: VS8R.V stores the full contents of the eight consecutive vector registers v8–v15 to memory, using the scalar integer register rs1 as the base address and writing v8 to the lowest‑addressed location followed by v9–v15 in ascending order. The instruction transfers whole registers regardless of EEW because the in‑register and in‑memory byte layouts are identical, and the nf field denotes the eight‑register group. It is reserved if the registers are not aligned as a vector‑register group or if vstart ≥ VLEN; stores are not suppressed when vstart ≥ vl.

INSTRUCTION: VSADD.VI
SUMMARY: VSADD.VI adds a signed immediate to each signed integer element of the source vector register vs2 and writes the results to the destination vector register vd using the same element width as vs2. If an addition exceeds the representable signed range, the result is saturated to the nearest bound and the vxsat bit in the vector status register is set to indicate that at least one element overflowed. The instruction accesses only vector registers and the status register, is allowed in any privilege mode where the vector extension is enabled, and handles overflow by saturation rather than raising an exception.

INSTRUCTION: VSADD.VV
SUMMARY: VSADD.VV reads signed integer elements from source vector registers vs1 and vs2, adds them element‑wise, and writes the saturated results to destination vector register vd. When an addition exceeds the representable range of the destination element type, the result is clamped to the nearest minimum or maximum value. Any such saturation causes the vxsat status flag to be set. The instruction is a normal vector operation with no special privilege or exception requirements beyond standard vector‑unit access checks.

INSTRUCTION: VSADD.VX
SUMMARY: VSADD.VX reads a signed scalar integer from integer register rs1, a source vector from vector register vs2, and optionally a mask register vm, and writes the results to destination vector register vd. It performs signed integer addition of the scalar to each element of vs2, producing a signed result for each element. When a mask is present, only elements whose mask bit is true are written; elements with a false mask bit remain unchanged, while in the absence of a mask all elements are written. No special privilege restrictions or exception conditions are specified for this operation.

INSTRUCTION: VSADDU.VI
SUMMARY: VSADDU.VI reads a vector source register (vs2) and a sign‑extended immediate, adds the immediate to each unsigned element of vs2, and writes the element‑wise results to the destination vector register (vd). The addition uses unsigned arithmetic; if any element would overflow, that element is saturated to the maximum unsigned value and the vector‑exception status bit vxsat is set. The instruction follows the normal vector‑instruction privilege model and requires no special access rights beyond those for standard vector operations.

INSTRUCTION: VSADDU.VV
SUMMARY: VSADDU.VV adds corresponding unsigned elements from source vector registers vs2 and vs1, writing the results to destination vector register vd. If an addition overflows the element width, the result is clamped to the maximum unsigned value and the vector‑exception status bit vxsat is set. Each element of vd is updated only when the matching mask bit in vm is true; otherwise the element is left unchanged. The instruction operates in any privilege mode and follows the standard vector register access rules with no additional restrictions.

INSTRUCTION: VSADDU.VX
SUMMARY: VSADDU.VX adds an unsigned integer scalar from integer register rs1 to each element of vector source register vs2, writing the saturated results to destination vector register vd. Results that exceed the element‑width maximum are clamped to that maximum and cause the vxsat status flag to be set. The instruction reads rs1 as a standard integer register and vs2/vd as vector registers, subject to the usual vector‑unit enable and user‑mode access permissions. No architectural traps are generated; overflow is handled solely by saturation and flag update.

INSTRUCTION: VSBC.VVM
SUMMARY: VSBC.VVM computes each element vd[i] as vs2[i] minus vs1[i] minus the corresponding mask bit from v0.mask[i]. The destination vector register vd receives the full vector of results, while the mask register v0 only contributes to the subtraction and does not control element writes. The instruction processes all element positions regardless of the mask bits. Encoding is reserved (illegal) when the destination register is v0; otherwise it is a normal, unprivileged vector operation.

INSTRUCTION: VSBC.VXM
SUMMARY: VSBC.VXM subtracts the scalar integer register rs1 and the corresponding borrow‑in bit from each element of the source vector register vs2, using the implicit mask register v0 to supply the borrow‑in bits, and writes the result to every element of the destination vector register vd. The operation ignores the architectural mask bits and always updates all elements, and the implicit mask register v0 is the sole source of carry‑in information. Executing the instruction with vm = 1 (unmasked form) or with vd = v0 is reserved and must not be implemented.

INSTRUCTION: VSE16.V
SUMMARY: VSE16.V stores the 16‑bit elements of vector register vs3 to memory using unit‑stride addressing beginning at the address held in integer register rs1. For each active element i, the target address is computed as rs1 + i × 2 bytes. If a mask register is provided via the vm field (e.g., v0.t), the store is performed element‑wise only when the corresponding mask bit is true; otherwise all elements are written. The instruction does not modify any integer or floating‑point registers and is not restricted to privileged modes.

INSTRUCTION: VSE32.V
SUMMARY: VSE32.V stores 32‑bit elements from a source vector register group (vs3) to memory starting at the address in an integer base register (rs1) using a unit‑stride of 4 bytes per element. The target address for element i is computed as rs1 + i·4, and the store proceeds sequentially for all elements unless a mask operand vm is present. When vm is v0.t, only elements whose mask bit is true are written; if vm is omitted, all elements are stored unconditionally.

INSTRUCTION: VSE64.V
SUMMARY: VSE64.V stores 64‑bit elements from the vector source register group vs3 to memory, using the scalar register rs1 as the base address. Each element is written to consecutive 8‑byte locations (unit stride) beginning at the address in rs1. When a mask register vm is present (v0.t), only elements with a true mask bit are stored; if the mask field is omitted the store is unconditional, as if all mask bits were true. No special privilege or exception behavior is defined beyond the normal memory‑access checks.

INSTRUCTION: VSE8.V
SUMMARY: VSE8.V stores 8‑bit elements from the source vector register group (vs3) to memory starting at the address held in integer register rs1, subject to normal memory‑access permission checks. An optional mask register (vm) using the standard vector‑unit‑stride encoding can control which elements are written, and with the .t qualifier only elements whose mask bit is true are stored, leaving other bytes unchanged. When no mask is present the instruction performs an unmasked store, writing every element sequentially. In the unmasked case a source element that is zero causes a zero byte to be written to the corresponding memory location.

INSTRUCTION: VSETIVLI
SUMMARY: VSETIVLI writes the vector‑type CSR (vtype) with the value supplied in its vtypei operand and writes the vector‑length CSR (vl) with the length computed from the unsigned immediate according to the vector‑length algorithm. The vtype value encodes the mask‑agnostic and tail‑agnostic attributes together with the selected element width (SEW) and register‑group multiplier (LMUL). Any attempt to set the illegal‑type (vill) bit or any reserved high bits in vtype causes an illegal‑instruction exception (mcause = 2). Writes to vtype and vl are CSR accesses that require the appropriate privilege level (machine or supervisor).

INSTRUCTION: VSETVL
SUMMARY: VSETVL takes the vector‑type configuration from rs2, writes it to the vtype CSR (clearing unsupported bits and setting the vill flag on illegal values), and resets the architectural vstart register to zero. It then computes the vector length (VL) from the AVL supplied in rs1 (or uses the maximum unsigned value or the current VL when rs1 = x0) together with the newly written vtype, updates the vl CSR with the result, and writes that VL to rd according to the rs1/rd zero‑register rules. The instruction extracts the element width, LMUL, and the mask‑ and tail‑agnostic attributes from the vtype fields to govern subsequent vector operations. An illegal‑instruction exception is raised if vill is set, any unsupported vtype bits are present, or the resulting VL is not an integer multiple of the required element‑group size.

INSTRUCTION: VSETVLI
SUMMARY: VSETVLI writes a new vector type to the architectural vtype CSR, encoding the element width, LMUL multiplier, and the ta/ma flags that control tail and mask handling for subsequent vector instructions, and then computes the maximum vector length (VLMAX) from the supplied AVL and this vtype, storing the result in the vl CSR. The computed vl is also written to the integer destination register rd unless both rs1 and rd are x0, in which case vl remains unchanged (provided VLMAX is not reduced) while vtype may still be updated. The AVL operand is taken from rs1 when it is not x0, from the maximal unsigned integer when rs1 is x0 and rd is not x0, or from the current vl when both rs1 and rd are x0. An illegal vtype occurs if the vill bit is set, and any non‑zero reserved bits make the resulting vtype value reserved.

INSTRUCTION: VSEXT.VF2
SUMMARY: VSEXT.VF2 reads a source vector register whose elements are half‑width (EEW) of the selected destination element width (SEW) and writes a destination vector register with SEW‑sized elements produced by sign‑extension. The instruction is only defined when EEW is exactly one‑half of SEW; any other width is unsupported. Execution raises an illegal‑instruction exception if the source vector's EMUL does not equal LMUL/2. The resulting destination vector has element width SEW and EMUL equal to the current LMUL.

INSTRUCTION: VSEXT.VF4
SUMMARY: VSEXT.VF4 reads a source vector register (vs1) whose element width is one‑fourth of the destination element width (SEW) and sign‑extends each element to produce a destination vector register (vd) of width SEW. The instruction is valid only when that quarter‑width is a supported size and the resulting EMUL meets the minimum LMUL; otherwise the encoding is reserved. When a mask register (vm) is supplied, sign‑extension is performed only for elements with a true mask bit, leaving masked‑off destination elements unchanged. The instruction is decoded when the vs1 field equals binary 00101 and imposes no special privilege requirements.

INSTRUCTION: VSEXT.VF8
SUMMARY: VSEXT.VF8 reads a vector integer source register whose element width is one‑eighth of the destination element width (EEW = SEW/8) and writes the sign‑extended values into a destination vector register with element width SEW. The instruction's LMUL field specifies the intended vector grouping, and the effective LMUL (EMUL) of the destination must equal this field; a mismatch raises an illegal‑instruction exception. Encodings in which the source EEW is unsupported or the computed EMUL is below the minimum legal LMUL are reserved and not executable. The operation is a sign‑extension variant and executes with the normal privilege level for vector instructions.

INSTRUCTION: VSHA2CH.VV
SUMMARY: VSHA2CH.VV is a vector‑vector operation that reads two source vector register groups and writes the result to a destination vector register group. The destination group must not overlap any source register group, otherwise the encoding is reserved. It is only defined for SEW = 32 in the zvknha extension and for SEW = 32 or 64 in the zvknhb extension; any other SEW makes it reserved, and it raises a RETIRE_FAIL illegal‑instruction exception when LMUL × VLEN is less than the element‑group width.

INSTRUCTION: VSHA2CL.VV
SUMMARY: VSHA2CL.VV writes 4 × SEW bits to destination register group vd, storing for each element the concatenation {a @ b @ e @ f} after performing two SHA‑256 rounds when SEW = 32 or two SHA‑512 rounds when SEW = 64. The instruction is only defined for SEW = 32 in a Zvknha implementation and SEW = 32 or 64 in a Zvknhb implementation, and the destination register group must not overlap either source group vs1 or vs2; otherwise the encoding is reserved. An illegal‑instruction exception with RETIRE_FAIL is raised if LMUL × VLEN < EGW, otherwise the instruction retires successfully.

INSTRUCTION: VSHA2MS.VV
SUMMARY: VSHA2MS.VV is a vector SHA‑2 message‑schedule expansion instruction that, for SEW = 32 operates on SHA‑256 and for SEW = 64 on SHA‑512, reading source vector groups vs1/vs2 and writing four SEW‑sized words (W[19]…W[16]) into the destination vector register group vd as a 128‑bit or 256‑bit result respectively.
