The instruction processes element groups of exactly four SEW elements; if a group does not contain four elements the destination receives an unpredictable value, and only the first (vl/4) groups are updated while later elements are left undefined. Encodings are reserved for the zvknha extension when SEW≠32 and for zvknhb when SEW is neither 32 nor 64, any overlap between vd and the source groups makes the encoding illegal, and an illegal‑instruction exception with retire failure is raised when LMUL·VL is smaller than the element‑group width. The computation of W[16]–W[19] uses SEW‑dependent sig0 and sig1 functions defined by specific rotate‑right and logical‑right shifts.

INSTRUCTION: VSLIDE1DOWN.VX
SUMMARY: VSLIDE1DOWN.VX slides each active element of source vector register vs2 one position toward lower indices, writing the result into destination vector register vd; elements with index i < vstart remain unchanged, and for vstart ≤ i < vl‑1 the new value is taken from vs2[i+1] only when the corresponding mask bit is set. The element at index vl‑1 is replaced with the scalar integer register x[rs1] after XLEN‑to‑SEW conversion (sign‑extend if XLEN<SEW, truncate if XLEN>SEW) provided its mask bit is enabled; otherwise it is left undisturbed. Elements with index i ≥ vl follow the configured tail policy, and the instruction never accesses memory and modifies only the destination register group. All operations respect the current mask and tail policies and are subject to the usual privilege checks for vector‑scalar instructions.

INSTRUCTION: VSLIDE1UP.VX
SUMMARY: VSLIDE1UP.VX writes the scalar register rs1 into element 0 of the destination vector register group vd (sign‑extending when XLEN < SEW or truncating when XLEN > SEW) if element 0 is active, otherwise it follows the mask‑agnostic/undisturbed policy. For each active element i with 0 ≤ i < vl‑1, the instruction copies vs2[i] to vd[i+1], implementing a one‑element upward shift, while elements with index ≥ vl are left untouched and handled by the current tail policy. Overlap between the source vector group vs2 and the destination group vd is illegal, and such an encoding is reserved. The instruction operates under the normal vector‑unit privilege and mask/tail handling rules without additional access restrictions.

INSTRUCTION: VSLIDEDOWN.VI
SUMMARY: VSLIDEDOWN.VI forms a source start index by zero‑extending the 5‑bit immediate to XLEN and reads elements from the source vector register vs2, supplying zero for any index that exceeds VLMAX. The instruction writes at most the current vector length vl into the destination register vd, leaving elements with index i < vstart unchanged, writing elements vstart ≤ i < vl only when the corresponding mask bit in v0.mask is enabled, and handling elements i ≥ vl according to the active tail policy. Out‑of‑range source accesses (i + OFFSET ≥ VLMAX) produce zero, and the same tail‑policy rules apply to destination elements beyond vl.

INSTRUCTION: VSLIDEDOWN.VX
SUMMARY: VSLIDEDOWN.VX uses the full‑XLEN unsigned value in integer register rs1 as an offset to read elements from source vector register vs2 and write them to destination vector register vd. For each element index i with vstart ≤ i < vl, it selects src[i] = vs2[i + OFFSET] when i + OFFSET is within VLMAX (otherwise src[i] = 0) and writes src[i] to vd[i] only if the corresponding mask bit is enabled, leaving indices i < vstart unchanged. Writes are limited to the current vl; elements i ≥ vl are processed according to the configured tail policy. If vstart ≥ vl, no element writes occur and only the tail policy is applied.

INSTRUCTION: VSLIDEUP.VI
SUMMARY: VSLIDEUP.VI uses a 5‑bit unsigned immediate (uimm) that is zero‑extended to XLEN as the slide offset, and when XLEN > SEW the offset is not truncated to SEW bits. Up to vl destination elements may be written: for each index i with OFFSET ≤ i < vl, vd[i] is set to vs2[i‑OFFSET] only if the mask bit is enabled and i ≥ max(vstart,OFFSET); elements i < max(vstart,OFFSET) remain unchanged when vstart < vl. The destination vector register group must not overlap the source group (overlapping encodings are reserved), and mask‑disabled elements retain their previous value. Destination elements with indices i ≥ vl but < VLMAX become undefined without raising an exception.

INSTRUCTION: VSLIDEUP.VX
SUMMARY: VSLIDEUP.VX takes an unsigned start index OFFSET from integer register rs1 and slides source‑vector elements upward into a non‑overlapping destination‑vector register group, respecting the current vstart, vl, mask, and tail‑policy settings. The OFFSET is used directly (no SEW‑bit truncation even when XLEN > SEW) and the instruction writes at most vl elements, populating destination indices i from 0 up to min(vl, max(vstart, OFFSET)) with the source element i‑OFFSET when the mask bit for i is enabled. Destination elements with indices between max(vstart, OFFSET) and vl‑1 remain unchanged, and elements beyond vl up to VLMAX are processed according to the configured tail‑policy. Encodings that cause the source and destination vector register groups to overlap are reserved and raise an illegal‑instruction exception.

INSTRUCTION: VSLL.VI
SUMMARY: VSLL.VI shifts each element of the source vector register group (vs2) left by a constant amount and writes the results to the destination vector register group (vd). The shift amount is supplied by the 5‑bit immediate field uimm, which is zero‑extended and then masked to the low lg2(SEW) bits, where SEW is the current element width. The instruction is subject to the normal vector‑unit access and privilege checks; no additional architectural exceptions are defined beyond those already applicable to vector operations.

INSTRUCTION: VSLL.VV
SUMMARY: VSLL.VV reads element‑wise shift amounts from vector register vs1 and the data to be shifted from vs2, writing the shifted results to destination vector register group vd. For each element, only the low lg2(SEW) bits of the corresponding shift‑amount value are used, and the operation performs a purely logical left shift without sign‑ or zero‑extension. The instruction is a regular vector operation with no special privilege requirements and no defined architectural exceptions.

INSTRUCTION: VSLL.VX
SUMMARY: VSLL.VX reads the shift amount from the scalar integer register rs1, using only the low lg₂(SEW) bits as the effective shift count. It logically left‑shifts each element of the source vector register vs2 by that amount and writes the results to the destination vector register group vd. No special privilege or exception behavior beyond the normal architectural checks is defined for this instruction.

INSTRUCTION: VSM.V
SUMMARY: VSM.V uses the integer register rs1 as the base address and stores a byte‑wide vector whose length is ceil(vl/8) elements. It performs an unmasked byte store (EEW = 8) and treats the architectural vstart as a byte count, incrementing it by one for each element processed. The effective vector length is evl = ceil(vl/8) (EMUL = 1), and execution is illegal—and raises an illegal‑instruction exception—if the vill bit in vtype is set.

INSTRUCTION: VSM3C.VI
SUMMARY: VSM3C.VI compresses an SM3 state and a 256‑bit message block supplied in source vector registers into eight 32‑bit state words written to a destination vector register. The instruction is only defined for SEW = 32, requires each element group to be exactly eight 32‑bit elements (256 bits), a non‑overlapping destination register group, and LMUL·VL ≥ 256; otherwise an illegal‑instruction exception (or RETIRE_FAIL) is raised. It processes vl/EGS element groups starting at vstart/EGS, byte‑swaps each input word from big‑ to little‑endian before the SM3 round computation selected by the 5‑bit immediate rnds, and applies the appropriate GG function per round index. After compression it byte‑swaps the eight result words back to big‑endian before writing them to the destination register, leaving any unprocessed elements unchanged.

INSTRUCTION: VSM3ME.VV
SUMMARY: VSM3ME.VV operates on vector register groups, using a destination register group and a source register group vs2, and is defined only for a standard element width of 32 bits. The instruction raises an illegal-instruction exception and fails to retire when the product of LMUL and VLEN is less than the required 256-bit effective group width, and it retires successfully when that product is at least 256 bits. Encodings with SEW not equal to 32 bits or with the destination group overlapping the source vs2 group are reserved and must not be executed. No other privilege or access restrictions are specified beyond the illegal-instruction condition.

INSTRUCTION: VSM4K.VI
SUMMARY: VSM4K.VI operates on vector element groups that are exactly 128 bits (four 32‑bit elements) and writes the computed result to a destination vector register. The instruction is only defined when both the vector length (vl) and the start index (vstart) are multiples of four; any deviation causes an illegal‑instruction exception. If the hardware processes a number of element groups other than vl / EGS, the destination register's contents are undefined and will not match the result of processing exactly vl / EGS groups. No special privilege mode is required beyond the normal illegal‑instruction checks.

INSTRUCTION: VSM4R.VS
SUMMARY: VSM4R.VS is a vector‑scalar instruction that uses a scalar element‑group operand (EMUL = ceil(128/VLEN)) and a source register group vs2 to produce, for each element, a 128‑bit SM4 round value (x7 @ x6 @ x5 @ x4) written to the destination register group vd. It is defined only for SEW = 32, requires LMUL·VLEN ≥ 128, and mandates that both vl and vstart be multiples of 4 (with the scalar operand needing LMUL alignment only when VLEN < 128). Overlap of vd with the source group containing vs2 (the .vs form) makes the encoding reserved and, if executed, raises an illegal‑instruction exception (mcause = 2). Any violation of the size, alignment, or overlap constraints triggers an illegal‑instruction exception; otherwise the instruction executes in any privilege mode without additional access checks.

INSTRUCTION: VSM4R.VV
SUMMARY: VSM4R.VV consumes four round‑key registers (x7, x6, x5, x4) and, for each element‑group i, writes the assembled 128‑bit value back to the corresponding group of destination vector register Vd. In the .vv variant the round keys are read from source vector Vs2 in the natural order rk0‑rk3, defining encryption versus decryption. The instruction is only legal when the effective element width (LMUL × VLEN) is at least 128 bits; otherwise it raises an illegal‑instruction exception and forces RETIRE_FAIL. When all element groups are processed the instruction retires successfully (RETIRE_SUCCESS).

INSTRUCTION: VSMUL.VV
SUMMARY: VSMUL.VV performs element‑wise multiplication on vector registers and sets the vector‑status flag vxsat when any element's result saturates. The instruction is defined only for effective element widths of 8, 16, and 32 bits in any Zve* extension and is explicitly unavailable for EEW = 64. If any trap condition occurs, a precise trap is taken: the instruction does not retire, PC is redirected to the trap handler, and mcause receives the corresponding exception code. No other privilege or access restrictions beyond the standard vector‑extension availability are imposed.

INSTRUCTION: VSMUL.VX
SUMMARY: VSMUL.VX multiplies each SEW‑width element of the source vector register vs2 by the signed scalar in integer register x[rs1], forming a double‑width product that is right‑shifted by SEW‑1, rounded, and saturated to SEW bits, with the final value written to the destination vector register vd. If any element saturates, the vector‑exception status bit vxsat is set. The instruction is present in all Zve* extensions except when the effective element width is 64 bits in a Zve64* implementation. Execution incurs precise traps on all supported extensions and has no additional privilege restrictions.

INSTRUCTION: VSOXEI16.V
SUMMARY: VSOXEI16.V stores each element from vector register vs3 to memory addresses formed by adding the 16‑bit index element taken from the corresponding element of vector register vs2 to the base address in integer register rs1. The EEW field is decoded as the width of the index (fixed at 16 bits) and does not affect the width of the stored data. The instruction requires normal vector‑store access rights and can execute in any privilege mode that permits the underlying memory stores. A memory‑access fault is raised if any computed address is mis‑aligned or not writable.

INSTRUCTION: VSOXEI32.V
SUMMARY: VSOXEI32.V stores each element from the source vector register group vs2 to memory using an address formed by adding the 32‑bit index element from the index vector vs3 to the base address in rs1, and the store width equals the current SEW. The stores are performed as ordered operations, so they become globally visible in program order relative to other memory accesses. Execution follows the normal vector‑extension privilege and access‑control checks without additional restrictions. Any fault (e.g., misaligned address or access‑fault) is handled by the standard RISC‑V memory‑access exception mechanism.

INSTRUCTION: VSOXEI64.V
SUMMARY: VSOXEI64.V performs an ordered store of 64‑bit elements from the source vector register vs3. For each element i, the effective address is calculated as the integer base register rs1 plus the byte‑offset taken from element i of the index vector register vs2. The operation obeys the usual memory‑access permission checks and raises a fault on any address‑translation, protection, or alignment error.

INSTRUCTION: VSOXEI8.V
SUMMARY: VSOXEI8.V writes each element of the source vector register to memory at addresses base + index × (SEW/8), storing exactly SEW bits (SEW/8 bytes) per element in increasing index order. The index operand is an 8‑bit element width defined by the statically encoded EEW field and is independent of the data element width. The instruction operates under normal memory‑access permissions and imposes no additional privilege constraints. If the implementation does not store the elements in program order, the bytes at successive offsets from the base address will contain the vector elements out of their original sequence.

INSTRUCTION: VSRA.VI
SUMMARY: The VSRA.VI instruction performs an arithmetic right shift on each element of a source vector register group, writing the shifted results to a destination vector register group. The shift amount is taken from a zero‑extended 5‑bit immediate (uimm) and reduced to the low lg2(SEW) bits to form the effective shift count. Vacated bit positions are filled by sign‑extending the original element's most‑significant bit. It is a normal vector operation with no special privilege restrictions and does not raise an exception for out‑of‑range immediates, as the masking guarantees a valid shift amount.

INSTRUCTION: VSRA.VV
SUMMARY: VSRA.VV reads the destination vector register as the data source and uses the vector register group indicated by vs1 to supply per‑element shift amounts, writing the shifted results back to the destination register group. Each element is arithmetic right‑shifted with sign‑extension, and only the low lg2(SEW) bits of the corresponding shift‑amount element determine the shift count. The instruction requires the vector extension to be enabled and does not raise any additional architectural exceptions.

INSTRUCTION: VSRA.VX
SUMMARY: VSRA.VX reads a vector register group (vs2) and a scalar integer register (rs1), applies a sign‑extending arithmetic right shift to each element of vs2 using the shift amount supplied by the low lg2(SEW) bits of rs1, and writes the resulting elements to the destination vector register group (vd). The remaining bits of rs1 are ignored. The instruction executes under the normal integer‑vector privilege model with no extra access restrictions. No architectural exceptions are specified for the shift operation itself.

INSTRUCTION: VSRL.VI
SUMMARY: The VSRL.VI instruction logically right‑shifts each element of the source vector register group vs2 by a shift amount derived from the zero‑extended 5‑bit immediate uimm, using only the low lg2(SEW) bits of that immediate. The shifted results are written to the destination vector register group vd, and the vector mask vm controls which elements are updated, leaving masked‑off elements unchanged. Execution of VSRL.VI is not privileged and has no special access‑control restrictions. No architectural exceptions are specified for normal operation.

INSTRUCTION: VSRL.VV
SUMMARY: VSRL.VV reads a source vector from register group vs1 and writes the shifted result to the destination vector register group vd, using the corresponding element of vs1 as the per‑element shift amount. Only the low lg2(SEW) bits of each shift‑amount element are used, effectively masking larger values, and the operation performs a logical right shift, inserting zeros into the vacated bits. The instruction is available in all privilege modes with no special access‑control restrictions. No exception is raised for shift amounts outside the element width because they are masked before the shift.

INSTRUCTION: VSRL.VX
SUMMARY: VSRL.VX shifts each element of the source vector register group (vs2) right logically, inserting zeros into the vacated most‑significant bits, and writes the results to the destination vector register group (vd). The shift amount for all elements is taken from the low lg2(SEW) bits of the scalar integer register rs1. The instruction operates under the normal vector‑instruction access model with no special privilege restrictions and raises only the standard architectural exceptions.

INSTRUCTION: VSSE16.V
SUMMARY: VSSE16.V stores 16‑bit elements from a vector source register to memory using a base address register and a signed stride field, which may be negative or zero. The instruction performs a normal memory write, so if the target address is outside an RVWMO idempotent region, any later load by another agent must return the value written by VSSE16.V. No special privilege or access‑control checks are required beyond the standard memory permission checks. Negative or zero stride values are accepted without raising an exception.

INSTRUCTION: VSSE32.V
SUMMARY: VSSE32.V stores 32‑bit vector elements to memory using a base address register and a stride field that may be negative, zero, or positive. The stride field determines the address offset applied between successive element stores. The instruction is unprivileged and must appear to perform each individual memory write, so any memory‑access fault is taken as if each access occurred.

INSTRUCTION: VSSE64.V
SUMMARY: VSSE64.V stores 64‑bit vector elements to memory using a base address and a stride supplied by register rs2, which may be positive, negative, or zero. When rs2 ≠ x0 and the stride register contains zero, the implementation must issue one write for each active element to the same address, so the final memory location holds the last element's value and the write count equals the number of active elements. If the instruction omits a store for any element address, the original memory contents must remain unchanged and be observable by a subsequent load. For accesses to non‑idempotent memory regions, every element store must be performed, and the mapped device must observe the full sequence of writes.

INSTRUCTION: VSSE8.V
SUMMARY: VSSE8.V stores 8‑bit elements from a source vector register to memory using a base address in rs1 and a stride taken from integer register rs2, which may be negative, zero, or positive. When rs2 is not x0 and the value in x[rs2] is zero, the instruction still issues one store per active element, though the stores are unordered. The operation obeys the standard RVWMO memory‑ordering and privilege checks for store instructions, and all defined stores must be performed for non‑idempotent memory regions; omission is allowed only for idempotent regions where no architectural effect would be observed. If a store to a non‑idempotent region is omitted, the corresponding memory byte retains its original value.

INSTRUCTION: VSSEG3E32.V
SUMMARY: VSSEG3E32.V takes the base address from integer register rs1 and writes a packed vector of three 4‑byte segments to consecutive memory locations starting at that address. The instruction follows the normal memory‑access rules of the current privilege mode, requiring the destination to be writable and properly aligned. A memory‑access fault (e.g., page fault or misaligned address) is raised if the store cannot be completed.

INSTRUCTION: VSSRA.VI
SUMMARY: VSSRA.VI takes a zero‑extended 5‑bit immediate, extracts the low lg2(SEW) bits as the effective shift amount, and performs a sign‑extending arithmetic right shift on each element of the source vector register group vs2 that is enabled by the mask register vm. The shifted result is rounded according to the current vector rounding‑mode register vxrm and written to the corresponding element of the destination vector register vd, while elements with a false mask bit remain unchanged. No special privilege or access‑control requirements are defined for this instruction. No architected exceptions are specified for normal execution of VSSRA.VI.

INSTRUCTION: VSSRA.VV
SUMMARY: VSSRA.VV is a vector arithmetic instruction that writes the destination vector register vd, reading per‑element shift amounts from the source vector register group vs1 and the values to be shifted from the source vector register group vs2. For each element i it performs a sign‑extending arithmetic right shift of vs2[i] by the low lg2(SEW) bits of vs1[i], with the bits shifted out rounded according to the current vxrm rounding mode. The instruction obeys the configured element width (SEW) and vector length, and is subject only to the standard vector‑unit enable and privilege checks; no extra architectural exceptions are defined.

INSTRUCTION: VSSRL.VI
SUMMARY: VSSRL.VI is a vector instruction that writes to destination register vd, reads source register vs2, uses a 5‑bit unsigned immediate uimm, and may be masked element‑wise by vm. For each active element, the shift amount is the low lg2(SEW) bits of the zero‑extended immediate and the element is logically right‑shifted with zero‑extension, applying the unsigned rounding defined by roundoff_unsigned. Elements masked off by vm remain unchanged. The operation is permitted in any privilege mode that enables the vector extension and raises only the standard illegal‑instruction fault if the vector unit is unavailable.

INSTRUCTION: VSSRL.VV
SUMMARY: VSSRL.VV shifts each element of the source vector register group vs2 right by a per‑element amount taken from the corresponding element of the vector register group vs1, using only the low lg2(SEW) bits of each shift‑amount element. The shift is logical, the discarded bits are rounded according to the current rounding mode (vxrm), and the resulting value is zero‑extended to the destination element width. The rounded, zero‑extended results are written to the destination vector register group vd, with writes optionally masked by the vector mask vm so that masked‑off elements remain unchanged. This is an ordinary unprivileged vector instruction with no extra privilege restrictions.

INSTRUCTION: VSSRL.VX
SUMMARY: VSSRL.VX shifts each element of the source vector register vs2 right logically by the amount specified in the low lg2(SEW) bits of the scalar integer register rs1, producing a zero‑extended unsigned intermediate value. The logically shifted result for each element is then rounded according to the current vxrm rounding mode, and the rounded value is written to the corresponding element of the destination vector register group vd. The instruction uses normal vector‑register read/write permissions and does not invoke any special privilege checks or exceptional conditions beyond the standard rounding behavior.

INSTRUCTION: VSSSEG2E32.V
SUMMARY: VSSSEG2E32.V is a strided segment store that writes 32‑bit elements from the second segment of the source vector register v3[i] to memory. The base address comes from rs1 and a signed byte‑stride from rs2 (which may be negative, zero, or positive); for each element i (0 ≤ i < VL) the store address is rs1 + i*rs2 + s*4 bytes, where s = 0 selects the first segment and s = 1 selects the second (offset of 4 bytes). The operation obeys the normal load‑store privilege and address‑translation checks, and any misaligned or otherwise illegal address causes a memory‑access exception.

INSTRUCTION: VSSUB.VV
SUMMARY: VSSUB.VV reads signed integer elements from source vector registers vs1 and vs2, subtracts each vs2 element from the corresponding vs1 element, and writes the results to destination vector register vd. If a subtraction exceeds the signed range of the destination element type, the result is saturated to the nearest representable value. Any saturated element sets the architectural vxsat flag. The instruction uses the standard architectural vector registers and imposes no special privilege or access‑control restrictions.

INSTRUCTION: VSSUB.VX
SUMMARY: VSSUB.VX reads a signed scalar value from rs1 and a vector operand from vs2, writing results to vd while respecting the element‑wise mask vm. For each mask‑true element it computes the signed subtraction vd[i] = vs2[i] − rs1, leaving mask‑false elements unchanged. If any subtraction exceeds the destination type's range, the element is saturated to the nearest representable value and the vxsat status bit is set; otherwise vxsat is left unchanged. The instruction is a regular, non‑privileged vector operation that does not generate architectural traps beyond the optional saturation flag.

INSTRUCTION: VSSUBU.VV
SUMMARY: VSSUBU.VV subtracts each unsigned element of source vector register vs1 from the corresponding element of source vector register vs2, writing the saturated results to destination vector register vd. The operation performs element‑wise unsigned subtraction and, if any result falls outside the representable range of the destination element type, the result is clamped to the nearest representable value and the vxsat bit in the vector status register is set. No additional privilege or access‑control checks are defined for this instruction. This provides a saturating unsigned subtraction across all vector elements.

INSTRUCTION: VSSUBU.VX
SUMMARY: VSSUBU.VX subtracts the unsigned integer scalar in integer register rs1 from each unsigned element of source vector register vs2 and writes the element‑wise saturated unsigned results to destination vector register vd. If a subtraction would exceed the representable range of the destination element width, the result is replaced by the nearest unsigned value and the vxsat flag is set; otherwise the flag remains cleared. The instruction operates as an ordinary unprivileged vector instruction, requiring only normal read/write access to the involved vector and integer registers.

INSTRUCTION: VSUXEI16.V
SUMMARY: VSUXEI16.V stores vector data to memory using unordered 16‑bit indices, with the stored element size set by the current SEW. The `ei` suffix means the statically encoded EEW applies to the index elements rather than to the data elements. The instruction reads a base address register, a source vector register for the data to be stored, and an index vector register, and writes the indexed values to memory. It is available in all privilege modes and only raises the usual memory‑access exceptions.

INSTRUCTION: VSUXEI32.V
SUMMARY: VSUXEI32.V scatters 32‑bit elements from a source vector register group (vs3) to memory addresses computed as the scalar base address in rs1 plus a byte offset taken from a second vector register group (vs2), with the offsets scaled for the 32‑bit element width. The instruction is only defined for EEW = 32; any other EEW value triggers an illegal‑instruction exception (mcause = 2). A mask register (e.g., v0.t) predicates each element store, and elements with a false mask bit are not written, leaving memory unchanged.

INSTRUCTION: VSUXEI64.V
SUMMARY: VSUXEI64.V stores 64‑bit elements from vector register vs3 to memory addresses computed as rs1 + (vs2[i] * 8), where the index element width is defined by the statically‑encoded EEW field while the stored data width follows the current SEW. A mask register vm controls which elements are written, with only vm[i]=1 causing a store for element i. The individual element stores are unordered; the architecture provides no ordering guarantee, so memory may contain the stored values in any permutation. The instruction is subject to the normal privilege and memory‑access checks, and the alias VSXEI64.V produces the identical architectural effect.

INSTRUCTION: VSUXEI8.V
SUMMARY: VSUXEI8.V stores 8‑bit elements using the scalar register rs1 as a base address, while the vector register vs2 supplies a byte offset for each element and vs3 provides the data to be written. The vector mask vm selects which elements are active; for each active element the 8‑bit value from vs3[i] is stored to memory at address rs1 + vs2[i], and inactive elements generate no store. All memory accesses are subject to the normal privilege and permission checks, and no additional exceptions are defined beyond the mask‑controlled behavior.

INSTRUCTION: VSUXSEG2EI32.V
SUMMARY: VSUXSEG2EI32.V stores two 32‑bit fields of a destination vector register group (first field from v2[i], second from v3[i]) to memory addresses formed by adding the scalar base in rs1 to the byte offset in the corresponding element of the index vector register group (v5[i]), with the second field further offset by 4 bytes (field_index × 32‑bit width). The instruction is legal only when the index group's EMUL equals (EEW/SEW)·LMUL for EEW = 32 and when EMUL·2 ≤ 8; otherwise an illegal‑instruction exception is raised (mcause = 2 for the EMUL‑size violation). Overlap between the destination data registers and the index register group is permitted, and no special privilege restrictions apply beyond the normal instruction‑fetch checks.

INSTRUCTION: VWADD.VV
SUMMARY: VWADD.VV reads corresponding SEW‑wide elements from source vector registers vs1 and vs2, sign‑extends each operand to 2×SEW bits, adds them to produce a signed 2×SEW result, and writes each sum to the matching element of destination register vd. The element‑wise mask field vm controls which additions are performed, leaving masked‑off destination elements unchanged. The instruction accesses only vector registers and is allowed in any privilege mode; no special architectural exceptions are defined for this operation.

INSTRUCTION: VWADD.VX
SUMMARY: VWADD.VX reads a vector register (vs2) and a scalar integer register (rs1), sign‑extends each SEW‑wide element of both operands to double width, adds them element‑wise, and writes the 2*SEW results to a destination vector register (vd). A mask register (vm) selects active elements; only those positions are written, while masked‑off elements of vd retain their previous values. The result width is exactly twice the standard element width of the source operands. When rs1 is x0 the scalar contributes zero, so the operation writes a double‑width copy of the sign‑extended vs2 elements to vd.

INSTRUCTION: VWADD.WX
SUMMARY: VWADD.WX reads a signed scalar from integer register rs1 and signed SEW‑width elements from vector register vs2, adds the scalar to each element, and writes the 2*SEW‑width results to vector register vd. The destination element width is defined as twice the source SEW, and both operands are treated as signed values. If rs1 is x0, the scalar is taken as zero, causing each source element to be sign‑extended into the double‑width destination with the upper half zero and the lower half containing the original value. The instruction follows the normal vector and integer register access rules and requires no special privilege level.

INSTRUCTION: VWADDU.VV
SUMMARY: VWADDU.VV adds corresponding unsigned elements of source vector registers vs1 and vs2, producing a result for each element that is widened to twice the standard element width (2*SEW) before being written to the destination vector register vd. The instruction may include an optional mask register vm; when present, vm controls which destination elements are updated, and when omitted all elements of vd are written. It operates on the vector register file with no special privilege or access restrictions beyond those of ordinary vector instructions. No other side-effects or exceptions are defined beyond the mask-controlled element update behavior.

INSTRUCTION: VWADDU.VX
SUMMARY: The VWADDU.VX instruction reads a source vector register and a scalar integer register and writes the result to a destination vector register. If the scalar operand is the zero register (x0), each integer element of the source vector is zero‑extended to twice its original width, producing a widened result. The operation is unprivileged and may be executed in any privilege mode. No special exceptions are defined beyond the normal illegal‑instruction trap for unsupported element configurations.

INSTRUCTION: VWADDU.WX
SUMMARY: VWADDU.WX adds an unsigned scalar register (rs1) to each unsigned element of the source vector register (vs2), zero‑extending both to twice the configured element width (2 × SEW) and writing the modulo‑2^(2*SEW) result to the destination vector register (vd) for elements enabled by the mask vm. Elements whose mask bit is clear retain their previous vd values, and processing is limited to the active vector length (VL) determined by SEW and LMUL. The instruction accesses only vector registers and a scalar integer register and imposes no special privilege or access‑control restrictions. No additional exceptions beyond the mask‑controlled element handling are defined.

INSTRUCTION: VWMACC.VV
SUMMARY: VWMACC.VV treats the source registers vs1 and vs2 as vectors of signed SEW‑bit elements, computes a full‑width 2 × SEW‑bit product for each element i selected by the current VL, adds the existing 2 × SEW element in vd[i], and writes the 2 × SEW result back to vd[i]. The operation is element‑wise; when a mask register vm is present, only elements with mask bit 1 are updated, otherwise all active elements are written. No unsigned operand combinations are defined, and any attempt to use them is illegal. The instruction is available in all privilege modes with no special access restrictions.

INSTRUCTION: VWMACC.VX
SUMMARY: VWMACC.VX multiplies a signed SEW‑bit scalar from integer register rs1 with each signed SEW‑bit element of source vector vs2, producing a 2*SEW‑bit product. The product is added to the existing 2*SEW‑bit value in the corresponding destination element vd, and the sum overwrites vd[i] for elements whose mask vm bit is true, while masked‑off elements retain their prior value. The instruction uses the scalar register rs1 and the vector register groups vs2 and vd, and it is a normal vector‑extension operation that is legal in any privilege mode when the V extension is enabled. An illegal‑instruction fault occurs only if the required SEW or vector length is not supported.

INSTRUCTION: VWMACCSU.VV
SUMMARY: VWMACCSU.VV reads vector register vs1 as signed SEW‑bit elements and vs2 as unsigned SEW‑bit elements, and writes a 2*SEW‑bit result to vector register vd, which serves as a widening accumulator. For each element i it computes signed(vs1[i]) × unsigned(vs2[i]) to produce a full 2*SEW‑bit product, adds this to the existing 2*SEW‑bit value in vd[i], and stores the sum back to vd[i]. The instruction is a widening integer multiply‑add, always yielding a 2*SEW‑bit result regardless of operand signs. It is executable in any privilege mode that permits vector instructions and raises only the standard architectural exceptions (e.g., illegal‑instruction or vector‑length overflow); no additional exception behavior is defined.

INSTRUCTION: VWMACCSU.VX
SUMMARY: VWMACCSU.VX interprets the scalar source register rs1 as a signed integer and each element of the vector source register vs2 as unsigned, multiplying them as SEW‑bit × SEW‑bit operands to produce a full 2 × SEW‑bit intermediate product. The intermediate product is added to the existing 2 × SEW‑bit value held in the corresponding element of the destination vector register vd, and the resulting sum overwrites vd[i] for elements whose mask bit vm is true. Elements with a false mask bit are left unchanged. The instruction therefore performs a widening signed‑unsigned multiply‑add per element and does not generate any special exceptions beyond the normal masked‑write behavior.

INSTRUCTION: VWMACCU.VV
SUMMARY: VWMACCU.VV reads unsigned SEW‑bit elements from source vectors vs1 and vs2, forms a full‑width 2×SEW product for each active element, adds that product to the existing 2×SEW value in destination vector vd, and writes the 2×SEW sum back to vd. The optional mask vm selects which element positions are processed; masked‑off elements are left unchanged. All arithmetic is performed as unsigned multiplication and addition. The instruction follows the standard vector register access rules and imposes no special privilege requirements.

INSTRUCTION: VWMACCU.VX
SUMMARY: VWMACCU.VX multiplies the unsigned SEW‑wide scalar in x[rs1] with each unsigned SEW‑wide element of source vector vs2, adds the existing 2·SEW‑wide value in the corresponding element of destination vector vd, and writes the 2·SEW‑wide sum back to vd for all element positions where the mask vm is true; elements with a false mask are left unchanged. The destination register vd is fully overwritten with the newly computed 2·SEW results for the selected elements. The operation uses unsigned‑unsigned widening semantics and is subject only to the normal vector execution privilege, with no additional access restrictions.

INSTRUCTION: VWMACCUS.VX
SUMMARY: VWMACCUS.VX multiplies the unsigned scalar in rs1 by each signed element of source vector vs2, producing a 2×SEW‑bit product that is added to the existing 2×SEW‑bit value in the corresponding element of destination vector vd, and the sum overwrites the previous addend. The operation yields a 2×SEW result per destination element and is gated by the optional element‑wise mask vm, which prevents any modification when the mask bit is zero. The instruction accesses integer and vector registers in the normal architectural state and carries no special privilege or exception requirements beyond standard execution.

INSTRUCTION: VWMUL.VV
SUMMARY: VWMUL.VV reads a signed SEW-bit element from each of the source vector registers vs1 and vs2, multiplies them to produce a full 2*SEW-bit product, and writes that product into the corresponding element of the destination vector register group vd. The destination elements are twice the standard element width, matching the 2*SEW result size. The instruction operates on the current vector length and requires only normal vector execution privilege; no special privileged mode is required. No architectural exceptions are defined for this operation beyond the usual vector‑length checks.

INSTRUCTION: VWMUL.VX
SUMMARY: VWMUL.VX multiplies each signed SEW‑bit element of the source vector register group (vs2) by the signed SEW‑bit value in scalar register rs1, producing a signed 2×SEW‑bit product per element. The full 2×SEW‑bit results are written to the corresponding elements of the destination vector register group (vd), which is sized to hold 2×SEW‑bit elements. If a vector mask vm is present, only elements with mask bit 1 are updated; otherwise all elements are written. The instruction executes in normal privilege modes with no special access restrictions.

INSTRUCTION: VWMULSU.VV
SUMMARY: VWMULSU.VV is a vector‑integer operation that multiplies each signed SEW‑bit element from source vector register vs2 by the corresponding unsigned SEW‑bit element from source vector register vs1, producing a 2 × SEW‑bit product per element. The resulting 2 × SEW value is written to the matching element of destination vector register vd only when the corresponding mask bit in vm is set. The instruction obeys the current vector length and SEW settings and imposes no special privilege or access‑control restrictions beyond those of ordinary vector instructions. No additional architectural exceptions are defined for this operation.

INSTRUCTION: VWMULSU.VX
SUMMARY: VWMULSU.VX interprets each element of the source vector register vs2 as a signed SEW‑bit integer and the scalar register rs1 as an unsigned SEW‑bit integer, multiplies them to produce a full 2 × SEW‑bit product for each element, and writes that product into the corresponding 2 × SEW‑bit element of the destination vector register vd. Only elements whose mask bit vm is true are written; elements with a false mask bit retain their previous value. The instruction executes in the normal privilege mode with no special access controls. No exception is raised for overflow because the full‑width product is stored.

INSTRUCTION: VWMULU.VV
SUMMARY: VWMULU.VV reads unsigned SEW‑bit elements from the source vector register groups vs1 and vs2, multiplies each pair, and produces a full 2 × SEW‑bit product per element. The resulting double‑width products are written to the corresponding elements of the destination vector register group vd. An optional mask register vm can control element updates, and any element whose mask bit is clear retains its previous value in vd. The instruction is part of the vector extension and may be executed in any privilege mode that permits vector operations.

INSTRUCTION: VWMULU.VX
SUMMARY: The VWMULU.VX instruction multiplies each active element of the source vector register group vs2 by the unsigned integer in scalar register rs1, producing a full 2 × SEW‑bit product. The widened result for each element is written to the corresponding element of the destination vector register group vd. Element‑wise execution is gated by the mask register vm, with multiplication performed only where the mask bit is set. No additional privilege checks or exception conditions are specified beyond the standard vector masking behavior.

INSTRUCTION: VWREDSUMU.VS
SUMMARY: VWREDSUMU.VS reads all elements of the source vector register vs2, zero‑extends each SEW‑wide element to 2 × SEW bits, and computes their arithmetic sum. It then adds the 2 × SEW‑width scalar element from source scalar register vs1 to that sum. The resulting 2 × SEW‑bit value is written to the destination scalar register vd, with any overflow wrapping modulo 2^(2 × SEW). The instruction is unprivileged and does not raise an exception on overflow.

INSTRUCTION: VWSLL.VI
SUMMARY: VWSLL.VI widens each SEW‑sized element of the source vector register vs2 to double width by zero‑extension, left‑shifts the widened value using the low log₂(2×SEW) bits of the 5‑bit immediate, and writes the result into the corresponding element of the destination vector register vd, whose elements are 2×SEW bits wide. The operation is applied only to active elements i satisfying vstart ≤ i < vl. The instruction executes with normal retirement and does not raise any architectural exception under defined operation. It is available in all privilege modes and imposes no special access‑control restrictions.

INSTRUCTION: VWSLL.VV
SUMMARY: VWSLL.VV reads a vector register vs1 for per‑element shift amounts and a vector register vs2 whose elements are zero‑extended to twice the standard element width (2 × SEW) before shifting. For each active element i (vstart ≤ i < vl) the low log₂(2 × SEW) bits of the corresponding vs1 element are used as the shift count to left‑shift the zero‑extended vs2 element, and the result is written to destination vector register vd with element width EEW = 2 × SEW. The instruction completes normally after all active elements have been processed. VWSLL.VV is defined only when the Zvbb extension is present; otherwise the encoding is reserved and causes an illegal‑instruction exception.

INSTRUCTION: VWSLL.VX
SUMMARY: VWSLL.VX uses the integer register rs1 to supply a shift amount, taking only the low log2(2 × SEW) bits of that value. Each SEW‑wide element of source vector register vs2 is zero‑extended to 2 × SEW bits, left‑shifted by the masked amount, and the 2 × SEW result is written to the corresponding element of destination vector register vd. The operation processes only vector elements with indices from vstart through vl‑1, following the normal vector execution model and requiring no special privilege. If any active element has not been processed when retirement is attempted, the instruction does not retire and vd retains its previous contents.

INSTRUCTION: VWSUB.VX
SUMMARY: VWSUB.VX subtracts a signed SEW‑wide scalar value in integer register rs1 from each signed SEW‑wide element of source vector register vs2, widening both operands to 2 × SEW before the subtraction and producing a 2 × SEW result element. Each result element is written to the corresponding element of destination vector register vd, subject to the active mask vm so that masked‑off elements remain unchanged. The operation is performed only for the active elements defined by the current vector length VL; elements beyond VL are not written. Execution follows the normal vector‑instruction privilege model and requires no special access permissions.

INSTRUCTION: VWSUB.WX
SUMMARY: VWSUB.WX reads a SEW‑wide element from source vector register vs2 and a signed scalar from integer register rs1, subtracts the scalar from each element using signed arithmetic, and sign‑extends the SEW‑wide difference to double‑width (2 × SEW). The double‑width results are written to the corresponding elements of destination vector register vd, with the write masked by vm so that elements whose mask bit is clear retain their prior value. The instruction obeys the normal vector‑unit privilege model and can trigger only the standard illegal‑instruction or misaligned‑access exceptions applicable to vector operations.

INSTRUCTION: VWSUBU.VX
SUMMARY: VWSUBU.VX performs a widening unsigned subtraction of each element of the source vector register vs2 and the scalar value in integer register rs1, zero‑extending both operands to double width before subtracting. The double‑width result is written to the corresponding element of destination vector register vd. The vm mask field controls elementwise writes: only elements with a set mask bit are updated, while masked‑off elements retain their prior contents. No special privilege checks or defined exceptions are associated with this instruction.

INSTRUCTION: VWSUBU.WX
SUMMARY: VWSUBU.WX is a widening unsigned subtract that takes a scalar unsigned integer in rs1 and a vector of narrower unsigned integers in vs2, producing double‑width results stored in the destination vector register group vd. For each element where the mask vm is true, the vs2 element and rs1 are zero‑extended to double width, the extended rs1 is subtracted from the extended vs2 element, and the double‑width result is written to the corresponding element of vd. Elements masked off are left unchanged in vd, and the instruction imposes no special privilege or exception requirements beyond normal vector execution.

INSTRUCTION: VXOR.VI
SUMMARY: VXOR.VI reads a source vector register group (vs2) and a 5‑bit signed immediate (imm), performs an element‑wise bitwise XOR with each active element, and writes the result to the destination vector register group (vd). The operation is masked by the vector mask register (vm), leaving any element for which vm is clear unchanged in vd. When imm equals –1, the instruction writes the bitwise complement of each active vs2 element to vd instead of a XOR result. It is a normal vector‑scalar instruction with no special privilege or access‑control restrictions beyond standard vector execution.

INSTRUCTION: VXOR.VV
SUMMARY: VXOR.VV reads two source vector registers (vs1, vs2) and a mask register (vm) and writes the result to a destination vector register (vd). For each element i whose mask bit is true, it computes the bitwise exclusive‑OR of vs1[i] and vs2[i] and stores the value in vd[i]; elements with a false mask bit are left unchanged. The instruction imposes no special privilege constraints or additional exception behavior beyond the standard masking semantics.

INSTRUCTION: VZEXT.VF2
SUMMARY: VZEXT.VF2 reads a source vector register (vs2) whose element width is half the destination width (SEW/2) and writes zero‑extended elements of full SEW into a destination vector register (vd), applying an optional element‑wise mask vm. The instruction is only defined when the implementation supports the half‑width element size (EEW = SEW/2); otherwise it raises an illegal‑instruction exception. No special privilege level is required beyond normal vector‑unit access, and the mask controls which destination elements are updated.

INSTRUCTION: VZEXT.VF4
SUMMARY: VZEXT.VF4 zero‑extends each element of the source vector register group (vs2), whose element width is SEW/4, into SEW‑wide elements written to the destination vector register group (vd), optionally controlled by a mask register (vm). The instruction mandates source EEW = SEW/4, destination EEW = SEW, source EMUL = LMUL ÷ 4, destination EMUL = LMUL, and EMUL ≤ 8; a mismatch, unsupported EEW, or illegal EMUL raises an illegal‑instruction exception. Overlap between source and destination groups is allowed only if the EEW relationship and register boundaries satisfy the defined constraints (equal EEW – any overlap; smaller EEW – lowest registers must align; larger EEW – highest registers must align) and the group does not exceed eight registers, otherwise the encoding is reserved. Encodings that use a non‑lowest‑numbered register to specify the group or violate any overlap rule are reserved, and when overlapping with differing EEWs the operation is mask‑ and tail‑agnostic regardless of vtype settings.

INSTRUCTION: VZEXT.VF8
SUMMARY: VZEXT.VF8 reads a source vector whose element width equals SEW/8 and writes a destination vector of element width SEW, zero‑extending each selected element. The destination register must be configured with element width SEW and EMUL = LMUL, and the source register must have element width SEW/8 with EMUL = (EEW/SEW) × LMUL; mismatches raise an illegal‑instruction exception. Only elements whose mask bit vm is set are updated, leaving masked‑off elements unchanged. Encodings are illegal when the source element width is unsupported or its EMUL would be below the minimum legal LMUL, also causing an illegal‑instruction exception.

INSTRUCTION: WRS.NTO
SUMMARY: WRS.NTO may be executed in any privilege mode. During its execution the core watches for pending interrupts and, if one is observed, aborts the instruction and takes the interrupt, setting mcause and jumping to the trap‑handler exactly as a WFI would. The instruction is constrained by the mstatus.TW bit: when TW=1 and it is executed outside M‑mode and does not finish within the implementation‑defined bounded time, an illegal‑instruction exception (mcause = illegal‑instruction) is raised. No illegal‑instruction exception occurs when TW=0, even in U‑mode.

INSTRUCTION: WRS.STO
SUMMARY: WRS.STO triggers a stall whose termination condition is reflected in the WRS status CSR by a timeout‑indicator bit that is set to 1 if a short‑timeout ends the stall and cleared otherwise. The instruction is legal in every privilege mode, and the status CSR is readable without restriction. If an interrupt becomes pending while the stall is active, the stall is aborted and execution continues following the same semantics as the WFI instruction. No other privilege checks or exceptions are defined for this instruction.

INSTRUCTION: XNOR
SUMMARY: XNOR reads integer registers rs1 and rs2, computes the bitwise NOT of their XOR (i.e., a bitwise exclusive‑NOR where each result bit is 1 if the corresponding bits of rs1 and rs2 are equal), and writes the XLEN‑wide result to integer register rd. In RV32 the operation uses 32‑bit values and in RV64 it uses 64‑bit values. The instruction is defined by the Zbkb extension and is present on RV64 implementations that implement this extension; no special privilege or access restrictions are specified. No exceptional conditions such as traps or illegal‑instruction faults are defined for XNOR.

INSTRUCTION: XOR
SUMMARY: The XOR instruction reads the contents of the source registers rs1 and rs2, computes a bitwise exclusive‑OR of each corresponding XLEN‑wide bit, and writes the resulting value to the destination register rd. The three register fields (rs1, rs2, rd) together define the operand and result locations. Execution of XOR is allowed in all privilege modes and does not impose any special access‑control checks. No architectural exceptions are specified for this operation.

INSTRUCTION: XORI
SUMMARY: The XORI instruction takes an integer source register rs1, a 12‑bit immediate that is sign‑extended to XLEN, computes the bitwise exclusive‑OR of rs1 and the extended immediate, and writes the XLEN‑wide result into destination register rd. It performs a normal integer‑register write with no special privilege or access‑control restrictions. If the immediate is –1 and rd = rs1, the operation instead yields the bitwise NOT of the original rs1 value, functioning as the NOT (or compressed c.not) pseudoinstruction. This exception applies only for the –1 immediate with rd equal to rs1.

INSTRUCTION: XPERM4
SUMMARY: XPERM4 reads rs1 as a vector of XLEN/4 four‑bit elements and rs2 as a parallel vector of four‑bit indexes; for each nibble position it extracts the index from rs2, selects the corresponding nibble from rs1 if the index is less than XLEN/4, otherwise substitutes a zero, and places the selected (or zero) nibble into the same position of rd. The destination register is zero‑initialized before the per‑nibble processing and the fully assembled XLEN‑bit result is written to rd on retirement. The instruction executes in the normal privilege mode with no special access controls; any out‑of‑bounds index yields a zero nibble rather than causing a trap. If the sources are not interpreted as 4‑bit vectors, rd will not contain the defined permuted value, which constitutes a specification violation.

INSTRUCTION: XPERM8
SUMMARY: XPERM8 is an integer instruction that permutes the bytes of source register rs1 according to byte‑wise indices supplied by rs2, writing the selected byte to the same byte position in destination register rd; each rs2 byte is interpreted as an unsigned 8‑bit index, and if the index is ≥ XLEN/8 the corresponding rd byte is set to zero, otherwise the byte from rs1 at that index is copied. The result register is zero‑initialized before the byte‑wise selection, and the instruction completes without raising an exception for valid indices. Execution is permitted only when one of the Zbkb, Zbkc, or Zbkx extensions is implemented; otherwise an illegal‑instruction exception (mcause = 2) is raised and the instruction does not retire. No other architectural exceptions are defined for this instruction.

INSTRUCTION: ZEXT.H
SUMMARY: ZEXT.H (c.zext.h) reads a source register rs and writes the result to a destination register rd, both of which must be among x8‑x15. It extracts rs's low‑order 16 bits, zero‑extends them to the processor's XLEN (32 bits on RV32, 64 bits on RV64), and writes that XLEN‑wide value to rd, clearing all bits above bit 15. The instruction is defined only when the Zbb extension is present; otherwise it is illegal, and an illegal‑instruction exception is raised if the register range constraint is violated. No architectural state other than rd is affected and no privilege checks are performed.

INSTRUCTION: ZIP
SUMMARY: The ZIP instruction reads a 32‑bit value from source register rs1 and writes to destination register rd a word whose bits are formed by interleaving the lower half (bits 0‑15) into the even positions and the upper half (bits 16‑31) into the odd positions. It is defined only for XLEN = 32 implementations that implement the Zbk b (bit‑manipulation for cryptography) extension; otherwise the encoding is reserved and execution raises an illegal‑instruction exception. The result is the bitwise inverse of the UNZIP operation, so applying UNZIP to rd restores the original rs1 value.
